### MAIMONIDES, Moses (Moses ben Maimon) (1139–1204)

Book of the *Guide for the Perplexed* (Hebrew title) *The Teacher of the Perplexed* <note>Latin: *Doctor Perplexorum*</note> ... translated by <note>Latin: *conversus a*</note> Johannes Buxtorf. Basel, 1629. First edition of this version. Small quarto. Index. [40 introductory], 532, [52 index] pages. Slightly browned. Contemporary vellum <note>A durable binding made from treated calfskin</note>.

This greatest of all Jewish doctors lived under the gentle rule of the Muslims of Spain; and the people of all three faiths <note>Judaism, Christianity, and Islam</note> united to weep over his grave and to consecrate his memory. The *Guide for the Perplexed* <note>original Hebrew: *More Nevochim*</note> is his masterpiece, in which he replaces the diverse and often fantastic opinions prevailing in regard to the interpretation of Scripture with a <unclear>xxxxx</unclear> rational and uniform exposition. This Latin version by Buxtorf, one of the most noted Hebrew scholars of his age, follows the Hebrew translation made from the Arabic of Maimonides by Samuel ben Tibbon.

<note>This catalog entry highlights the 17th-century Christian interest in Maimonides, as Johannes Buxtorf the Younger was a famous Christian Hebraist who translated this foundational Jewish philosophical work into Latin to make it accessible to European scholars.</note>

<summary>This page describes a 1629 Latin edition of Maimonides' "Guide for the Perplexed," translated by the scholar Johannes Buxtorf. It emphasizes Maimonides' historical significance as a bridge between faiths and his rational approach to scriptural interpretation.</summary>
<keywords>Maimonides, Guide for the Perplexed, Johannes Buxtorf, Samuel ben Tibbon, Jewish philosophy, Sephardic history, Hebraism, 17th-century printing, vellum binding</keywords>

# RABBI MOSES MAIMONIDES
## The Book
### Guide for the Perplexed <note>original Hebrew: "Moreh Nevukhim"</note>
# TEACHER
## OF THE PERPLEXED:
### Containing, as it were, a KEY for the more correct understanding of the doubtful and more obscure passages of SCRIPTURE,
->As is more fully explained in the PREFACE, in which the life of the AUTHOR and the purpose of the entire WORK are discussed:<-

->FIRST WRITTEN by the AUTHOR in the ARABIC LANGUAGE approximately 450 <note>The Roman numeral CCCCL indicates 450 years before the 1629 publication date</note> years ago in EGYPT:<-

->THEN TRANSLATED into the HEBREW LANGUAGE by Rabbi SAMUEL IBN TIBBON of Spain, in a philosophical and academic <term>scholastic: referring to the rigorous, technical style used in medieval university settings</term> style, and thus a most difficult one:<-

->NOW indeed newly, for the more abundant spread of the knowledge of the Hebrew language, and to more clearly declare its use and breadth to the schools of Christians, clearly and faithfully TRANSLATED into the LATIN LANGUAGE <note>While this current translation is in English, the text here describes Johannes Buxtorf's 1629 Latin version</note> by<-

# JOHANNES BUXTORF, the Son. <note>Johann Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664), a renowned scholar of Hebrew and son of the famous Hebraist of the same name</note>
->*Indexes of Scriptural passages, subjects, and Hebrew terms have been added.*<-

<meta>This page begins the preface by Johannes Buxtorf the Younger for his translation of a work by Maimonides. It establishes the scholarly necessity of the edition and provides biographical details of the author.</meta>

<sig>* 2</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal band of floral and scrollwork ornaments at the top of the page.</image-desc>

# JOHANNES BUXTORF THE YOUNGER
## TO THE KIND READER
->Greetings.<-

There are two things that seem to be particularly required of those who rescue ancient writers from the shadows and bring them into the light. First, they must—as much as possible—clearly and briefly outline and demonstrate the history, life, status, learning, virtue, reputation, and dignity of the writer, as well as the subject matter, goal, and essence of the writing. Second, they must provide an account of their own undertaking, their labor, and whatever they have achieved in publishing that work. Consequently, it seemed worthwhile for us to provide these same things regarding this Author whom we are now presenting; and all the more so, because there are so few—indeed, as the Hebrews are accustomed to say from the Prophet Jeremiah, chapter 3, verse 14: **one from a city and two from a family** <note>original: "אחד מעיר ושנים ממשפחה" / "Unus ex urbe & duo ex familia"</note>—who either read, or are even able to read, the monuments of the ancient Hebrews.

And so, our present Author is called <term>Rabbi Moses son of Maimon</term> <note>Latin: Rabbi Moses Filius Majemon</note>, and by his <term>acronym</term> <note>original: "ראשי תיבות" (rashei tevot), literally "heads of words"</note>, that is, abbreviated as <term>Rambam</term> <note>original: "רמבם"</note>, as they commonly pronounce it. He was born, according to the common calculation of the Jews, in the year of the World 4891 (which for us is the year 1131 from the Birth of Christ) in Cordoba, a famous city in Spain, on the very eve of Passover, which had fallen on the Sabbath day, at three o'clock in the afternoon, as Rabbi Abraham Zacuto reports in the *Book of Genealogies* <note>original: "Juchasin"</note> folio 131, column 2. Others, however, such as Rabbi Isaac in the book *The Foundation of the World* <note>original: "Jesod olam"</note>, defer his birth to the year 4895. This agrees with the testimony of Rabbi David, the grandson of our Author, found in a certain Talmudic manuscript of his, concerning which see the book *The Chain of Tradition* <note>original: "Schalscheleth hakkabbalah"</note> folio 41, column 2. His ancestors also seem to have been illustrious and famous. For he himself lists them in this way at the end of his Commentary...

<summary>In this preface, Johannes Buxtorf the Younger introduces Maimonides (Rambam), providing his birth details in 12th-century Cordoba and citing various historical Hebrew sources to establish his scholarly lineage.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger, Maimonides, Rambam, Cordoba, Jewish history, 12th century, Juchasin, bibliography, biography</keywords>

# PREFACE

...on the *Mishnayot* <note>The Mishnah, the foundational written collection of Jewish oral traditions</note> (according to the citation of the Hebrews, for in our printed Hebrew copies I find nothing); "I," he says, "Moses son of Maimon the judge, son of Joseph the wise, son of Isaac the judge, son of Joseph the judge, son of Obadiah the judge, son of Solomon, son of Rabbi Obadiah the judge, etc." Regarding his father, whom Rabbi Gedaliah <note>Gedaliah ibn Yahya, a 16th-century historian</note> writes on page 44 was a distinguished sage and judge, he relates this story—or perhaps a fable—from an ancient fragment:

Namely, that when Maimon had intended to lead a life of celibacy, a Man appeared to him in a dream in the middle of his life and commanded him to take as his wife the daughter of a certain butcher living in a neighboring city. When he disregarded the dream, it was repeated so many times that he finally decided to travel to that city. While he was there, the dream returned so often that he eventually betrothed that daughter. From that marriage this Rabbi Moses was born, but with such a difficult labor that the mother redeemed the life of her son with her own death. Afterward, the father took another wife and fathered several more children.

As for his disposition, they say that in his boyhood he was of a slow intellect and had no inclination for studies, so much so that his father not only often treated him poorly with harsh words and blows—indignantly calling him **the son of a butcher** <note>original Hebrew: "בן הקצב" (*ben ha-katsav*). This was a slur referring to his mother's social class</note>—but finally, weary of his dullness, he cast him out of the house. Having been expelled by his father, he betook himself to the Synagogue, and there he fell asleep and spent the night. Upon waking, he noticed that he had become a completely different man. Fleeing the sight of his father, he set out for a certain city in which <term>Rabbi Joseph ibn Megash</term> <note>Latin: R. Joseph F. Megas; a famous Spanish halakhist</note> was teaching; and under this teacher, he laid the foundations of his studies and made miraculous progress. 

After many years, he returned to his dear homeland of <term>Cordoba</term> <note>A major center of Jewish culture in medieval Spain</note>, but he did not visit his father's house at all. Instead, on a certain Sabbath day, he preached publicly to the astonishment of all who heard him. When the sermon was finished, his father and brothers approached him, greeted him, and received him with great kindness, etc. 

Rabbi Gedaliah relates these and many other things about him. It is also written in the *Tzemach David* <note>original: *Tzemach David* (The Sprout of David), a chronicle by David Gans</note>, folio 55, column 1, that Rabbi ibn Megash was the teacher of Rabbi Moses. However, <term>Abraham Zacuto</term> <note>Latin: Abraham Zachuth; a 15th-century astronomer and historian</note> refutes this opinion, stating in his Chronicle that Rabbi Moses learned and was instruct—

<meta>This page continues the biography of Maimonides, transitioning from the legends of his early education and difficult relationship with his father into a scholarly discussion of his actual teachers and his eventual migration to Egypt.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>* 3</sig>

### ->TO THE READER<-

...writes that it was so. However, because everyone agrees by unanimous consent that Rabbi Megas <note>Rabbi Joseph ibn Migash (1077–1141), a leading Spanish Talmudist</note> was his teacher, and because he himself writes in a certain place that he learned from him; for this reason, the author thinks it likely that he only saw his books, but did not hear him speak in person. Indeed, it would be impossible for it to be otherwise given the circumstances of the time; for Rabbi Megas died in the year 901 <note>The year 4901 in the Hebrew calendar, or 1141 CE</note> and Rabbi Moses was born in the year 891 <note>4891 in the Hebrew calendar, or 1131 CE</note>, so that he would have been only ten years old at the time of the teacher's death. To this he also adds that Rabbi Moses writes somewhere in his commentary on the *Mishnayot* <note>The Mishnah, the first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions</note>: *I have collected all the books that came into my hands, from the explanations of my lord father and others, up to Rabbi Joseph Ha-Levi <note>another name for Rabbi Megas</note>, may his rest be in the Garden of Eden: for that man’s mind was admirable in the exposition of the Talmud, and none like him has ever existed.* From this it seems possible to conclude that he only made use of his books. Furthermore, Rabbi Gedaliah <note>Gedaliah ibn Yahya, a 16th-century historian</note> relates that tradition holds he hid in a certain cave with his teacher for twelve continuous years for fear of the Roman officials <note>Original Latin: "Romanorum." This likely refers to the local Almohad authorities or is a stylized way of referring to oppressive government</note>, and there he thoroughly learned the Law of Moses. Having been educated in his studies in this manner and having reached manhood, he himself was blessed by <term>GOD</term> <note>original: *DEO*</note> with children, among whom his son Rabbi Abraham was prominent, a man likewise excelling in wisdom and learning in his own time. There exists a responsive letter of his to several questions proposed to him by a certain Rabbi Joseph, son of Rabbi Gersom, which was printed in a small book of letters written to Rabbi Moses Maimonides from almost the entire world. From that book we have selected choice excerpts and recently added them to our father’s book on the writing of Hebrew letters.

Furthermore, Hebrew histories teach us that he moved from Spain to Egypt. Rabbi Gedaliah says (page 42, column 1) that the cause was certain envious people and detractors. While he stayed there, he immediately gained disciples, especially from Alexandria and Damascus; he established a school and, by teaching, spread the fame of his supreme erudition far and wide throughout the whole world. In the book *Shevet Yehudah* <note>The Scepter of Judah, a history of the Jewish people</note> it is written that around the year 902 of the minor count <note>4902 in the Hebrew calendar, or 1142 CE</note> he fled to Zoan <note>A biblical name used here to refer to Cairo/Fustat</note> in Egypt, and in the following times became the Sultan's physician. Truly, [his] erudition...

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Moses Maimonides, moving from his early education under various teachers to a description of his extraordinary linguistic abilities and his mastery of both the sciences and Jewish law.</meta>

### ->PREFACE<-

...he possessed a varied and manifold talent. For he was most skilled in various languages, as well as in many arts and disciplines. Among other languages, he was perfectly fluent in *Hebrew* and *Arabic*; but when he noticed that he could only become famous among the *Jews* through these languages, he also applied his mind to learning others, such as *Chaldean* <note>Aramaic, the language of the Talmud</note>, *Turkish*, and *Persian* <note>Original Latin: "Medicam"</note>, in which he became perfect through tireless study within the space of a few years. Besides these, he seems not to have been ignorant of *Greek*; for he frequently cites the books of *Aristotle*, *Plato*, *Galen*, *Themistius*, and others in his own works, unless perhaps he possessed them translated into the *Hebrew* or *Arabic* tongue. In the *Shalshelet Ha-Kabbalah* <note>The Chain of Tradition, a 16th-century history by Gedaliah ibn Yahya</note>, folio 42, column 2, it is asserted that he wrote many things in the Greek language.

As for the arts, he was most expert in all philosophical disciplines, and especially in Mathematics, as is proven both by his specific writings and by the learned discussions of them scattered throughout his books. He also testifies somewhere in this very book, the *Moreh* <note>The *Moreh Nevukhim* or *Guide for the Perplexed*</note>, how much benefit he received from the knowledge of mathematical disciplines. There exists a Hebrew letter of his concerning Astrology, written to the *Jews of Marseille*, which *Johannes Isaac Levita* <note>A Jewish convert to Catholicism and scholar of Hebrew (1515–1577)</note> translated into Latin and published in *Cologne* in the year of Christ 1555. This work is especially commended because it aligns elegantly with the Christian religion on that subject.

In Theology, he was a distinguished Teacher in his own religion and faith, and (a praise that must be granted to him) he was very little devoted to *Talmudic* fables and traditions. For he writes thus in a letter to *Rabbi Joseph*, his disciple: >Beware, lest you waste your time in the exposition and laborious consideration of the Gemara <note>The component of the Talmud comprising rabbinical analysis and commentary</note>; for I have lost much time in those things, and derived little benefit.

Regarding Jewish Jurisprudence, the fact that no one was more skilled than he is proven by this: he not only illustrated the entire body of Hebrew Law with commentaries, but also reduced it from a confused and most intricate order, and from an impure and varied language, into a certain order and clear Aphorisms, using pure Hebrew in an easy and elegant style <note>A reference to his legal masterpiece, the *Mishneh Torah*</note>.

We have heard that he possessed an almost incredible experience in Medicine. Indeed, it was chiefly for this reason that he was chosen by the King of *Egypt* <note>The Sultan Saladin, whom Maimonides served as court physician</note> as his Physician...

<summary>This page outlines Maimonides' immense scholarly range, including his mastery of multiple languages, his preference for rational mathematics over "Talmudic fables," and his monumental achievement in codifying Jewish law.</summary>
<keywords>Maimonides, linguistics, Arabic, Aramaic, Greek philosophy, Astrology, Gemara, Jewish Law, Mishneh Torah, Medicine, Egypt</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Maimonides, specifically moving from his linguistic and mathematical mastery to his arrival at the Egyptian royal court and the legendary medical contest between him and the jealous court physicians.</meta>

### TO THE READER.

...summoned and admitted, he was held in the highest value and greatest estimation. To this point we should refer what Rabbi Gedaliah writes in his Chronicle <note>The *Shalshelet Ha-Kabbalah* by Gedaliah ibn Yahya</note> in these words: "It was an accepted custom at that time in Egypt that on certain days the King would ascend the Royal throne, and next to him seven other thrones were placed upon steps. On these sat seven men who were the most learned in the whole Kingdom, being most practiced in the seven liberal arts: namely, Grammar, Logic <note>Original Latin: *Dialectica*</note>, Rhetoric, Arithmetic, Geometry, Astrology <note>At this time, often used interchangeably with Astronomy</note>, and Music."

When the King observed this custom one day, he was in doubt as to which step he ought to place this Rabbi Moses; for he had learned that Moses surpassed and excelled all the wise men of Egypt in all these arts. Seeing this, the other Royal Physicians began to seethe with envy against him, and they therefore denounced him before the King with false accusations. From this, the matter came to a formal debate before the King regarding medical skill. They proposed that they would drink any poison before the King that Rambam <note>An acronym for **Ra**bbi **M**oses **ba**n **M**aimon</note> might offer them, on the condition that the Jew first drain the cup that they would offer to him. 

Rambam accepted the condition, and on a clear day he explained the business to his disciples, who were greatly displeased by the matter. He, however, laughed at them and commanded that certain medicines be prepared, which they were to give to him both before and after he had taken the poison. They soon prepared everything with the greatest faith and diligence; they declared a fast and poured out burning prayers to <term>GOD</term> <note>Original: *DEUM*</note> for their teacher. 

He appeared undaunted before the King and drank the cup offered to him by the other physicians. This done, he hurried quickly to his own house and received from his disciples the medicines they had faithfully prepared, by which—through the grace of <term>GOD</term>—he was preserved in life. On the third day, to the admiration of all, he returned to the Royal palace, himself equipped with a poison to be given to the remaining Royal Physicians. Trembling, they took the poison, and as soon as it was drained, ten of them fell dead to the ground in the sight of the King. How much favor and honor this deed [brought him...]

<summary>This page recounts a legendary trial at the Egyptian court where Maimonides (Rambam) outwits jealous rival physicians by surviving their poison through medical skill and then dealing a fatal counter-poison to them.</summary>
<keywords>Maimonides, Egypt, Royal Physicians, seven liberal arts, poison contest, Gedaliah ibn Yahya, medical debate</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Maimonides, moving from the contest of medical skill to a legendary account of his survival and the historical evidence found in his correspondence with Samuel ibn Tibbon.</meta>

# PREFACE

It cannot be expressed how much prestige and authority this won for Rambam <note>An acronym for **Ra**bbi **M**oses **ba**n **M**aimon</note> with the King and all the courtly nobles. *There are some, however, who recount this history differently, namely: when the King was once sick and Rambam alone was employed to treat him, the other physicians, seizing the opportunity, injected poison into the medicine that Rambam had prepared for the King. Having done this, they approached the King and secretly warned him that they were certain poison had been mixed into the medicine that Rambam was about to present to the King. When this potion was offered by Rambam to the King, the King ordered that a little of it first be given to a dog that was present to taste. The dog took it and died suddenly. The most wise King, suspecting that the wickedness of envious men might have intervened here, did not immediately boil over with anger against Rabbi Moses.*

*Rambam, however, became like a body without a soul <note>He was paralyzed with shock or despair at the accusation</note>. The King turned to him and said: "You see that you are guilty of death; nonetheless, because of your other good services to me, I graciously grant you that you may choose the death by which you wish to be punished." Rambam asked for a space of three days to deliberate. Having meanwhile shared the matter with his disciples, he indicated to them that he would choose this: that the King's physicians should open his veins on all sides and draw out all his blood; for in this way, his soul would depart peacefully. But they <note>his disciples</note> were to be equipped with a medicine he ordered them to prepare, which they should offer to him while the blood was flowing. For he was certain that there was a specific vein in the heart that was commonly unknown to physicians, which he was sure would remain untouched. It happened thus, and by a prosperous success he was preserved in life.* 

*Rising and coming forth, Rabbi Moses hid himself in a certain cave, in which he lay hidden for twelve years, and there he composed his immense work which he called Yad <note>original: "Jad". The Hebrew word for "Hand" is Yad (יד), which has a numerical value of 14, referring to the fourteen books of his legal code, the Mishneh Torah</note>, because it was divided into fourteen books.* 

Thus says Rabbi Gedaliah <note>Gedaliah ibn Yahya, a 16th-century chronicler</note>. Regarding these matters, we leave the judgment to the reader. More certain are the things the author himself writes in a certain letter to Rabbi Samuel ibn Tibbon <note>A famous translator who turned Maimonides' works from Arabic into Hebrew</note>, namely regarding what office he held in the Egyptian royal court, and how much business and trouble he had on account of medical matters. Rabbi Samuel had written to him previously, [consulting] him about certain doubts...

<summary>This page recounts a legend from Rabbi Gedaliah about Maimonides surviving a poison plot and a death sentence, which allegedly led to his twelve-year seclusion where he wrote the "Yad". The author contrasts this folklore with Maimonides' own historical letters to Samuel ibn Tibbon.</summary>
<keywords>Maimonides, Rambam, King of Egypt, medical plot, poison, Yad, Mishneh Torah, Gedaliah ibn Yahya, Samuel ibn Tibbon, medical history</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the legendary accounts of Maimonides's medical prowess to historical evidence found in his own letters. Specifically, it quotes a famous letter from Maimonides to his translator, Samuel ibn Tibbon, describing his exhausting daily schedule in the service of the Sultan in Egypt.</meta>

<sig>**</sig>

->TO THE READER.<-

*...had consulted with certain people, and at last added that, when he should find leisure, he himself would meet with him, and discuss each matter more fully in person; R. Moses <note>Rabbi Moses Maimonides</note> responded to him among other things in this way:* Regarding what you signaled about your arrival to us; "come, blessed of the LORD, may you be blessed among all who come" <note>original: "veni benedicte DOMINI, benedictus esto inter omnes advenientes"</note>. I have indeed rejoiced greatly about it: I also desire to enjoy your most pleasant conversation and to see your face; though it seems hard to me that you must undergo the danger of the sea. But I signify and advise you candidly, do not cast yourself into danger for my sake; for you will achieve nothing else than seeing my face: do not hope to carry back any benefit in the sciences and arts, or to converse and talk with me alone, even for a single hour, whether by day or by night. For the mass of my business is immense, as you will understand in person. I live in Egypt <note>Referring to Fustat, or Old Cairo</note>, and the King lives in Cairo <note>original: "Alkaira"</note>, between which two places the distance is about two Sabbath boundaries <term>Sabbath boundary (Tehum Shabbat): a distance of approximately 2,000 cubits or 1 kilometer, the limit one is permitted to walk outside a city on the Sabbath</term>. Moreover, I have a most heavy custom or duty at the King's court: for ordinarily every day I must visit him early in the morning, but when he himself, or any of his children, or his concubines falls ill, I am not allowed to leave from there at all. Thus I spend almost the whole day in the King's Court. Furthermore, when one or another official <note>original: "praefectus"</note> is sick, their care is entrusted to me. In short; every day early in the morning I go up to Cairo, where when I find nothing adverse, I return to my own seat immediately after noon. Having returned there, and nearly dead with hunger, behold I find all the crossroads filled with Gentiles and Jews, nobles and commoners, judges and tax collectors, friends and enemies, who greedily await the time of my return. Descending from my horse, having washed my hands according to custom, I greet all the guests humbly and dutifully, and I kindly advise them to wait patiently for my finished light lunch. This happens daily. After taking food, I go out to treat them and prescribe remedies. There are those who persist until night; often even, by the faith of the law <note>original: "per fidem legis"; a form of oath</note> through many...

<meta>This page continues the translation of a letter from Maimonides to Samuel ibn Tibbon. In the preceding text, Maimonides described his grueling daily commute to the Sultan’s court and his medical duties. This page concludes that description and transitions into a discussion regarding his title "The Egyptian" and a legendary account of his travels to France to verify a Torah scroll.</meta>

### PREFACE

...hours until deep into the night I am occupied in listening, speaking, advising, and prescribing, so that sometimes I fall asleep from excessive exhaustion, and I am so worn out that I can scarcely speak, etc. From this it appears why he is commonly called <term>ha-Mizri</term> <note>original: המצרי</note> **THE EGYPTIAN**; namely, because he lived in *Egypt and spent the greatest part of his life there.* Furthermore, it seems he also reached European lands, if it is true what Rabbi Azariah <note>Azariah dei Rossi, a 16th-century Italian Jewish physician and scholar</note> writes in the book *Me'or Einayim* <note>The Light of the Eyes</note> on leaf 53, column 2, saying: 

But as for what I promised, that I would write something toward the end of this chapter concerning the book of the Law <note>The Torah scroll</note> which Ezra wrote—lest the memory of it perish—I will transcribe what I found in a certain ancient manuscript Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> among the books of Solomon Sason left here in Ferrara, in these words: 

*I, Moses son of Maimon, led by zeal for the God of Israel—when I had seen the books of the Law in Egypt, and that their <term>Parashot</term> <note>The "open" and "closed" section breaks in a Torah scroll</note>, both closed and open, and the greater divisions were not written as is fitting—I said to myself: "It is time to act for the Lord: for they have made void Your Law."* <margin>*Psalm 119:126*</margin> *I therefore set my studies toward writing a book of the Law of our God; namely, the five books of the Law together in <term>quinternions</term> <note>Gatherings of five folded sheets of parchment</note>, so that from them other books could be corrected and amended. Now that exemplar from which I transcribed contains all twenty-four sacred books, and it is well enough known that it has been in Jerusalem since the times of the <term>Tannaim</term> and <term>Amoraim</term>* <note>The generations of rabbinic sages who compiled the Mishnah and the Talmud</note>, *and we rely upon it. And when I had brought my book to a conclusion, God so disposing, my heart moved me to set out from Egypt for the Kingdom of Burgundy, which is in France, to the city of <note>Lunel</note>, which is on the river <note>Rhône</note>. For I had diligently inquired and searched out that there was to be found there a book of the Law of God, written by the holy hand of Ezra the High Priest and ready scribe.* <note>The biblical Ezra (5th c. BCE), traditionally credited with standardizing the Hebrew script</note> *I brought with me the "Correction of the Scribes," the open and closed <term>Parashot</term>, and the greater distinctions which I had transcribed from the Bible brought from Jerusalem, and I found everything there to correspond exactly with that exemplar which I had brought with me. This affected me with the greatest joy, so that I vowed to celebrate that day every year with a solemn feast. Now it was the 28th day of the month of Ziv <note>The second month of the Hebrew calendar, usually April or May</note>, that is, April. Furthermore, I labored for the sake of the glory of God and excerpted from that book all the words that were defective or redundant,* <note>Referring to the "male" and "chaser" spelling variations in the Hebrew text, where certain vowels are either represented by letters or omitted</note>

*...large, small, varying, and inverted letters, so that I might establish the book of the Law of God without excess or defect. Having returned to my people, I wrote the book of the Law, as is proper, and from it everyone now transcribes. Remember me, my God, for good.* <note>A reference to Nehemiah 13:31</note> *Thus far his words.* But it is beyond all doubt to me (adds Rabbi Azariah), that those words were copied from elsewhere and were not written by the hand of Maimonides <note>original: Rambam</note> himself. To hide nothing, they seem quite miraculous in my eyes. For if they are true, why did he make no mention of this matter in the *Laws of the Torah Scrolls* <note>original: Halachot Siphre Thorah</note> chapter 8, just as he mentions the deeds of Ben Asher? <note>Aaron ben Moses ben Asher, a 10th-century scribe who refined the Masoretic system</note> But perhaps this occurred at the end of his days, after he had published 그 book, etc. *These things are from Rabbi Azariah. Other chronicles mention nothing of him.*

*As for his scholarly works, they are very numerous; it is neither necessary nor within our power to list them all here. He wrote the greatest part in the Arabic language, with which he was quite familiar. Concerning these, Rabbi Gedaliah writes as follows:* If I wished to recount all of his distinguished works, time would fail me sooner than they would. For he composed many books: <term>Halachoth</term> <note>Jewish religious laws</note>, legal constitutions, Questions and Answers <note>Responsa</note>, and finally commentaries. Because these are well known, there is no need to list them. But many are hidden from us in theology, philosophy, logic, medicine, and in many languages, such as Arabic, Greek, and Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note>. I testify that I have seen many medical books translated into the Latin tongue bearing his name. I have also heard for certain that a great number of them still exist in the Greek and Arabic languages; likewise the responses which he made to the Sages of the city of **Lunel** <note>A center of Jewish learning in Southern France</note>, while in Egypt, and other places, which have never reached us, and which their owners have never wished to publish or circulate. *In the preface to the Mishnah he writes that he had written commentaries on three parts of the <term>Gemara</term> <note>The component of the Talmud comprising rabbinical analysis</note>, but they do not exist today. The principal works which we possess in the Hebrew language are the Book of the <term>Yad</term> <note>The "Hand," a reference to the 14 books of his legal code</note>, which is also called the <term>Mishne Torah</term> <note>The Repetition of the Law</note>, the **Guide for the Perplexed** <note>original: More Hannevochim</note>, and the <term>Perushim</term> <note>Commentaries</note>, or commentaries on the Mishnah.*

He began to write the commentaries on the Mishnah in the year of his life...

*...twenty-third year, and he finished them in Egypt when he was thirty years old.*

*He wrote the book <term>Jad</term> <note>The "Mighty Hand," also known as the Mishneh Torah, Maimonides' massive code of Jewish law</note> twelve years later, as the author of the book <term>Juchasin</term> <note>The "Book of Lineage," a historical work by Abraham Zacuto</note> relates. In the book <term>Schalscheleth</term> <note>The "Chain of Tradition" by Gedaliah ibn Yahya</note> it is read: He composed the book <term>Mischne Thorah</term> <note>The "Repetition of the Law"</note> in the year 936 of the minor era from the creation of the world, which is the year 1107 from the destruction of the Temple, etc. And soon after: In the book <term>Jesod Olam</term> <note>The "Foundation of the World" by Isaac Israeli</note> it is written that he labored on it for five continuous years. In <term>Tzemach David</term> <note>The "Branch of David" by David Gans</note>, first part, folio 55, column 2: <term>Rambam</term> <note>The common acronym for Rabbi Moses ben Maimon</note> composed the book <term>Jad</term> in the year 930. I found it thus in chapter 9 of the laws concerning the <term>Sanctification of the New Moon</term>. It is possible, however, that he did not finish it until the year 939—that is, twelve years after his commentary on the <term>Mishnah</term> <note>original: Mischuajoth</note>, according to the book *Juchasin*. He wrote this book in the purest and most elegant Hebrew language. Regarding its contents, see the Rabbinic Library of my father, <term>may the memory of the righteous be a blessing</term> <note>original: זצ"ל, a common Hebrew honorific for the deceased</note>. It was held in the highest esteem by many nations, so much so that according to it—as if according to an authentic law—they decided controversial cases, as can be seen in *Schalscheleth* folio 41, column 2, and 42, column 1. By some, however (doubtless out of envy), it was criticized, and specifically on this account: that it does away with the Talmud <note>Maimonides was sometimes accused of trying to replace the study of the Talmud with his own concise code</note>, teaches many things contradictory to it, and suppresses the names of the Sages under whose names the traditions are contained in the Talmud. Against this accusation, there exists a certain apologetic letter of his to Rabbi Pinchas, the judge of Alexandria; see this in that appendix of letters already cited by us several times. He wrote the book <term>More Nevochim</term> <note>The "Guide for the Perplexed," Maimonides' primary philosophical work</note> in his 50th year.*

*<term>Iggereth thechijat hammetim</term>, or the "Letter on the Resurrection of the Dead," he composed five years later.*

*But if he wrote much, he was of even far greater reading, as not only this present book but also his other writings testify. For he studied not only the Hebrew authors he could find, but also Arabic, Turkish, Greek, Egyptian, Talmudic, Philosophical, Pagan <note>original: Ethnicos</note>, and other authors, who are cited by him everywhere. He testifies in his "Letter on Astrology," and elsewhere in the "Guide for the Perplexed," that he had read all the ancient authors on idolatry who had come into his hands—*

*...came into their hands, to have read them diligently, and from them to have learned the reasons for the Mosaic Precepts. In his preface to the <term>Mishnah</term> <note>The first major written collection of the Jewish oral traditions</note>, he writes that he had read all the philosophical books. Regarding which authors he persuaded his disciples to read, one should consult the Letter he wrote to his son, Rabbi Abraham, and another to Rabbi Samuel ibn Tibbon. Some report that even before his death, he had learned the <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>The Jewish tradition of mystical interpretation</note>. For so writes Rabbi Elijah Hayyim in the preface of the book he calls <term>Iggeret Chamudot</term> <note>original: "Epistle of Delights"</note>: "I saw a certain Letter of Rabbi Moses, son of Maimon, which he wrote from Jerusalem to Egypt to his disciple, in which he spoke thus: 'After I came into the glorious land (Jerusalem), I found a certain old man who enlightened my face in the ways of the Kabbalah; and if I had known in former times those things which I know now, I would have written many things which I have hitherto neglected.'" Rabbi Yom Tov son of Abraham (whose name they write in abbreviated form as <term>Ritva</term>) in his Commentary on the book <term>Yad</term>, in the <term>Halachot</term> <note>or "statutes"</note> of the <term>Yesodei HaTorah</term> <note>original: "Foundations of the Law"</note> near the end of chapter 1, writes that he knows and has seen that <term>Rambam</term> understood the Kabbalah at the end of his days.*

*Regarding his death, they seem to write diverse accounts. For those whom I just cited feel that, since he had lived in Palestine for a long time and was seventy years old, he died there and was buried in upper Galilee, according to the book <term>Shalshelet</term> <note>The "Chain of Tradition," p. 43, col. 1</note>. On the contrary, others maintain that he closed his final day in Egypt. For so writes Samuel Shullam in the book <term>Yuchasin</term>: "I found in a certain gathering of pages that the funeral ark of Rabbi Moses (whose memory be a blessing) was carried out in the year 965 of the minor reckoning <note>AD 1204</note> in Egypt; and the Jews and Egyptians wept for him for three days, and they called that year <term>nehi nihyah</term> <note>original: "A lamentation of lamentations," from Micah 2:4; the Hebrew word "nehi" (lament) numerically equals 65, matching the year</note>." On the seventh day the rumor reached Alexandria, and on the eighth day, Jerusalem. In Jerusalem they made a great mourning and proclaimed a fast and assembly: the synagogue caretaker read those rebukes from Leviticus 26, "If in my statutes..." etc. The <term>Haftarah</term> <note>original: "Dismissing the assembly," the concluding reading from the Prophets</note> was read: "And the word of Samuel came to all Israel," etc. from 1 Samuel 4, and it ended with: "Because the Ark of God is taken," verse 22. After these things they carried*

<meta>This page continues the biography of Moses Maimonides, moving from the legends surrounding his death to the specific dates of his life and the controversy sparked by his philosophical works. It picks up from the account of his body being transported for burial.</meta>

-># PREFACE<-

...him into the land of Israel, and on the way they fell upon bandits. Out of fear of them, those who were carrying the coffin <note>original Latin: *arcam*</note>, being terrified, abandoned it and sought safety in flight. The bandits, seeing them flee, approached and wanted to throw the coffin into the sea, but with all their strength they were unable to move it from the spot, even though there were more than thirty of them. Therefore they said: "Without a doubt, this is a holy man," and they went to the Jews, persuading them to carry it to its place; indeed, they even accompanied the Jews themselves, and thus he was buried in Tiberias <note>A city on the Sea of Galilee, still a major site of pilgrimage to Maimonides' tomb today</note>. Some, however, say that he was committed to the earth among the Patriarchs in Hebron <note>The Cave of the Patriarchs, the traditional burial site of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note>. *These things are from the Additions of Samuel Shullam in the Juchasin <note>original: "Book of Lineage," a famous Jewish historical work</note>. Rabbi Azariah writes in the book Meor Enayim <note>original: "Light of the Eyes"</note>, page 3, chapter 25, near the end:* "Among the books of the most wise Rabbi Ezra Pippano, residing in Mantua, I saw the *Commentary of Rambam <note>An acronym for Rabbi Moses ben Maimon</note> on the Tractate Rosh Hashanah <note>The Talmudic volume regarding the Jewish New Year</note>, etc.*" at the end of which, in the hand of Rambam’s grandson, it was written thus: "*Rabbi Moses, the author of this book, was born to his father in the month of Nisan, on the 14th day, in the year 1446 according to the Era of Contracts <note>A dating system starting from 312 BCE; this corresponds to 1135 CE</note>, in the city of Córdoba: and he died in the year 1516 of the Contracts <note>Corresponding to 1204 CE</note>, on the 2nd day of the month of Tevet, that is, December. He therefore enlightened the eyes of the captivity <note>A poetic reference to the Jewish people in exile</note> for seventy years, minus 83 days, etc. See also Tzemach David <note>original: "Plant of David," a chronicle by David Gans</note> page 46, column 2.*" Rambam ascended to God in Egypt, etc. After he was buried, a magnificent monument was erected, upon which, among other things, as mentioned in the *Shalshelet* <note>The *Shalshelet ha-Kabbalah*, or "Chain of Tradition"</note>, was inscribed: *The choicest of all men* <note>original Hebrew: "Mivchar ha-Enoshi"</note>. However, certain restless men, who stirred up those troubles concerning the book *Moreh Nevuchim* <note>The "Guide for the Perplexed," Maimonides' most controversial philosophical work which attempted to harmonize Aristotelian philosophy with Torah</note> (which we shall discuss shortly), came and "corrected" those words, substituting in their place: *Excommunicated and a Heretic* <note>original Hebrew: "Muchram u-Min"</note>. Nevertheless, Rabbi Gedaliah writes that these men were later led by repentance and restored the epitaph to its original state, stating that he died in the year 965 according to the minor count <note>A common Jewish shorthand for the year 4965, or 1204 CE</note>; the memorial sign of which is *The weeping of the mourning of Moses* <note>original Hebrew: "Bechi Evel Mosheh"</note>, from Deuteronomy 34:8, where the numerical value of the first two words equals 65, the year in which he died.

*Regarding his virtues and natural gifts, he was a seeker of peace and harmony, distant from ambition, contention, and the desire for revenge, etc.*

<meta>catchword: doro. This page transitions from the biographical details of Maimonides’ life to a discussion of his enduring reputation and the immense respect he commanded among both Jewish and Christian scholars in the centuries following his death.</meta>

->TO THE READER.<-

*Whoever wishes to know more concerning these matters should read the Letter to his disciple, Rabbi Joseph, which begins:* "He has already testified," etc. <note>original Hebrew: *kevar he’id*</note>.

*One thing still remains: to show what reputation he left behind (the only thing which, born of virtue and learning, remains after death), and in what regard he has been held in all subsequent ages, both among Jews and among Christians. Various proofs of this are found here and there, from which we shall select only a few. It is a well-worn saying among the Jews, and widely known, what the Hebrews say of him:* "From Moses to Moses, there has not arisen one like Moses" <note>original Hebrew: *Mi-Moshe ad Moshe lo kam ke-Moshe*</note>, *that is, from Moses (the Prophet) to Moses (this Egyptian), there has not arisen anyone like this Moses, who in doctrine and learning could be compared to Moses the Lawgiver.* This is cited by Elijah Levita <note>A famous 15th-century Hebrew grammarian</note> in the prose preface to his book *Tishbi*. When they write his name in its abbreviated form, adding the customary blessing for the deceased, in this way: **RAMBAM**, of blessed memory <note>original: רמבם זל, an acronym for **R**abbi **M**oses **b**en **M**aimon, **z**ichrono **l**i-vrachah</note>; they are then very frequently accustomed to divide and arrange those letters thus: **RAM BaMAZAL** <note>original: רם במזל</note>, *which can be read as "Exalted in his Star," meaning he was born under a high, auspicious, and happy star—using this to denote both his name and his excellence and greatness at the same time. This occurs throughout the writings of the Hebrews wherever he is cited. At other times, they also most commonly call him the* **Teacher of Righteousness** <note>original: *Moreh Tzedek*</note> *and the* **Light of the Exile** <note>original: *Or Ha-Golah*</note>.

*Among the Christians, those who knew his learning and understood it thoroughly from his writings have celebrated it. I will bring forward only two witnesses of the highest caliber, to whom it would be a religious duty not to add others, lest their brilliance be obscured or their credibility seem diminished. That distinguished light of the whole world and peak of all learning, Joseph Scaliger* <note>One of the greatest scholars of the Renaissance</note>, *writes in a certain letter to Isaac Casaubon:* "I value not only that book (the *Guide for the Perplexed* <note>original: *More Nevochim*</note>), but even all the works of that Master so highly, that I would say he alone among the Jews has ceased to talk nonsense." *Isaac Casaubon* <note>A renowned classical scholar</note> *mentions him in his* Exercises against Baronius <note>A work of ecclesiastical history and criticism</note>, *Exercise 16, number 77, almost never without some exceptional praise (as he does often):* "Moses Maimonides (who is also called Moses the Egyptian), a man of solid and vast learning, about whom I think I can truly say what Pliny once said of Diodorus..."

<meta>Continuity: This page continues a discussion of Maimonides' reputation, concluding a reference to the Greek historian Diodorus Siculus from the previous page. It then transitions into the specific introduction of his most famous work, the *Moreh Nevukhim* (Guide for the Perplexed).</meta>

# PREFACE

...Diodorus Siculus <note>original: "doro Siculo." A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE. The author is likely quoting a scholar who compared Maimonides to Diodorus</note>, that he was the first among his people to cease talking nonsense, etc.

*Enough has been said about the Author in general—at least as much as we have been able to investigate from the Hebrew books currently in our hands. Although some things in them seem legendary or fictitious, we did not think they should be kept silent, because the Hebrew books from which they were taken are not available to everyone, and yet there are many who enjoy seeing these things and take delight in them.*

*As for the present book, it has always been held in higher esteem than his other writings, not only by the Author himself but also by other learned men. He wrote it in the fiftieth year of his life, as the Hebrew Chronicles record, in the Arabic language, for the sake of a certain disciple of his, Rabbi Joseph. This is confirmed not only by the dedicatory letter prefixed to the work but also by other letters which still exist today, which bear witness to it. The subject matter and purpose of the book is to serve as a Guide and Key to the correct understanding of the words, vocabulary, phrases, metaphors, parables, allegories, and indeed all those matters in Holy Scripture which, according to the literal sense, seem to teach something strange, heterodox, paradoxical, or absurd, or seem to have little or no use. And for this reason, the name given to the book is **Moreh Nevukhim** <note>original Hebrew: מורה נבוכים</note>, the **Teacher of the Perplexed**; it was written to assist those who become perplexed <note>original Hebrew: נבוכים, *nevukhim*</note> regarding those words, phrases, and matters, so that they hesitate whether they should take and understand them according to the literal sense or figuratively and metaphorically. He seems to have alluded with this title to what God said to Moses in Exodus 14:3—that Pharaoh, when he saw the Israelites setting out on their journey toward the Red Sea, would say: "They are bewildered <note>original Hebrew: נבוכים הם, *nevukhim hem*</note> or confused in the land." Just as Moses of old led those who (in Pharaoh's judgment) were "perplexed" and showed them a path through the Red Sea, so too he wishes to say that he, like a second Moses, will direct those into the path of truth who wander around that great sea of the Word of <term>GOD</term> <note>original Latin: *DEI*. Using the capitalised term emphasizes the divine nature of the text being navigated</note> and are perplexed. For this reason, he explains all kinds of figures of speech and figurative modes of expression <note>original Latin: *rationes loquendi impropriæ*. In this context, "improper" refers to non-literal or metaphorical language</note> concerning God, the creation of the world, and the parables, metaphors, hyperboles, and allegories of the Prophets. For this reason...*

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the introduction to Maimonides' "Guide for the Perplexed," shifting from its general purpose as a scriptural key to the specific theological topics it covers and the intense controversy its rationalist approach sparked within the Jewish communities of medieval France.</meta>

### TO THE READER.
***

*...touches upon the Vision of Ezekiel <note>A famous and mystical prophetic vision of a divine chariot (the Merkavah) described in the first chapter of the Book of Ezekiel</note>, the Story of Job, and other similar matters. Furthermore, on this occasion, not only are almost infinite and countless passages of Scripture subtly explained and illustrated by him, but also certain commonplaces <note>standard theological topics or themes</note> are more fully discussed, such as the names and attributes of God, the origin and destruction of the world, the Prophets and Prophecy, God's general Providence, and His particular Knowledge. Because it was asserted by many at that time that the Mosaic Precepts <note>the 613 laws given to Moses in the Torah</note> were given by God with no deliberate plan and for no certain reasons, but were proposed solely from His mere good pleasure and will; therefore, he disputes against these people, both in general and in specific cases, showing that each individual Precept has its own causes and reasons. Finally, toward the end of the book, he also deals with those things which, according to the literal sense, seem to have no utility in Scripture, teaching how even those were not committed to writing in vain. However, he does not deal with these in such a way as to discuss everything and every detail absolutely and fully, but brings forward and proposes only certain examples of each kind, according to which the rest can and ought to be judged. But the Author himself discusses these things in his own Preface.*

Regarding the manner in which the Author explains all these things, it should be known that he by no means proceeds in the way of the Kabbalists <note>Jewish mystics who sought hidden, esoteric meanings in the Hebrew alphabet and divine emanations</note> or the Talmudists <note>scholars focused on the legal and traditional traditions of the Talmud</note>, nor does he bring forward their interpretations, but argues primarily from Scripture itself, from Philosophy, and from sound reason. This single fact undoubtedly gave rise to the hatred of some Jews and those disturbances stirred up in France on his account. For since they were ignorant of Philosophy and too addicted to Talmudic fables, they thought he was attempting something new in this book, and thus they held him almost as a heretic. It is clear that he was already contradicted by some during his lifetime, from a certain letter of his to Rabbi Joseph, which begins with the words "It has already been testified" <note>original Hebrew: כְּבָר הֵעִיד (*kevar he'id*)</note>. But mention must especially be made here of those movements which were stirred up in France after the book was translated into the Hebrew language by Rabbi Samuel Ibn Tibbon <note>a celebrated translator (c. 1160–1230) who made Arabic Jewish philosophy accessible to Hebrew speakers in Europe</note> and carried into France and other nations and circulated. The firebrands of that sedition were a certain Rabbi Solomon, son of Rabbi Abraham of Montpellier <note>original: "Montispessulani," the Latin name for the city of Montpellier in southern France</note>, along with two...

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the historical account of the controversy surrounding Maimonides' "Guide for the Perplexed." The previous page ended while describing how the opposition was led by Rabbi Solomon of Montpellier and his followers.</meta>

### P R E F A C E
***
<meta>catchword: more</meta>

...to his disciples, **Rabbi Jonah** (who eventually fell away from the Jewish religion, as they write) and **David son of Saul**, whose name *David ben Saul* <note>Hebrew: דוד בן שאול</note> they converted through **Gematria** <note>A mystical Jewish system of numerology where the numerical value of letters in one word is equated to another</note> to his own disgrace into *Bastard son of a menstruant* <note>Hebrew: ממזר בן הנדה; the author notes that the numerical value of the letters in both phrases is equivalent</note>. These men, burning with hatred and envy, rose up in **Montpellier** <note>original: Montispessuli; a major center of Jewish learning in southern France</note>, and they incited many others and drew them to their side against **our Rabbi Moses** <note>Maimonides</note>, attacking him with various slanders, and seizing every kind of opportunity to defame this book, to reject it, and even to devote it to the flames. Moved by these injustices, the Sages of various places and Synagogues in **Narbonese Gaul** <note>The region of Provence in southern France</note> excommunicated those three seditious men. Those men, seeing that their cause was not succeeding well among their neighbors, sent a messenger with letters to the Sages in other parts of France and achieved enough that these others, in turn, sent out **Excommunications** and **Anathemas** <note>Formal religious bans and curses intended to expel a person from the community</note> to the Synagogues of Narbonese Gaul. They proclaimed as heretics all those who studied foreign arts and languages or read the books of Rabbi Moses.

The people of **Narbonne**, seeing the dangerous fire of sedition that had been lit, chose the elderly **Rabbi David Kimchi** <note>A famous medieval rabbi, biblical commentator, and grammarian, often known by the acronym Radak</note> from the entire number of their Sages so that he might travel to the individual Synagogues of the **Kingdoms of Catalonia and Aragon**. He was to urgently request that all the individual Cities, Provinces, and Synagogues assemble to deliberate and consult on settling this discord and restraining those malicious men. Kimchi began the journey but was unable to complete it; hindered by a storm and illness, he stopped in the middle of his path. However, what he could not do in person, he accomplished through his letters. For in a short time, solemn Excommunications and the thunderbolts of Anathema were sent by common consent, not only by the leading Rabbis of those Kingdoms but by all the individual Synagogues. By these, the three men mentioned above were struck down and prostrated. Nevertheless, a few men remained on the side of Rabbi Solomon and his followers, among whom **Rabbi Joseph son of Alphachar**, a Physician, held the primary place. This is not surprising, for there is an ancient proverb: *Every craftsman hates the son of his craft* <note>Aramaic: כל אומנא סני בר אומנותיה; a proverb suggesting that professional jealousy is common among peers</note>. To these matters, the Sages of France...

## TO THE READER.
->*** 2<-

...were seized by such fear and dread that they repented and were ashamed of their actions; they sought forgiveness from the people of **Provence** <note>original: Provincialibus; the Jewish communities of Southern France</note> and excused themselves most humbly before them. **Rabbi Solomon** <note>Rabbi Solomon ben Abraham of Montpellier, the leader of the anti-Maimonidean faction</note>, seeing himself abandoned by the French and realizing he could not reach his goal as he wished, turned to the **Christians**. First, he approached the lowest and most common people and persuaded them that various heretics were arising among the Jews, who were drinking in all sorts of pernicious opinions from the writings of **Rabbi Moses Maimonides** <note>The famous 12th-century philosopher also known as the Rambam</note>. He told them that since they were accustomed to burning their own heretics, he hoped they would proceed in the same way against the Jewish heretics and command that those books be burned—specifically the *Book of the Guide* <note>original: Liber More; The Guide for the Perplexed</note> and the *Book of Knowledge* <note>original: Liber Maddah; the Sefer HaMadda, the first section of Maimonides' legal code, the Mishneh Torah</note>. Nor did he rest here, but he also went to the leaders of the **Church hierarchy** and spoke with them in the same manner, so that the matter eventually reached the **Cardinal** who then presided over the churches there, plunging the Jews into the greatest danger and the deepest disgrace. 

This is a brief and summary narrative of that history, just as I was able to extract it from the authentic letters and the aforementioned **Anathemas** <note>Formal religious bans or excommunications</note>. It would be far too long and tedious to pursue every single detail here. Whoever wishes to know and read them should consult those letters which we have already cited several times. **Augustine Justinian** <note>Agostino Giustiniani (1470–1536), a Genoese Dominican and Bishop of Nebbio, who published an earlier Latin translation of the Guide in 1520</note>, Bishop of Nebbio, speaks of this matter, writing in his preface to this book as follows:

> "Furthermore, this author was honest and not at all superstitious; certainly more devoted to the truth than to the troublesome trifles of the modern Jews. Because of this, it happened that all the French Jews conspired together and condemned the book to the flames, because it seemed to favor the **heresy of the Christians** too much. But these otherwise perverse-minded men could not accomplish what they had devised. For the book was held not only in France, but was spread throughout almost the entire world in many copies."

Furthermore, as soon as this book, written in **Arabic** by the author, began to be known and gain a reputation among the Jews in the East, they desired that it be translated into the **Hebrew language** for the use of those who do not know Arabic. Hence, certain men distinguished for their learning and wisdom, especially from the **Valley of Jericho** <note>original: Vallis Jericho; a poetic name for the town of Lunel in Provence, a center of scholarship</note>, [requested] from **Rabbi Samuel, son of Rabbi Je-** <note>Samuel ibn Tibbon, who completed the most famous Hebrew translation of the Guide in 1204</note>

# PREFACE
<meta>catchword: title</meta>

...of Judah Ibn Tibbon, who was Spanish by birth but lived in Lunel <note>original: "Jerichunti"; likely a Latinization of Lunel, which scholars of the time sometimes associated with Jericho due to the shared connection to the "Moon"</note>, urgently requested that he take up this burden. They asked him because of the excellent skill he possessed in both the Arabic and Hebrew languages, and they hoped he would bind them to him through this great favor. He complied; and since the Author <note>Maimonides</note> was still living, he consulted him by letter regarding doubtful matters. He completed his translation while the Author himself was still alive, by whom it was approved as genuine, true, and sincere. This is evidenced not only by the Preface that Rabbi Samuel Ibn Tibbon wrote for the work, but also by other letters of both his and of Rabbi Moses, which still exist today and can be consulted on this matter. 

In the *Yuchasin* <note>original: "Juchatin"; referring to the Sefer Ha-Yuchasin, a historical work by Abraham Zacuto</note> it is written that they requested Rabbi Moses to translate it himself into the Holy Language <note>Hebrew</note>; but he replied that he was no longer able to do so himself, yet there was among them the wise Rabbi Ibn Tibbon, of whom he was certain that he would perform the task well. But this does not square with what the Rambam <note>An acronym for Rabbi Moses ben Maimon</note> himself writes in his letter to Rabbi Ibn Tibbon, which begins *According to his intelligence...* <note>original: "לפי שכלו וכו" (L'phi sikhlo); the opening words of Maimonides' letter</note>. For there he writes that he had not known that his [Samuel's] father had left a son until he had received his letter, and so on. 

After him, Rabbi Judah Al-Harizi also translated it, as is cited here and there in the commentaries; but his translation was not approved. For thus writes Abraham Zacuto in the book *Yuchasin*: *The translation of Al-Harizi is not worthy* <note>original: "העתקת אל חריזי אינה ראויה" (Ha-ataqat Alcharisi einah ra'uyah)</note>, that is: "The translation of Al-Harizi is not fitting."

In following times, it was also translated into the Latin language. In the *Chain of Tradition* <note>original: "Schalscheleth" [Ha-Kabbalah]; a historical work by Gedaliah ibn Yahya</note>, page 63, mention is made of a certain Rabbi Mantino the Italian <note>Jacob Mantino, a 16th-century Jewish physician and translator</note>, who translated the book *The Guide* into the Latin tongue. Whether he is the author of that version which was published by Agostino Giustiniani, Bishop of Nebbio, in the previous century, and printed in Paris by Jodocus Badius in the year 1520, I doubt. For he [Mantino] seems to have lived around the year 320 <note>This refers to the year 5320 in the Jewish calendar, which is 1560 AD</note>—that is, about seventy years ago—while the edition published by the Bishop of Nebbio is far older. 

Regarding that edition, I prefer to relay the judgment of the eminent Joseph Scaliger <note>A famous 16th-century scholar and polymath</note> rather than my own. In a certain letter to Casaubon <note>Isaac Casaubon, another leading scholar of the time</note>, he writes: "*The Guide for the Perplexed* <note>original: "More Novochim"</note> cannot be praised enough." And soon after: "I have that book in a manuscript in the Arabic language, written in Jewish characters <note>Judeo-Arabic</note>. A certain learned Jew translated..."

# TO THE READER.

...translated <note>The text begins mid-sentence, referring to Samuel Ibn Tibbon translating the work</note> it into Hebrew, which we also possess. But whoever translated it into Latin <note>This refers to the older, medieval Latin translation known as the *Dux Neutrorum*</note> translated from the Hebrew, not the Arabic, and indeed he often wanders and fails to grasp the Author’s <note>Maimonides'</note> meaning. *A little later: There is a great harvest of errors in the Latin. In addition to those faults born from the ignorance of the Translator, the ignorance of the Scribes or Printers has also been added. For almost always one reads "special" [specialem] where "spiritual" [spiritualem] should have been read. The error was born from an abbreviation <note>In medieval manuscripts, "specialem" and "spiritualem" were often abbreviated in nearly identical ways</note>. From the same source flowed "Prophecy" [Prophetia] instead of "Philosophy" [Philosophia]. In chapter 32 of book 1, "Goodness" [Bonitatem] was written where "Brevity" [Brevitatem] should have been read. In the following chapter, "natural height" [altitudo naturalis] was written instead of "natural aptitude" [aptitudo]. I could recount infinite examples of this kind if place and time demanded. These are the words of Scaliger.* <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a titan of Renaissance scholarship and chronology</note>

It is truly as that incomparable hero writes; that book teems with infinite faults and errors, most of which arose from the ignorance of the Scribes—who did not properly understand the abbreviations and characters of old handwriting—and no fewer flowed from the ignorance of the translator <note>original: "Authoris"; here referring to the creator of the Latin version</note>. I could produce hundreds of passages in which he sets down the plain opposite of what the Author intended. Just as many where words, lines, or entire sentences were omitted. Innumerable ones where, due to the corruption of punctuation, the sense is either non-existent or confused; for very often, where the sense required a break or a colon, the speech was continued; and where it should have been continued, a break was placed. I will say nothing of the distorted words. Passages of Scripture are neither expressed according to the mind of the Author, nor even identified.

Added to all this is the impurity, obscurity, and difficulty of the speech and style, according to the standard of those times. I can frankly testify to this: in doubtful passages, the Hebrew text was always clearer to me; so far was I from receiving much help from that Latin source. Furthermore, it must not be overlooked here what the illustrious Scaliger again testified regarding this book in a certain letter to Stephanus Hubertus <note>A professor of Hebrew at the Collège de France</note>; he said, *The Guide for the Perplexed <note>original: "More Nevochim"</note>, written in Arabic by Rabbi Moses ben Maimon, not yet published in Jewish letters <note>Meaning the original Arabic text written in Hebrew characters, often called Judeo-Arabic</note>, of which we also have the Hebrew translation, is a book full of good fruit, of hidden learning, and especially necessary for Christian theologians.* In another letter to Richard Thomson <note>An English scholar and book collector</note>: *With how much joy your [letters] regarding the book of Mo...*

<summary>This preface critiquing the existing Latin translation of Maimonides' Guide for the Perplexed highlights numerous errors caused by bad scribes, printers, and a lack of linguistic expertise. It cites the famous scholar Scaliger to emphasize the work's importance for Christian theologians despite these textual corruptions.</summary>

# PREFACE

<meta>catchword: new</meta>

...of the *Guide for the Perplexed* <note>original: "More hannevochim"</note>, it would have to be proven to you by some other argument than a letter. Know, therefore, that this book is worth more to me than if you had sent an equal weight of gold <note>original Greek: "ἰσόµετρον χρυσὸν"</note>. The same author says in his *Refutation of the Three Sects, chapter 20*: "This is also clearly discussed by Rambam <note>An acronym for Rabbi Moses ben Maimon, also known as Maimonides</note> in his divine Arabic work, the *Guide for the Perplexed*." *Isaac Casaubon <note>A renowned classical scholar</note> in his Exercises against Baronius, Exercise 15, no. 13:* "Moses Maimonides in the *Guide for the Perplexed*, a work of admirable learning, etc." *Agostino Giustiniani <note>Bishop of Nebbio and editor of an earlier polyglot psalter</note> in the Preface to Etienne Poncher, Archbishop of Sens:* "The *Guide for the Perplexed* is truly a work of profound and by no means trivial learning, in which many things are demonstrated by philosophical reasons, and many more are brought forward which contribute wonderfully to the understanding of the Sacred Books." *And near the end:* "You will perceive, furthermore, that he treats religious matters religiously, philosophical matters philosophically, Talmudic matters according to the Talmud <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism</note>, and finally, divine matters divinely." But I refrain from citing more testimonies. "Go out and look at what is in his jar," <note>original Aramaic: "פוק חזי ליה בקנקניה"</note> as the ancient Hebrews used to say in the Babylonian dialect, *Bava Basra, folio 22, column 1.* <note>A reference to the Talmudic tractate regarding judging a vessel by its contents rather than its appearance</note>

*As for reviewing this book anew and providing it with a genuine translation, there are various reasons that impelled and invited me to do so. For when about six years ago a copy of the Nebiensis edition <note>The 1520 Latin edition by Agostino Giustiniani</note> fell into our hands, and we hoped to gain great benefit from comparing it with the Hebrew, we immediately discovered such obscurity and corruption from the very start that we could not only barely derive any meaning from it, but could hardly even find traces of the Hebrew text. For this reason, it excited not only a disgust for that version, but also a desire and effort for a new undertaking. Because I saw it praised and celebrated everywhere by men learned in these letters above all other books, and out of the innate love I bear for these languages—for "sheep follows sheep, and the son follows the deeds of his fathers" <note>original Hebrew/Aramaic: "רחילא בתר רחילא אזלא וכרא בתר עובדיה דאבותי"</note> as the Hebrews say—I persuaded myself that I would be wasting no effort if, relying on their judgment, I were not only to study it thoroughly but also (since it could be done with almost the same effort)...*

<summary>This page presents endorsements of Maimonides from famous Christian Hebraists like Scaliger and Casaubon, followed by the author's explanation that he began this new translation because the previous Latin edition (the Nebiensis) was hopelessly corrupted and unreadable.</summary>

# TO THE READER.

*...I might illustrate it with a new translation and make it a common good. I therefore undertook the work with divine favor, but I soon found it nearly too heavy for my shoulders <note>original: "humeris meis impar," a classical idiom for being unequal to a task</note>. Obstacles repeatedly presented themselves, which deterred me and slowed the work I had begun. For besides the difficulty of the subject matter—as philosophical topics among the Hebrews are most difficult to understand—the style is also truly tangled. I believe the reason for this is that the author <note>Maimonides</note> adapted his writing to an Arabic model and to that language. As a result, he introduced new words and phrases into the Hebrew idiom. However, helped by the aids I had at hand, and confirmed in my purpose by the judgments and advice of learned men, I have at last brought the work to a conclusion with the assistance of Divine grace.*

*For I made use of both a printed edition and a manuscript on parchment. The printed edition was published in Sabbioneta <note>A famous center for Hebrew printing in Italy</note> and includes the commentaries of three Rabbis: Ephodæus <note>Profiat Duran, a 14th-century scholar</note>, Shem Tov <note>Shem-Tov ibn Shem-Tov, a 15th-century philosopher</note> (who I suspect may be the same as that Kalonymus the Hebrew who translated the Metaphysics of Averroes, as mentioned in his preface; for "Kalonymus" in Greek means the same as "Shem Tov" in Hebrew <note>Both names mean "Good Name"</note>), and Crescas <note>Hasdai Crescas, a major philosopher and critic of Aristotle</note>, who frequently brought me light and were of great assistance. And when the printed edition perhaps contained errors (of which I discovered not a few), the manuscript copy was a timely aid, from which it was easy to correct them—especially on page 146b, where several lines had been omitted, which surely no one could have guessed or conjectured without this resource.*

*I am well aware that not everyone will judge this book or my labor in the same way, especially in these times when, because of the din of arms, laws are silent <note>A reference to Cicero’s famous maxim "Silent enim leges inter arma"</note> and studies lie prostrate. This is according to the Hebrew proverb:* **If there is a book, there is no sword; if there is a sword, there is no book** <note>original Hebrew: "אם ספרא לא סייפא ואם סייפא לא ספרא"</note>, *found in the tractate Avodah Zarah <note>A section of the Talmud concerning "Foreign Worship" or idolatry</note>, page 10, column 1. Yet, by a strange fate, while these letters <note>meaning literature or scholarship</note> have reached their highest peak, they simultaneously lie at their lowest point; nevertheless, I decided that the work should not be suppressed for that reason. Just as we plant trees and build houses not for ourselves alone, but especially for posterity, so too must we consider the future, in which there will be those who will one day seek out and cherish these studies more diligently. Nor, however, do I doubt...*

<meta>catchword: doubt,</meta>

He reflects on the struggle of maintaining scholarship during a time of war, invoking the "Sword and Book" proverb to emphasize the decline of learning.</summary>

# PREFACE

[I do not] doubt that there will also be those now who will judge this work more fairly and perceive its usefulness; namely, those who have immersed themselves a little more deeply into Hebrew Philology <term>Hebrew Philology: The study of the Hebrew language and its literary history</term>; The hearts of the wise have eyes with which they see <gloss>The hearts of the wise have eyes, with which they discern those things which simpler people do not see.</gloss> What the foolish do not see <gloss>as our author says here in his Letter on Astrology. If these things are not absolutely necessary either for one's daily bread <note>original: "ad farinam," a Latin idiom meaning for one's physical livelihood</note> or for salvation, nevertheless, knowing and understanding them will do no harm. If they are not wisdom itself, let them be</gloss> delicacies for wisdom <gloss>that is, the delights, seasonings, or ornaments of wisdom and learning, as Rabbi Eliezer <note>Eliezer ben Hurcanus, a prominent 1st-century sage</note> says regarding the mathematical sciences in the *Ethics of the Fathers*, chapter 3. If they are not all good and excellent, some surely will be; and accordingly let us read them just as Rabbi Meir <note>A famous 2nd-century teacher known for learning from a controversial mentor</note> advised that the books of the Rabbis should be read:</gloss> He found a pomegranate; he ate its center and threw away its peel; <gloss>He who finds a pomegranate (he said) should eat the fruit within and throw away the peel, in the Talmudic tractate *Chagigah*, folio 15, column 2. Regarding those matters which might perhaps seem contrary to the Christian Religion in this work—not many of which we have touched with a censor’s wand <note>original: "virgula censoria," referring to small marks used to indicate points of disagreement or correction</note> in the margin—they are not numerous, nor are they bitter. It did not seem advisable to reject or cut them out. We did not wish to castrate the author <note>A common scholarly term for removing "offensive" or "heretical" passages from a text</note>. No one will interpret those parts as if they were approved by us; nor will anyone be led astray by them. Books of this kind are read only by the learned, who are endowed with a firmer judgment. Such things are also found in the books of the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum," referring to ancient Greek and Roman authors</note>, who are nonetheless not banished from our schools for that reason. Although this work contains many things which may also be found among our own authors—and perhaps more clearly and fully—it also possesses many other unique points which are most worthy of observation. There are various things which, until now, learned men have drawn from here for the illustration of fine literature. There will perhaps be others in the future who will do so, and will be able to do it better when aided by this version. Furthermore, if it offered nothing else, it still provides this not-to-be-despised utility: that whoever wishes to compare this with the original Hebrew will be able to make immense progress in that language. There are no better teachers for reading Rabbinic works than such writings which, translated into a common language, lead us as if by the hand to understand the Hebrew. For if the sense of a word and a sentence is clear to us, it will also easily be established...</gloss>

<summary>The translator justifies publishing Maimonides' work despite its non-Christian origins, arguing that scholars can "eat the fruit and discard the peel." He emphasizes the text's value as a tool for Christians to master Rabbinic Hebrew.</summary>
