<meta>This page concludes the translator's preface "To the Reader." Having previously justified the study of Jewish literature, the translator now explains his specific editorial choices, including his translation philosophy, the addition of scholarly tools like indices, and his reasons for excluding the original Hebrew text in this edition.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

[...there] will be [advice] regarding the method of reading and the adding of vowel points. In translating, I have observed this method: not to depart too far from the Hebrew phrasing <note>original: "Hebraica dictione"</note>, but also not to cling to it too tenaciously <note>original: "mordicùs," literally "with the teeth"</note>, lest the meaning become too obscure or the grace and flow of the source be lost. I was forced to temper the style to the subject matter; since it is philosophical and scholastic, it demanded that type of speech. If I have happened to err anywhere, one must consider that in such a vast work it could hardly be otherwise, and therefore I should be pardoned. It is one thing when only a sentence or a specific period is translated from the Hebrew; it is another when an entire treatise is. If the speech and style are not everywhere to everyone’s taste and smack of "Hebraism" <term>Hebraism: A translation style that mimics the structure and idioms of the original Hebrew rather than following natural English flow</term>, one should know that some indulgence was granted here for those who wish to make progress in the study of the Hebrew language, so they are not hindered when comparing the texts. 

And it is certain that things translated from another language by another person can hardly be completely pure; especially in these languages which are not in common use but are learned from books: something of the author's style and the original language always sticks. Often there are also unique emphases and energies lying beneath the surface, which we wished to retain and offer to the learned for their understanding and judgment. For the sake of greater enjoyment, I have illustrated the book not only with marginal notes but also with indices. I have retained the Index of Scripture Passages which was in the Hebrew manuscript, in the same way and order it is found there, but translated into English <note>the translator originally translated these into Latin</note>, adding the notes for books, chapters, and verses which are missing there. My intention was indeed to add the Hebrew text and notes as well, and it would have been useful, but there are certain reasons which have prevented that for now. When I see how these things are received and if those others are desired, it could still happen, if God extends his grace, strength, and health. Written at Basel, the 20th of February <note>original: "x. Kal. Mart.," or ten days before the Kalends of March</note> in the year

-> 1629. <-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut printer's ornament featuring symmetrical floral and scrollwork motifs.</image-desc>

He concludes by expressing a hope to publish a bilingual edition in the future, health and reception permitting.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded the translator's Latin preface, where he discussed the difficulty of translating Hebrew philosophical terms into Latin/English. This page transitions to the original front matter of the Hebrew source: a celebratory poem in honor of the editor, Raphael Joseph of Treves, and the commentators included in this edition of Maimonides' *Guide for the Perplexed*.</meta>

### A SONG IN PRAISE OF THE BOOK BY THE SAGE RABBI RAPHAEL JOSEPH OF TREVES:

God spoke to Moses, and His word is engraved upon the book;
Every wisdom, both new and old, is bound within the *Guide* <term>Guide: Referring to the *Moreh Nevukhim* or *Guide for the Perplexed* by Maimonides</term> and its chapters.
Formerly, every man wandered lost within it, for his understanding was laid waste and void <note>original: "בוקה ומבולקה" (*bukah u-mebulakah*). This is a biblical idiom from Nahum 2:11 signifying utter desolation or confusion; here, it describes the state of a reader without a proper commentary.</note>.
To all who looked, his words were marvels, and his mysteries like a narrow, impassable breach.
Thus his book was locked away and cast aside, to a place where no investigation could reach,
Until this very day when it was printed and made plain; now it glows and shines like a torch.
Rabbi Shem Tov, Efodi, and Crescas <note>These are the three classic medieval commentators—Shem Tov ibn Shem Tov, Profiat Duran (known as the Efodi), and Asher Crescas—whose explanations were essential for understanding Maimonides' difficult philosophy.</note>;

<column-break/>

Their explanations surrounding it are sweet.
Look, O men! Go out, O senses, and seek it! In every corner and on every distant path.
Purchase the *Guide* without laziness or hesitation; why should it remain a stumbling block to you?
On this day, you shall seize eternal joy and form, and you shall understand the hidden precept and law.
Is this not a gift from God, like the rain? It is a noble offering and a *Guide* for righteousness.

<summary>This page contains a Hebrew panegyric (a poem of praise) celebrating a new printed edition of Maimonides' Guide for the Perplexed, highlighting the clarity provided by the editor Raphael Joseph of Treves and the included commentators.</summary>
<keywords>Raphael Joseph of Treves, Maimonides, Guide for the Perplexed, Shem Tov, Efodi, Crescas, Hebrew poetry, Jewish philosophy, printing history</keywords>

# P R E F A C E
## O F  T H E  A U T H O R.

Samuel son of Rabbi Ibn Tibbon <note>original: "Samuel Filius Rabbi Aben Tybbon." Samuel ibn Tibbon (c. 1160–1230) was the famous scholar who translated Maimonides' original Arabic text into the Hebrew version used by most medieval readers.</note> says; *Thus wrote the Author to his most excellent disciple, Rabbi Joseph son of Judah—may his rest be in the Garden of Eden.*

From the first time you came to me, wishing to enroll your name in the catalog of my students, it is incredible how much I have always valued you. This was both because of your singular industry in your studies and because of your great desire for speculative matters <note>speculative matters: referring to "speculative philosophy," or the deep study of physics and metaphysics rather than just practical law.</note>, which I was able to perceive from your poems. It happened that when your most elegantly composed letters and poems were delivered to me from Alexandria—before the power and sharpness of your intellect had been fully explored by me—I thought perhaps your desire and appetite were greater than your actual grasp of things. 

But after you trained under me in the study of Astronomy and the other Mathematical disciplines (the foundations of which you had already drawn from someone else), I rejoiced in you more than can be said. This was because of the excellent goodness of character I discovered in you, and the natural inclination of your mind toward those liberal Arts. I easily allowed you to be instructed in them, for even then I recognized and foresaw what kind of man you would become. 

When you had also learned the foundations of Logic from me, you greatly increased the hope and expectation I had conceived for you, so much so that I judged you worthy for me to reveal to you the Secrets of the Prophetic books <note>Secrets of the Prophetic books: Maimonides refers to the hidden, philosophical meanings within the Bible that are often obscured by literal, physical descriptions of God.</note>, so that you might be able to mark and observe those things in them which it is fitting for the perfect <note>perfect: a term used for those who have achieved a high degree of intellectual and spiritual maturity.</note> to look upon and turn their minds toward. And so I began, as if pointing a finger, to direct you toward certain things and to propose some of them to you in summary. 

But when you further asked and entreated me to explain more accurately the matters of Theology <note>Theology: In this context, "Divine Science" or Metaphysics—the study of God and the celestial world.</note> and the mind of the **sect of the Speakers** <term>sect of the Speakers: A translation of the Latin "Secta Loquentium," referring to the *Mutakallimun* (Arabic: "The Speakers"), a school of Islamic scholastic theologians who used rational arguments to defend religious doctrines.</term>, and when you asked me to indicate what I thought of their reasonings and proofs—whether they were truly demonstrative <note>demonstrative: logically certain and proven, rather than just persuasive or traditional.</note> or not—and when I saw that you had already received a taste of their doctrine from someone else, and were thus **perplexed**; on one hand, the difficulty of the matter terrified you, and on the other, the nobility of your mind urged you toward high and arduous things. Therefore, I did not wish to [deny] your most honorable and just pe-

to fail your request in this regard; but I advised instead that you should learn each and every thing methodically and in order, and so acquire for yourself a solid, rather than superficial, knowledge of things. Whenever, throughout all that time we lived together, a doubtful passage of Scripture occurred, or a saying of the Rabbis that seemed to teach some paradox, I applied all my strength to explain its true sense to you. However, when it seemed right to **God, the Best and Greatest** <note>original: "Deo Opt. Max." (Deo Optimo Maximo), a Latin formula frequently used to translate the Hebrew "Ha-Kadosh Baruch Hu" or the Arabic "Allah."</note>, to separate us, and you departed to where your destiny drew you, that unwelcome separation and the memory of our most pleasant conversation moved me to write this present Book (which I did not think of while you were present) for you and those like you, however few they may be. This book is divided into certain chapters and treats each and every thing methodically and in elegant order. **Farewell.**

<margin>The summary and argument of the Book.</margin>
The summary and argument of this Book consists, first, in explaining certain Names <note>In this context, "Names" refers to specific nouns, terms, or attributes used in the Bible.</note> which occur in the writings of the Prophets. Some of those Names are <term>Homonyms</term> <note>Words that are written the same way but have entirely different, unrelated meanings.</note> and equivocal, which the simple and unskillful understand only according to some of the things they describe. Others are <term>Metaphorical</term>, which the common people usually take in their primary and natural meaning, from which they have been transferred. Others are <term>Analogous</term> <note>Words where the meanings are different but related by a common principle.</note>, which people sometimes believe to be Univocal <note>Having only one possible meaning.</note>, and sometimes believe to be Equivocal. 

It is not, however, our purpose to explain those terms for the common people, or for beginners in speculation, or for those who study only the <term>Wisdom of the Law</term> <note>original: "sapientia Legis." This refers to the study of the Torah and Jewish religious law (Halakha) without philosophical inquiry.</note>; (for the intention of this Book, and others of this kind, is to teach the Wisdom of the Law according to the truth and from its foundations). Rather, our purpose is to instruct a man skilled and practiced in our Law, holding its true faith, perfect and blameless in doctrine and life, and knowledgeable in <term>Philosophy</term>, being finally of sound intellect. Such a man is one to whom the literal senses of the Law sometimes cause distress, and who—whether through his own study or from another—understands the true sense of those Equivocal, Metaphorical, and Analogous terms, and is thereby rendered **perplexed** <note>original: "perplexus." This is the core theme of the work—the struggle of the intellectual believer to reconcile the literal word of God with the logic of science and philosophy.</note> as to whether he should follow his own intellect and that sense of the terms...

<summary>Maimonides explains that he wrote this book to help a specific student and other intellectuals reconcile the literal language of the Bible with philosophical truth. He identifies his primary task as clarifying "homonyms" and "metaphors" in Scripture that confuse the learned reader.</summary>

Maimonides explains that such a person feels they must either abandon their faith to follow reason or suppress their reason to keep their faith.</meta>

### OF THE AUTHOR
**** 3
<meta>catchword: ce- (likely continuing "celari" or "celata," referring to things being hidden or concealed)</meta>

which he himself has learned, he should cast away (doing which he will think he is casting away the foundations of the Law); or whether he should embrace that sense while setting aside his own intellect (doing which he will think he is bringing some depravity or harm upon the Law). And so, remaining stuck in these imaginary thoughts, anxious and troubled, he does not know how he should extricate himself.

Next, this Book is concerned with the explanation of obscure and secret *Parables* <note>original: "Parabolarum," referring to the figurative or allegorical stories found in the Bible.</note> that occur in the writings of the Prophets. These are not explicitly called parables; rather, they are presented in such a way that the unlearned and inexperienced think they should be understood according to their literal sense, and nothing else appears to lie hidden beneath that outer husk. Nevertheless, when these passages are weighed and examined by learned and intelligent men according to the literal sense, they immediately leave a doubt and a "knot" in their minds. Hence, because these doubts and knots are untied while we explain those parables in this Book, <margin>Why the Book is called *More Nevochim*, that is, the Guide of the Perplexed?</margin> we wished to call it *More Nevochim*, that is, the *Guide of the Perplexed* <note>original: "Doctorem Perplexorum." While "Doctor" means teacher, this work is most famous in English as the "Guide of the Perplexed."</note>; not indeed because it removes every doubt, but because it takes away the greatest part of those most serious difficulties.

However, no one should desire or expect from me a perfect and absolute explanation and declaration of the terms and parables that I undertake to treat. (Indeed, not even the wisest and most eloquent person could express this with his mouth and tongue, much less encompass it with pen and book). I give this warning so that this book of mine does not become a target or a mark for the foolish and unlearned—who nevertheless think themselves remarkably wise—at which they might hurl the arrows of their ignorance and lack of skill. We have treated these matters long ago in our great Talmudic work <note>A reference to the *Mishneh Torah*, Maimonides' code of Jewish law.</note>, where among other things we also advised this: the *Work of Genesis* <note>original: "Opus Bereschit." This refers to Natural Science or Physics.</note> is Natural wisdom; the *Work of the Chariot* <note>original: "Opus Mercavah." This refers to Divine Science or Metaphysics.</note> is Divine wisdom. In that same place, we also cited the saying of our Sages: *In the Work of the Chariot, one does not teach even in the presence of one person, unless he be wise and understanding, and then they deliver to him only the sum and general headings.* Therefore, you should not expect anything here other than those main and general headings. Furthermore, these are not delivered in a strict order, but are scattered here and there throughout the whole book, mixed in with other things we are explaining. For my intention is to draw out from Divine wisdom, or the *Work of the Chariot*, those things that are most real, but in such a way that they are simultaneously hidden and concealed, lest we seem to act against the Divine intention, from which we consider it the highest wickedness to depart. For we see that in those things which are singular in the apprehension of <term>GOD</term> <note>original: "DEI." The author emphasizes that certain truths about the Divine must remain veiled from those not ready to receive them.</note>, ce-

# PREFACE
<meta>catchword: perpe-</meta>

<margin>Psalm 25:14</margin>...be hidden and concealed, just as David says: *The secret of the LORD is with those who fear Him* <note>original: "Secretum DOMINI timentibus eum." This implies that deep spiritual truths are reserved for those with a proper foundation of reverence.</note>. Indeed, it is even forbidden to completely publish and reveal Natural Wisdom <term>Natural Wisdom: In the medieval context, this refers to "Natural Philosophy," the study of the physical world and the laws of nature, which was seen as a precursor to understanding the divine.</term>, and there are certain principles within it which no one is permitted to teach in clear and open words. Hence, our Sages say regarding this: *The Work of Creation is not to be explained to two* <note>original: "Et non in opere Berefchit duobus." This is a reference to the Mishnah (Hagigah 2:1), which dictates that the deepest secrets of the universe, the "Ma'aseh Bereshit," should only be taught one-on-one to a student who is already wise.</note>. But if anyone wished to describe and explain all those things in a book, he would be doing the same as if he were explaining them to a thousand or more men. And from this we see that among the Prophets, these things were proposed through Parables; and our Rabbis, following the style and custom of the Sacred Books, are accustomed to speak of them through riddles and figures. The reason is that between that (namely, Natural Wisdom or Natural Theology) and Divine Wisdom, there is a great affinity and agreement, and it constitutes no small part of the science of Divine Mysteries.

<margin>The Degrees of the Perception of God in men.</margin>As for those profound Mysteries, do not think that they are perfectly and precisely known to any human: not at all. Rather, sometimes the truth seems to shine and sparkle for us like light or day, but afterwards it is hidden again, either by Nature or by Habit, so that we almost return to our former darkness. We are like those whom lightning occasionally illuminates in the thickest gloom of night. In this way, for some, the flashes are so frequent and follow one another so closely that they can perceive almost no interval; they seem to themselves to live in continuous light, and the night is like day to them. Such is the great degree of illumination of the Prophets <margin>Deuteronomy 5:31; Exodus 34:29.</margin>, of whom it is said, *But as for you, stand here with me* <note>original: "Tu autem sta hic mecum." This refers to Moses, who achieved a unique, constant state of divine communication.</note>. Likewise, *Because the skin of his face shone* <note>original: "Quia radiavit lux faciei ejus."</note>. For others, the lightning flashes are so spaced that there is a long interval between one and the next. This is the common degree of the greater part of the Prophets. For others, only a single flash appears throughout the whole night <margin>Numbers 11:25.</margin>. In this degree are those of whom it is said, *And they prophesied, and did not add* <note>original: "Et prophetârunt, & non addiderunt." This refers to the elders of Israel who received a temporary gift of prophecy that did not continue.</note>. To others it happens that a single flash appears through various strikes separated by a large or small interval (this is the meaning of the Author’s words, according to Shem Tov <note>Shem Tov ben Joseph ibn Shem Tov was a 15th-century Spanish philosopher and commentator on Maimonides.</note>). There are also some who clearly do not reach such a degree where their darkness is illustrated by lightning, but only by some pure, bright, and shining body, like a stone, a gem, or similar things, which are accustomed to scatter light in the darkness. And this light too, though thin and small, does not appear to some always and continuously, but interruptedly—sometimes shining, sometimes not—like the flame or the edge of the turning sword <note>A reference to the "flaming sword which turned every way" guarding the way to the Tree of Life in Genesis.</note>. And these are the degrees according to which men are distinguished in their perception of God. But those who see no light at all, but...

# AUTHOR'S PREFACE

<margin>Psalm 82:5</margin>
...they constantly grope like the blind; they are those of whom it is said: *They do not know, nor do they understand; they walk on in darkness without end, and the truth is hidden from them* <note>original: "Non sciunt, neq; intelligunt, in tenebris indefinenter ambulant, & abscondita est ab illis veritas"</note>, even though it shines and glows more clearly than the midday sun, just as
<margin>Job 37:21</margin>
we read elsewhere: *They did not see the bright light that is in the heavens* <note>original: "Non viderunt lumen lucidum, quod est in Cælis"</note>. And these are the common multitude of people, whom we have not taken into account in this book.

Furthermore, you should know that even one of the "perfected" <note>The "perfected" refers to those who have achieved a high level of intellectual and spiritual maturity.</note>, even if he wished to, would be unable to explain perfectly and in proper order—whether by word or pen—even the smallest part of a Mystery according to the degree of understanding he himself possesses, as one might explain other disciplines. Truly, the same thing will happen to him when he wishes to teach others as happened to him when he himself was learning: namely, that something may now seem clear and open to him, but soon after it becomes obscure and hidden again, as if the very nature of the subject were designed that way. For this reason, when the 
<margin>Why and how do the Sages speak through Parables and riddles?</margin>
Sages wished to teach and propose anything on this subject, they did so only through Parables and riddles, and they did so in many and varied ways. In some cases, they propose the matter they wish to teach at the beginning of the Parable; in others, in the middle; and in others, at the very end—specifically when they could not find a single parable that fit the proposed matter perfectly from beginning to end. Sometimes a subject, though it be one, is found dispersed across several Parables, even those far distant from one another. Occasionally—and this is more profound—a single Parable serves as a Parable for many things, so that its beginning fits one thing while its end fits another. Sometimes an entire Parable relates and belongs to two things that are closely related. Therefore, anyone who wishes to teach without Parables and riddles will be forced to use such depths and metaphors in his words that they will become just as obscure as the Parables and riddles themselves. Thus, it seems the Sages here follow a singular Divine instinct, just as they follow their natural dispositions in natural actions. Do you not see what the Holy and Blessed God did when He wished to make us perfect, and to direct and correct our customs and actions with His practical Laws? Because this could not be well accomplished without certain preceding intellectual maxims—the beginning of which is an Apprehension of the Creator suited to our capacity—and because this could not be perceived except by the help of Divine Wisdom; and since Divine Wisdom cannot be acquired except after Natural Wisdom <note>In this context, Natural Wisdom refers to Physics and the study of the material world, while Divine Wisdom refers to Metaphysics or the study of God.</note> (for Natural Wisdom 
<margin>Why did God begin the Holy Scripture with the History of Creation?</margin>
is the neighbor of Divine Wisdom, and precedes it in the order of learning, as is known to those who exercise themselves in this kind of speculation); for this reason, He began the start of His Holy Books with the Work of Bereschith <term>The Work of Bereschith: From the Hebrew *Ma'aseh Bereshit*, this refers to the "Work of Creation" or the mysteries contained within the first chapter of Genesis.</term>.

<summary>Maimonides explains that divine mysteries are naturally elusive even for the wise, necessitating the use of parables and riddles. He argues that the Bible begins with the physical account of creation (Bereschith) because the study of nature is a necessary prerequisite to understanding the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the author's methodology. While the previous page established that truth is often hidden from the masses, this section explains that the Creator and the Sages purposefully used "homonyms" (words with multiple meanings) and parables to protect profound physical and divine truths from being misunderstood by those not yet ready for them.</meta>

# PREFACE

->P R E F A C E<-

*Bereshith* <note>The Hebrew word for "In the beginning," here referring to *Ma'aseh Bereshit*, the "Work of the Beginning" or Natural Science/Physics.</note>, that is, concerning Natural Science; and both because of its excellence and preeminence, and because of our own insufficiency and imperfection in grasping such matters, He disclosed those profound things to us—which Divine Wisdom <note>referring to Metaphysics</note> compelled Him to disclose—through parables, riddles, and very obscure words. As our Rabbis of blessed memory said: *It is not possible to disclose the Work of Creation to flesh and blood* <note>original: "Indicare opus Berefchith Carni & Sanguini non est possibile"</note>. Therefore, the Scripture speaks absolutely: *In the beginning, God created*; with these words, they wished to warn us that these matters are absolute, closed, and not openly explained, just as <margin>Ecclesiastes 7:25</margin> Solomon says: *That which has been is far off (that is, Natural Science), and deep, deep (namely, Divine Science); who can find it out?* <note>original: "Procul est, quod factum est, (h.e. sapientia Naturalis), & profundum profundum, (sapientia Divina scil.) quis inveniet illud?"</note> Furthermore, He <note>the Creator</note> everywhere made use of <term>homonymous|Terms that have the same form but multiple, distinct meanings. In this context, it refers to words in the Bible that have a literal physical meaning and a deeper spiritual or philosophical meaning.</term> terms, so that the common people might understand them in one way according to their capacity and the thinness of their wit, while the more perfected might understand them in another sense.

Regarding all these matters, we promised long ago in our Talmudic Commentaries that we would explain them in the *Book of Prophecy* and in the *Book of Reconciliation*, in which we promised an exposition of all doubtful allegories which at first glance seem remote from the truth and appear to depart from the path of reason. However, after we began those books many years ago and had already written a few things, it did not seem right to us to approach that explanation by that path. For we saw that if we persisted in hiding and further entangling those things that ought to be hidden within parables, we would not be departing from the previous way and method, but merely exchanging one individual for another of the same species <note>The author means that replacing one parable with another parable doesn't actually provide new clarity.</note>. But if we were to explain those things that require explanation, our labor would be poorly suited and improper, because we would seem to be wanting to reveal the allegories and secrets of the Prophets to the common people. 

Furthermore, we saw that if an unskilled and unlearned person from the common crowd meditates on these allegories, he will conceive no doubt at all; for to the unlearned, who is ignorant of the nature of things, it is nothing new to believe things that cannot be, but are in fact impossible. However, if a perfected and eminent man meditates on them, he will either take them according to their literal sense and thus conceive only a sinister opinion of the author and regard him as ignorant—in which case no harm is done to the foundations of faith—or he will believe that they have an internal sense, and thus navigate in safety, thinking nothing but good of their author, whether he knows and understands that internal sense or not.

Wherefore, since in this book I have proposed to myself, by one path of exposition...

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his methodology for the "Guide for the Perplexed." Having established that divine truths are often concealed, he now explains why he abandoned his earlier plans for separate books on prophecy and parables in favor of this integrated approach, specifically targeting the reader who is torn between philosophical reason and the literal words of scripture.</meta>

->AUTHOR’S PREFACE<-

<meta>catchword: *****</meta>

...way to explain the nature of Prophecy and its levels, and the explanation of the Parables of the prophetic books; for that reason, I desisted from those other two books <note>Maimonides originally planned a "Book of Prophecy" and a "Book of Reconciliation" regarding difficult biblical passages</note>, and I deemed it sufficient to deal briefly with the foundations of the Faith and the general articles of belief. I have done this in much the same manner as I observed in our great Talmudic work <note>referring to the *Mishneh Torah*, his comprehensive code of Jewish law</note>, except that in this Book I am concerned with one who is imbued with the principles of philosophy, who knows the true sciences, and who believes in the words of the Law <note>original: "verbis Legis," referring to the Torah</note>, yet is perplexed by their meaning because of the <term>equivocal|Words that have multiple meanings, often a literal physical one and a deeper metaphorical one.</term>, analogous, and metaphorical terms that occur within them.

However, we will occasionally introduce a chapter in which no equivocal term is mentioned, but which instead will either lay a foundation for another chapter concerning the meaning of a specific term (which I do not wish to touch upon in that specific chapter), or it will explain a certain parable, or provide a warning that a certain passage is indeed a parable. Finally, it may contain an explanation of certain unknown matters in which—because of the <term>homonymy|The quality of a word having multiple meanings; "homonymia" in the Latin.</term> of the voice—that which is contrary to the truth is believed. This happens when that which is brought as a parable is taken literally for the thing it signifies, or when the thing signified is mistaken for the parable itself. These things being thus established, I shall now subjoin a certain proposition, which is as follows:

<margin>*The understanding of parables is the key to all understanding.*</margin>
Know that the key to the understanding and the science of the truth of all things spoken by the Prophets is the understanding of the meaning and the words of the Parables. For you know what God says through Hosea: *By the hand of the Prophets I have used likenesses.* <note>original: "Per manus Prophetarum propono fimilitudines," Hosea 12:10</note>. Likewise, it is said: *Put forth a riddle, and speak a parable.* <note>Ezekiel 17:2</note>. You also know that because the Prophets use parables so frequently, the Prophet said: *Ah, Lord! They say of me, "Does he not speak in parables?"* <note>original: "Ah ah Domine, ipfi dicunt mihi, An'non parabolicè loquitur parabolas ifte?" Ezekiel 20:49</note>. Furthermore, you are not ignorant of what Solomon says at the beginning of his Proverbs: *To understand a proverb and an interpretation, the words of the wise and their riddles.* <note>Proverbs 1:6</note>.

<margin>Hosea 12:11.
Ezekiel 17:2.
Ezekiel 20:49.
Proverbs 1:6.</margin>

<margin>*The words of the Law are like a deep well.*</margin>
Thus we read among our Sages in the Midrash <note>A method of interpreting biblical stories and laws</note>: *To what were the words of the Law like before Solomon arose? To a well whose waters are deep and cold, so that no man was able to drink from them. What did a certain prudent and ingenious man do? He tied rope to rope and thread to thread, and so he drew and drank. Thus Solomon went from parable to parable, and from one word to another, until he penetrated into the very depth or cistern of the Law.* <note>original: "foffam vel cifternam," literally the ditch or tank</note>. Thus far the Sages.

By those "words of the Law" mentioned in the Midrash—the understanding of which we say is reached through the understanding of parables—we do not mean, as is evident to anyone of sound mind, the words of the Law concerning rites and ceremonies (such as how to construct Tabernacles, or concerning palm branches, or the four categories of guardians, and similar things). Rather, we mean the understanding of profound and hidden things. As they say in that same place...

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of the purpose of his book, shifting from the intended audience (the "perplexed" philosopher) to the specific method of the biblical prophets: the use of parables to shroud deep truths.</meta>

# PREFACE
<meta>catchword: we [original: nos]</meta>

<margin>*A parable is like a cheap candle by which a precious pearl is found.*</margin>
...indeed; *Just as if someone lost a shekel or a precious pearl in the innermost part of his house, he lights a candle worth only a penny and finds it again: so too, Parables are in themselves nothing and of no value, and yet through them we see and know the words of the Law.* <note>The "Law" (Latin: *Lex*) here refers to the Torah.</note> These are again their words <note>referring to the Sages of the Midrash</note>, in which you should note how they show the inner or innermost part of the words of the Law to be like a pearl, but the literal sense of the Parable to be of no value at all. They show how the inner meaning of the Parable, hiding beneath the shell of the external letter, is likened to a precious pearl lost in a dark, closed house, crowded with much furniture and many tools. For just as a man knows the lost pearl is indeed in the house, but he does not see it, nor does he know where it is, and it has almost entirely slipped from his power because he is kept from its use until he lights a candle; so too is the nature of the understanding that Parables provide to us.

<margin>Proverbs 25:11</margin>
The Wise Man says: *Like golden apples with maskijoth of silver figures, is a word spoken in its proper ways* <note>original: "Mala aurea cum maskijoth figuris argenteis..." This refers to Solomon's proverb about "apples of gold in settings of silver."</note>, to the explanation of which saying you should pay close attention.

*Maskijoth* <note>the Hebrew term *maskit*</note>, or those "figures," are engraved bodies with intricate or latticed carvings—fine and perforated like the work of goldsmiths. They are called so because sight passes through and penetrates them. For the Hebrew word *Nischkaph* or *Hischkiph*—meaning "to look forward" or "to look back"—is translated in the Targum <note>an ancient Aramaic translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> by *Isteche* <note>original: אסתכי, meaning "to look" or "to gaze"</note>, from which that term *Maskijoth* is derived. He says, therefore, that golden apples enclosed in such silverwork—latticed and perforated—represent a Parable of a "word spoken in its proper ways." Please note how admirable and excellently stated this is. He says: In a Word that has a double face—that is, in which there is something apparent that is seen and something hidden—it is required that the external and apparent part be like silver, but the internal part be better than the external, so that between the internal and external there is the same proportion as between gold and silver. Furthermore, the external must be so designed that it can teach the attentive reader about what is inside. For just as golden apples of this kind covered with perforated silver lattices appear to be merely silver when viewed from afar and without attention, but when inspected more closely by a man endowed with sharp sight, they reveal what they have enclosed within—namely, gold—so too in the Parables of the Prophets of blessed memory: the external and apparent part contains wisdom that is manifoldly useful in preserving and directing human society, but the interior contains wisdom that...

<summary>Maimonides explains that biblical parables function like silver latticework over golden apples; the outer "silver" literal meaning is useful for society, while the inner "golden" meaning contains profound metaphysical truths for the enlightened seeker.</summary>
<keywords>parables, metaphor, Solomon, Proverbs, maskit, golden apples, silver settings, Targum, prophecy, inner meaning, literal sense</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion on the nature of prophetic language, specifically focusing on how parables function as tools for instruction in religious matters.</meta>

### AUTHOR'S PREFACE

...it instructs us regarding those things in Religion that are to be believed and embraced.

<margin>There are two kinds of Parables.</margin>
Furthermore, Prophetic Parables are of two kinds. In some, individual words denote a specific and particular thing; in others, however, the parable as a whole represents the entire subject being signified. In the latter type, many words are used, but they do not each carry weight or add something new to the subject; rather, they serve only to provide elegance, or to better hide and involve the matter, following the logic of the imagery from which the parable was drawn.

<margin>Genesis 28:12</margin>
An example of the first kind of Parable is: *And behold, a ladder was set up on the Earth, and its top touched Heaven.* <note>original: "Et ecce scala erecta erat super Terram, & caput ejus tangebat Cælum." This refers to Jacob’s dream.</note> For here, the word *Ladder* teaches one thing; *was set up*, another; *its top touched Heaven*, a third; *And the Angels of God*, a fourth; *Ascending*, a fifth; *And descending by it*, a sixth; *And behold, the Lord stood above it*, a seventh. From this you can see that every single word of this Parable adds something to the reality signified through it.

<margin>Proverbs 7:6</margin>
An example of the second kind is: *Through the window of my house, through my lattice I looked out, and I saw among the simple, I perceived among the youths a young man lacking heart <note>meaning "lacking sense" or "judgment"</note>, passing through the street, near her corner, and he went the way to her house, in the twilight, in the evening of the day, in the darkness of the night and the gloom; and behold, a woman met him, with the attire of a harlot and a subtle heart, who was loud and defiant, etc. now outside, now in the streets, etc. and she caught him, etc. I have peace offerings with me, etc. therefore I came out to meet you, etc. I have decked my bed with ornaments, etc. Come, let us take our fill of love, etc. for the man is not in his house, etc. He took a bag of money in his hand, etc. With the multitude of her smooth speech, she caused him to yield.* From all of these details we learn nothing other than that the allurements of bodily pleasures are not to be followed. The author compares the <term>material cause</term> <note>Matter, in the philosophical sense, is often associated with the physical body and its desires, which "adulterate" the pure form of the soul.</note> of all these pleasures to a harlot and an adulteress who has a husband; he built his entire Book upon this Parable. I will explain in certain chapters of this Book the wisdom by which Ma-

<margin>Matter is like an adulteress.</margin>
tter is compared to an adulterous Wife; and why he seals and closes his Book with the praises of the Virtuous Woman <note>original: "Mulieris strenuæ," referring to the 'Woman of Valor' in Proverbs 31</note>, who does not play the harlot but is content with the adornment of her house and her husband. For all impediments and obstacles that hinder a Human in his final perfection, and all imperfections and all sins, come from no other source than from the side of <term>matter</term>, as I shall explain elsewhere in this Book. And this is the sum of the entire Parable: that a Human should not follow his sensuality—that is, his material nature alone. For the immediate matter of a Human is the same as the matter of all other living creatures. Therefore, now that I have explained this to you and revealed the secret of this Parable, it is not

<summary>The author distinguishes between two types of prophetic parables: those where every word has a specific symbolic meaning (like Jacob's Ladder) and those where the narrative as a whole conveys a single moral lesson (like the warning against the adulteress in Proverbs).</summary>
<keywords>parables, prophecy, Jacob's Ladder, Proverbs, matter, sensuality, moral perfection, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface to the Guide for the Perplexed, following the discussion of the two types of parables—those where every detail matters and those where only the overall message is significant.</meta>

### PREFACE

...that you might wish to find everything contained in the Parable also in the reality it signifies: for example, what is meant when it says, *I have peace offerings with me, etc.* <note>Latin: *Sacrificia Eucharistica sunt mihi*. This refers to Proverbs 7:14, where the seductive woman lures the youth.</note>; likewise what is signified when it says, *I have decked my bed with ornaments* <note>Proverbs 7:16</note>; or what further meaning is added to the subject when it says, *For the man is not in his house, etc.* <note>Proverbs 7:19</note>. For all these words simply follow the literal sense of the Parable and pertain only to it. These are all things that adulterous and shameless people are accustomed to saying among themselves. Observe this, therefore, with diligence; for it is a great foundation for the things I wish to explain to you.

Accordingly, when you see me explain some Parable in any chapter, and indicate to you its general theme and <term>scopus</term> <note>The "scope" or the central aim/target of a text.</note>, do not demand a reasoning or application for every word and detail contained in the Parable to the thing it signifies. For if you do so, you will fall into one of two traps: either you will stray from the purpose and intention of the Parable, or you will exhaust yourself seeking an explanation for things that cannot be explained. From this obsession, you would fall into the madness and vanity so common among many in our own time, who strive to find and dig out meanings from the words of authors that the authors themselves never even dreamed of.

Your goal in the greater part of the Parables ought to be to understand the gist of the matter the author of the Parable wishes to teach you. In other cases, let it be sufficient for you to hear from me simply that this or that *is* a Parable, even if I explain nothing further to you. For if you know it is a Parable, you will easily be able to guess what it signifies; therefore, my word stating that it is a Parable will have the same effect as someone removing an obstacle that stands between the eye and the object being seen.

->RULE OF THIS BOOK.<-

<margin>By what method this Book ought to be read.</margin>
If you wish to follow and learn precisely and perfectly <note>Latin: *ad unguem vel ad amussim* — literally "to the fingernail or the ruler," meaning with extreme accuracy.</note> everything contained in this Book, you must read each of its chapters in order. It is not enough to think it sufficient to understand the general summary of a chapter; you must also observe and consider every single word, even if it does not seem to belong to the primary intention of that chapter. (For the words have not been used or applied by chance, but with the highest accuracy and great diligence; care has also been taken so that the explanation of some doubt is not omitted, and no word is spoken in the wrong place). Furthermore, see that you do not "pursue it only with your thoughts" (that is, *do not run through it and read it cursorily and superficially*), for if you do so, you will harm me and gain nothing for yourself. Instead, it is necessary that you learn what is necessary and useful from it, and unroll it from start to finish <note>Latin: *à capite ad calcem* — literally "from head to heel."</note>.

<vocab>Parabola (Parable), sensus litteralis (literal sense), scopus (aim/scope), intentio (intention), explicatio (explanation), præceptum (rule/precept), ratio legendi (method of reading)</vocab>

<summary>The author warns against over-interpreting every minor detail in certain parables, which leads to confusion and "madness," and provides a strict rule for reading this specific book: every word is chosen with precision and must be read in order.</summary>
<keywords>hermeneutics, parables, interpretation, reading method, Proverbs, allegory, precision of language</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's preface. Maimonides provides a stern warning to his readers regarding the sensitive nature of the secrets contained within the "Guide," urging discretion and charitable interpretation while identifying the three types of readers who will encounter his work.</meta>

# OF THE AUTHOR.

...for in this way it will release you from many and great doubts concerning the Law <note>The Latin *Lex* here refers to the Torah.</note>, which all intelligent people are accustomed to struggle with.

<margin>An appeal to the reader.</margin>
Furthermore, I adjure by the Blessed God all who are about to read this book of mine, that they do not explain or set forth even a single word of it to another, except for those things which have already been explained by other wise men and well-known Doctors of our Law who preceded me. But as for those things which are learned from here that have been spoken by no one before me, let no one reveal them to another. Nor should anyone presume to answer or contradict my words, for it is possible that someone might not understand or grasp my true meaning, but rather the opposite; and thus he would affect me with loss and injury in exchange for the benefit I desired to grant him, returning to me evil for good. 

Let whoever comes into possession of this book read it and judge it; and if it should cure anyone of a sickness of the heart even in the smallest degree, let him give thanks to God, and let him keep what he has drawn from it for his own use. But if someone should find no benefit at all in it, let it be to him as if it had never been composed or sent out into the light. If, however, anything should seem to someone to be ruinous or less than right, let him interpret everything in the best possible light. For if we are bound to do and observe this toward any of the common people and the plebeians, how much more equitable is it that we do the same toward our Doctors and Sages, who strive to the best of their ability to promote our benefit in the knowledge of the truth. 

I know for certain that no beginners <note>Latin: *tyrones*, referring to students just starting their education.</note>, who as yet understand nothing in the way of speculation, will fail to perceive some advantage from certain chapters of this book. Those who are mature <note>Latin: *perfectos*, often referring to those who have reached a high level of intellectual and religious development.</note>, however, who harbor certain doubts, will all report a harvest of fruit from every one of its chapters—fruit not to be regretted, but such as will cheer them and be smooth and sweet to their ears. 

But as for the "dizzy" ones <note>Latin: *Vertiginosos*. Maimonides describes those whose minds are cluttered with confused or superficial ideas.</note>, whose brains are polluted and filled with vain, futile, and false opinions (which they nevertheless believe to be true), and who imagine themselves to be great Philosophers and Theologians when they do not possess even a drop of knowledge that could truly merit the title of Wisdom—I know that they will flee from many things and will raise many objections against them. This is because they understand nothing at all, and because through these pages the dross of their counterfeit coins is exposed—the very things that are their treasure and private wealth, kept ready for their own times of distress.

But the Blessed God knows how greatly I feared to write down those things which I wished to explain and record in this book. For because these are matters about which no one from our nation has written anything at all in this captivity <note>Latin: *captivitate*, referring to the Jewish Diaspora or Exile (*Galut*).</note> until now, by what reasoning should I be the first to step forward into this arena?

<vocab>Appeal, Law, Doctors of the Law, Wise Men, Philosophers, Theologians, captivity</vocab>

<summary>Maimonides concludes his preface by adjuring the reader to keep his original insights private and to interpret his words charitably. He distinguishes between beginners who will find some help, the "perfected" who will find great joy, and the falsely learned who will reject the work because it exposes their ignorance.</summary>

# PREFACE

...do I dare? But I am supported by two principles. First, that concerning this kind of matter it is said, <margin>Psalm 119:126</margin> *It is time for the Lord to act: they have made void your Law, etc.* <note>original: "Tempus est faciendi Domino: irritam fecerunt Legem tuam." This verse was traditionally used by Jewish scholars to justify writing down oral or secret traditions when they feared the knowledge might otherwise be lost.</note> Second, because our Sages <note>A common term for the Rabbis of the Talmudic era.</note> say, *Let all your deeds be done for the glory of God.* Relying, I say, on these two principles, I undertook the composition of this Book which I now present.

In summary: I am so disposed that, although I see myself pressed and constrained, and unable to teach the truth—which can be demonstrated—in any other way than by being forced to please scarcely one good and praised man while, on the contrary, displeasing a thousand ignorant and foolish people, I would still prefer to please that one man, disregarding the mockery of the rest, and to rescue him from his perplexities and doubts until he emerges perfected.

->ANOTHER PREFACE.<-

<margin>*What are the causes of the contradictions that are found here and there among writers?*</margin> The causes of inconsistencies and contradictions found in any Book or Treatise are seven.

The first cause is when an Author brings forward the opinions of various authors who defend different views, but keeps their names hidden and does not attribute each opinion to its specific author. In such a case, there appears to be an inconsistency or contradiction in his book, because of those two views or propositions, one belongs to this person and the other to another.

The second cause is when the Author of a book defends a certain opinion and later changes it, and both versions remain written in his Book.

The third is when everything is not understood according to the literal sense, but some things literally and some parabolically <note>Refers to the use of allegories or metaphors to hide deeper philosophical truths.</note>, having an internal and secret sense; or when both propositions, which seem to be clashing and contradictory, are Parables; for when they are understood literally, then one clashes with or contradicts the other.

The fourth is when some condition underlies a matter which is not expressed in its place out of necessity; or when there are two different subjects that are not explained, then there also appears to be a clash in words, though there is none in reality.

The fifth is the order of teaching and instruction. If there should be something abstruse and very difficult to understand, and it must necessarily be discussed, and it must be taken as a starting point in the explanation of another easier matter, then it is necessary that this [easier one] precedes the former (because one must always begin with easier things); and yet it will also be necessary for the Teacher to explain the former thing lightly and with a "cruder Minerva" <note>A Latin idiom meaning a plain, unrefined, or common-sense approach rather than a strictly technical one.</note>. Indeed, he will by no means do this accurately and <term>precisely <note>original: ἀκριβῶς (*akribōs*), a Greek term for exactness or rigor.</note></term>, but will leave it just as the student or listener imagines it for themselves, until they have understood that which he wished to instill in them now; later, however, he will treat its nature and truth properly in a suitable place.

<meta>catchword: Sixth,</meta>

<summary>Maimonides justifies his decision to write the book for the benefit of the few despite the potential for mass misunderstanding, and begins an analysis of seven reasons why contradictions appear in literature.</summary>
<keywords>justification, truth vs. popularity, contradictions in writing, pedagogy, parables, precision</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s list of seven causes for contradictions in books. The previous page discussed the first five causes, focusing on how different sources, varying pedagogical needs, and metaphors can lead to apparent inconsistencies.</meta>

<header>AUTHOR’S PREFACE.</header>
<meta>catchword: Repugnancies</meta>

The **sixth** cause is a hidden contradiction, such that it does not appear or get noticed until after many premises have been laid out. The more principles required to demonstrate it, the more hidden and concealed it remains. In this way, the Author is unaware of it and believes that his first two propositions do not oppose each other. However, if each is taken separately, and a corresponding proposition is added to each, and a legitimate conclusion is drawn from them; and if this conclusion is again used as a new proposition, and another fitting one is joined to it, and a further conclusion is drawn—and so on—then after many <term>syllogisms</term> <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed propositions.</note> it will finally result in a conflict or contradiction between the last two conclusions. Such things often escape even the wisest and most learned Writers. But if two propositions clearly contradict each other, suggesting that the Writer or Author forgot the first when writing the second, this is a very great error and oversight, and I do not consider such an Author to be numbered among those whose Books deserve to be read.

The **seventh** cause is the necessity of speaking about very profound matters, in which it is necessary to hide some things and reveal others. For necessity sometimes demands that a matter be spoken of here according to one proposition, and soon elsewhere according to another that is contrary and opposing to the first. In this case, care must be taken lest the common people notice the point of contradiction, and the Author must strive by all means to hide it.

<margin>*The nature of contradictions in the Mishnah.*</margin>
The inconsistencies and contradictions found in the *Mishnah* <note>The primary collection of Jewish oral traditions, compiled around 200 CE.</note> and the *Baraitot* <note>Jewish oral traditions from the era of the Mishnah that were not included in the final compilation.</note> are due to the first cause. Thus, you will very frequently find among them the phrase: *The beginning contradicts the end* <note>original: "Repugnat principium fini"</note> or *the first part contradicts the last*. In place of a solution, they say: *The beginning is by Rabbi N., the end is by Rabbi N.* <note>The "N." here functions like "X" or "So-and-so" to denote different scholars.</note> They often say: *Rabbi saw the words of Rabbi N. and judged definitively according to him; and, He saw the words of Rabbi N. and pronounced definitively according to him.* Elsewhere you will also find: *The general opinion is according to Rabbi N.; the Mishnah follows the view of Rabbi N.* and this occurs more often than can be fully listed.

<margin>*The nature of contradictions in the Talmud.*</margin>
The inconsistencies or contradictions found in the *Talmud* <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, consisting of the Mishnah and the Gemara (commentary).</note> are due to the first and second causes. Thus, you will often find them saying: *This and that is the opinion according to Rabbi N., and this and that is the opinion according to Rabbi N.* Likewise: *He ruled according to him in one matter, but disagreed with him in another.* Likewise: *There are two Amoraim <note>Jewish scholars of the 3rd through 5th centuries who commented on the Mishnah.</note>, but the decision follows Rabbi N.* All these examples follow the first cause. Examples of the second cause include: *Rav withdrew from that opinion;* or: *And he examined which of the two opinions was the final one.* So they say: *The first response of Rav Ashi tells us such and such; the later response tells us such and such.*

<summary>This page concludes Maimonides' list of seven reasons why authors might contradict themselves, highlighting that deep subjects often require deliberate, hidden inconsistencies for educational purposes. He then applies these logical categories to explain the structure of contradictions found in the Mishnah and Talmud.</summary>

<keywords>logic, syllogism, contradictions, Mishnah, Talmud, Baraitot, Amoraim, Rav Ashi, pedagogy, esoteric writing</keywords>

# PREFACE

...dare I? But I am supported by two principles: first, that regarding such business it has been said, <margin>Psalm 119:126</margin> *It is time for the Lord to act: they have made void Your Law, etc.* <note>The author uses this biblical verse to justify his intervention in difficult theological or philosophical matters, suggesting that when the Law is misunderstood or ignored, action is required.</note> Second, that which our Sages say: *Let all your works be done for the glory of God.* Relying on these two principles, I say, I have undertaken the composition of this book which I present.

In summary: I am so inclined that, although I see myself pressed and restricted, and unable to teach the truth that can be demonstrated except in a way that I can scarcely please one good and praised man, while being forced to displease a thousand ignorant and foolish ones, I would still rather please that one man—despising the mockery of the rest—and rescue him from his perplexities and doubts until he emerges perfected.

->ANOTHER PREFACE<-

<margin>How many are the causes of the contradictions that are found here and there among writers?</margin> The causes of inconsistencies and contradictions found in any book or treatise are seven.

The **first** cause is when an author brings forward the opinions of various authors who hold different views, but keeps their names secret and does not attribute each opinion to its specific author. Then, a conflict or contradiction appears to exist in that book, because of those two views or propositions, one belongs to one person and the other to someone else.

The **second** cause is when the author of a book defends a certain opinion and later changes it, and both remain written in his book.

The **third** is when not everything is understood according to the literal sense, but some things literally and others parabolically <note>Meaning allegorically or through a metaphor that contains a deeper truth.</note>, having an internal and hidden meaning; or when both propositions, which seem to be repugnant and contradictory, are parables—for when they are understood literally, then one opposes or contradicts the other.

The **fourth** is when some condition underlies a matter which is not expressed in its place out of necessity; or when there are two different subjects which are not explained, then also there appears to be a conflict in the words, even though there is none.

The **fifth** is the order of teaching and instruction. If there is some matter that is abstruse and very difficult to understand, and it must necessarily be discussed and taken as a starting point in the explanation of another easier matter, then it is necessary that this [easier] one precedes the former (because one must always begin from easier things). And yet, it will also be necessary for the teacher to explain the former matter lightly and plainly <note>original: "crassiore Minerva," a Latin idiom referring to a "rougher" or common-sense style of wisdom, rather than a refined or technical one.</note>. Indeed, he will by no means do this accurately and <term>precisely</term> <note>original Greek: ἀκριβῶς (akribōs), meaning with scientific or rigorous exactness.</note>, but will leave it as the student or listener imagines it for himself, until he has understood that which the teacher now wished to instill in him; afterward, however, he will treat its nature and truth according to its dignity in a suitable place.

<summary>The author justifies his work as an effort to guide the wise despite the potential scorn of the masses and begins an analysis of seven reasons why books contain apparent contradictions, covering the first five causes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of seven causes of textual contradictions from the previous page, specifically detailing the sixth and seventh causes. It then transitions into how these logical principles apply to the contradictions found within the Mishnah and the Talmud.</meta>

# OF THE AUTHOR.

**Sixth**, is the concealment of a contradiction, so that it does not appear or become noticed except after many principles have been established; and the more principles needed to demonstrate it, the more hidden and concealed it is. In this way, the Author is ignorant of it and believes that his first two propositions do not conflict with one another. However, if each is taken separately, and another fitting proposition is added to it, and a legitimate conclusion is drawn from them—and if this conclusion is again taken as a proposition and joined with another fitting one, and a conclusion is again drawn from these, and so on—eventually, after many <term>syllogisms</term> <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is reached from two or more related premises.</note>, it will finally reach a point where there is a clash or a contrariety between the final two conclusions. Such things often lie hidden from even the wisest and most learned Writers. But if two propositions clearly contradict each other, such that the Writer or Author had forgotten the former when he wrote the latter, it is a very great error and lapse, and I do not think such an Author should be numbered among those whose Books deserve to be read.

**Seventh**, is the necessity of speaking about matters that are very profound, in which it is necessary to hide some things and reveal others. For sometimes necessity demands that one must speak here according to one proposition, and soon elsewhere according to another proposition that is contrary and repugnant to the first. In this case, care must be taken lest the <term>vulgus</term> <note>The common people or those without specialized education.</note> notice the point of contradiction, and the Author must strive to conceal it by every means.

<margin>*What kind of contradictions are in the Mishnah.*</margin>
The inconsistencies and contradictions found in the <term>Mishnah</term> <note>The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions, compiled around 200 CE.</note> and the <term>Baraitot</term> <note>Jewish oral traditions not included in the official Mishnah.</note> follow the first cause <note>The first cause, mentioned on the previous page, refers to contradictions arising from collecting various authors' opinions without naming them.</note>, as you will very frequently find among them: <note>original: "Repugnat principium fini vel primum ultimo"</note> *The beginning contradicts the end, or the first contradicts the last*; and in place of a resolution, they say: *The beginning is by Rabbi So-and-so, but the end is by Rabbi So-and-so.* Thus they often say: *Rabbi <note>Rabbi Judah the Prince, the editor of the Mishnah.</note> saw the words of Rabbi N. and decided absolutely for us according to him; and, He saw the words of Rabbi N. and pronounced absolutely according to him.* Elsewhere you will also find: *The general opinion follows Rabbi N., but this specific Mishnah is from the school of Rabbi N.,* and so on, more often than can be recounted.

<margin>*What kind of contradictions are in the Talmud.*</margin>
The inconsistencies or contrarieties found in the <term>Talmud</term> <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, comprised of the Mishnah and the Gemara (commentary).</note> follow the first and second causes. Thus you will find them often saying: *Such and such is the opinion according to Rabbi N., and such and such is the opinion according to Rabbi N. Likewise: He ruled according to him in one matter, but disagreed with him in another. Likewise: They are two <term>Amoraim</term> <note>The Jewish scholars who "spoke" or explained the oral law from roughly 200 to 500 CE.</note>, but the opinion follows Rabbi N.* And all these examples follow the first cause. Following the second cause <note>The second cause refers to an author changing their mind or retracting a previous statement.</note> are these: *Rav retracted that opinion; Likewise: And he examined which of the two opinions was the final one.* Thus they say: *The first response of Rav Ashi <note>A primary editor of the Babylonian Talmud.</note> tells us such and such; the later response tells us such and such.*

<summary>The author concludes the seven causes of textual contradictions, highlighting how deep philosophical truths sometimes require intentional concealment. He then demonstrates how these logical categories explain the various conflicting opinions found in the Mishnah and Talmud.</summary>

# AUTHOR’S PREFACE.

<meta>catchword: RABBI MOSES</meta>

<margin>The nature of the contradictions that appear to exist in the Prophets.</margin>
The discrepancies and contradictions that seem to exist in the literal sense of certain Prophetic passages are all due to the third and fourth causes <note>Referring to the logical categories established on the previous pages.</note>. Indeed, it is for the sake of these that I have provided this entire preface. As you know, the Rabbis often write: *One Scripture says such-and-such, and another Scripture says otherwise.* In this way, they first point out a contradiction and then explain it, showing that a certain condition is missing or implied, or that the subject of the sentence has shifted. For instance, when they say: *Solomon, your words do not only conflict with the words of your father, but they also contradict each other,* and so on. These instances occur primarily, and for the most part, among the Prophets when they announce the judgments of God or denounce the corrupt morals of mortals. However, our purpose here is only to provide warning regarding those passages where there is an apparent contradiction in matters of Faith and Religion; I intend to explain some of these because they also concern the <term>secrets of the Law</term> <note>The "secrets of the Law" (Arcana Legis) refers to the deeper, esoteric meanings of the Torah that were traditionally hidden from the unlearned.</note>. But if you find a contradiction in the Prophets according to the seventh cause, then there is a need for deep consideration and exploration; nothing should be decided or pronounced rashly or thoughtlessly.

<margin>The nature of contrariety among the Philosophers.</margin>
The contrariety found among true Philosophers is according to the fifth cause.

<margin>The source of contradictions in other writers.</margin>
That which is found among other writers and commentators arises from the sixth cause. Many contradictions of this kind can be found in the <term>Midrashim</term> <note>Ancient commentaries on Hebrew Scriptures that use storytelling and homilies to explain laws or ethics.</note> and <term>Haggadot</term> <note>Non-legal Rabbinic traditions including stories, legends, and folklore.</note>, concerning which they say: <note>original Hebrew: "Ein makshin ba-Haggadah"</note> *One does not raise objections*—or *one does not proceed by formal logic—in the Haggadah.* One may also find contradictions in those works according to the seventh cause.

<margin>The source of contradictions in this book.</margin>
Finally, any contradiction or contrariety found in this Book <note>Maimonides' "Guide for the Perplexed."</note> arises from the fifth and seventh causes. This is something you must diligently notice and observe, so that you do not become "perplexed" in some of its chapters.

Therefore, with these things prefaced and set forth, I shall now begin the explanation of Terms, the sense of which is necessary to know and understand, so that one may know how they are to be taken in individual passages. For this shall be like a Key, by whose power we are admitted into places whose gates are closed; but when they are opened, a man enters them, souls find rest in them, eyes are feasted, and finally, bodies are refreshed from all labor and trouble.

->*Samuel, son of Judah, son of Tibbon says; Thus says the Rabbi who is the Author of this book (may the memory of the righteous and holy be a blessing): Open the gates, that the righteous nation which keeps faith and truth may enter.*<-

<margin>Isaiah 26:2.</margin>

# THE BOOK OF RABBI MOSES, SON OF MAIMON:
## The Book
# GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED:
->*That is,*<-
## THE TEACHER OF THE PERPLEXED,
->Translated into English.<- <note>original: "Latinè translatus" (Translated into Latin)</note>

### *PART ONE.*
### CHAPTER I.
### *Concerning the terms Tzelem & Demut, that is, Image & Likeness.*

<margin>Genesis 1:26</margin>Common people generally think that *Tzelem* <note>original: צלם (Tzelem)</note> in the Hebrew language signifies the likeness and form of a thing. This has led them to believe that God is entirely <term>corporeal</term> <note>Having a physical body (corporeitas).</note>, because the Scripture says: *Let us make man in our image, after our likeness* <note>original: "Faciamus hominem bidmutenu kidmutenu in imagine nostra, ad similitudinem nostram"</note>. They imagine that God has the same shape and figure as humans, and that physical existence is necessary for Him. From this, they have firmly believed in God's physical nature and estimated that if they were to turn away from this opinion, the Holy Scripture would be found guilty of falsehood. Furthermore, they think that God himself is denied if He is deprived of a body, a face, a countenance, hands, and other features, in the same shape and form that they themselves possess. They do, however, establish this difference: they say that His body (namely, God’s) is larger and more brilliant, and that His substance is not flesh and blood. It is only in this respect that they claim His majesty and splendor are greater. But as for those things which relate to removing corporeality from God and true... <note>The text continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>Maimonides begins his philosophical treatise by addressing the common misconception that God has a physical body, explaining that people misinterpret the Hebrew words for 'image' and 'likeness' in Genesis.</summary>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether God has a physical body. Maimonides is specifically distinguishing between three Hebrew words that are often confused: "Thoar" (physical shape), "Tzelem" (internal essence), and "Demuth" (likeness). He argues that when the Bible says man is made in God's "image," it refers to the human intellect, not a physical appearance.</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED
<meta>catchword: "ficat" (the beginning of "significat," meaning "it signifies")</meta>

...those things necessary for establishing His Unity (which cannot be true unless physical nature is removed) will be brought forward elsewhere in this book. Our purpose in this chapter is to explain the meaning of these two words: *Tzelem* <note>original Hebrew: צלם</note> and *Demuth* <note>original Hebrew: דמות</note>.

<margin>Part 1, chapter 46.</margin>
<margin>What is *Thoar*?</margin>
I say, therefore, that for FORM—as it is commonly understood as nothing other than the external appearance, shape, and representation of a thing—the proper name in the Hebrew language is *Thoar* <note>original Hebrew: תאר</note>. This is used when it is said, "Fair of form and fair of appearance" <note>Genesis 39:6; original Latin: "Jephethoar Pulcher formâ, & Pulcher afpectu"</note>. Likewise, "What is his form?" <note>1 Samuel 28:14; original Latin: "Mathoaro Quæ est forma hujus?"</note>. Likewise, "Like the form of the sons of a king" <note>Judges 8:18; original Latin: "Kethoar Secundùm formam filiorum Regis"</note>. From this, the word is also used to describe an artificial or crafted form, as in: "He marks it out with a line; he marks it out with a compass" <note>Isaiah 44:13; original Latin: "Jethoarehu Format illud amufsi: Et circino Jethoarehu format illud"</note>. This term *Thoar* in no way applies to God, the Blessed. Far be it!

<margin>Genesis 39:6.
1 Samuel 28:14.
Judges 8:18.
Isaiah 44:13.</margin>
<margin>What is *Tzelem*?</margin>
However, the word *Tzelem* <note>original Hebrew: צלם</note> is used to describe the <term>Natural Form</term> <note>In the philosophy of the time, this refers to the "essence" or the internal qualities that make a thing what it is, rather than its outer shape.</note>—that is, the very essence by which a thing exists and is what it is. For example, in a human being, this "form" is the quality by which "Intellectual Apprehension" exists. Because of this, it is said of man, "In the image of God He created him" <note>Genesis 1:27; original Latin: "Bezelem In imagine Dei creavit ipfum"</note>. Likewise, it is said, "You shall despise their image" <note>Psalm 73:20; original Latin: "Tzalmam Imaginem ipforum contemnis"</note>. For the "contempt" here refers to the soul—meaning the internal and <term>specific form</term> <note>The defining characteristic of a species.</note>—and not to the arrangement and shape of the limbs (the external form). 

<margin>Genesis 1:26.
Psalm 73:20.</margin>
<margin>Why are images or idols called *Tzelamim*?</margin>
Thus I say that "images" or "idols" are called *Tzelamim* because of the power which their worshipers attribute to them in their imagination, and not because of their external shape. I say the same regarding the phrase "Images of their tumors" <note>1 Samuel 6:5; original Latin: "Tzalme Imagines anorum fuorum"</note>; this word *Tzelem* is used for them not because of their outward shape, but because of the spiritual power and influence attributed to them, by which the plague of hemorrhoids affecting the Philistines was removed. 

<margin>1 Samuel 6:5, 11.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 1:26.</margin>
<margin>What does *Demuth* signify?</margin>
If, however, idols and these "images of tumors" are called by this name entirely in respect to their external shapes, then the word *Tzelem* would be a <term>homonym</term> <note>A word that has two or more distinct and unrelated meanings.</note>, used both for the specific internal form and for the artificial external shape. But truly, what is said in "Let us make man in our image" <note>Genesis 1:26; original: "bezalmenu"</note> refers to the specific internal form—the capacity for rational thought—and by no means to the external shape and figure. Thus we have explained the difference between *Tzelem* and *Thoar*, and the meaning of *Tzelem*. It follows now that we discuss the word *Demuth*.

*Demuth* <note>original Hebrew: דמות</note> is a verbal noun from the root *Damah* <note>original Hebrew: דמה</note>, "to be like," and it signifies...

<summary>Maimonides distinguishes the Hebrew word "Thoar" (physical shape) from "Tzelem" (essential form or intellect), arguing that when the Bible describes man as made in God's image, it refers to the human mind rather than a physical body. He supports this by analyzing how these words are used in different biblical contexts, including references to idols and the Philistine plague.</summary>
<keywords>Maimonides, Tzelem, Thoar, Demuth, image of God, intellect, corporeality, Hebrew linguistics, Biblical exegesis, Guide for the Perplexed</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Hebrew word "Demuth" (likeness) to prove that biblical descriptions of God do not imply He has a physical body.
