# Part I. Chapter I. (Continued)
<sig>A 2</sig>

<margin>Psalm 102:7</margin>
...[The word *Demuth* <note>original Hebrew: דמות</note>] signifies **Likeness** in a specific quality or characteristic. For example: "I have become **like** <note>original Hebrew: *Damithi*; original Latin: *Assimilatus sum*</note> a pelican of the desert." This does not refer to a likeness in wings or feathers, but rather a likeness in wailing and grief. Similarly: "No tree in the garden of God **was like** <note>original Hebrew: *damah*</note> it in its beauty." Here, the comparison is made in respect to beauty. Also: "Their venom is **according to the likeness** <note>original Hebrew: *Kidmuth*</note> of the venom of a serpent." Also: "**His likeness** <note>original Hebrew: *Dimjono*</note> is as a lion." All of these examples denote a likeness in a certain attribute, not in physical shape or figure. In this same way, we should understand the phrase: "**The likeness** <note>original Hebrew: *Demuth*</note> of a throne." By "likeness of a throne," the text means highness and majesty, not its squareness, thickness, or length, as the common people mistakenly imagine. And it is in this sense that we must also understand the phrase: "**The likeness** of the living creatures."

<margin>Ezekiel 31:8</margin>
<margin>Psalm 58:5</margin>
<margin>Psalm 17:12</margin>
<margin>Ezekiel 1:26</margin>
<margin>Ezekiel 1:5, 13</margin>

<margin>*Why is Man said to be created in the Image and likeness of God?*</margin>
Since, therefore, Man possesses a unique property that belongs to him alone—and is found in no other creature under the sphere of the Moon—namely, the **Intellectual Faculty** (or Apprehension), which does not require any physical sense or the work of the body (such as the hand, foot, etc.), Scripture therefore likens this to the Intellect of the Creator. This is because the Divine Intellect likewise does not exist within a physical instrument or bodily limb. Even though this likeness is not a "true" or perfect one (since every analogy "limps" <note>This is a Latin idiom meaning no comparison is perfect in every detail</note>), but is rather <term>according to common conception</term> <note>original Greek: κατὰ δόξαν (kata doxan); meaning a likeness as perceived by the mind rather than an exact physical replica</note>; nevertheless, it is because of this Divine Intellect within him that Man is said to be "in the **Image and likeness of God.**" It is certainly not because God possesses a body or an external shape.

### Chapter II.

->The solution to a certain objection regarding the verse:<-
->**And you shall be as gods, knowing good**<-
->**and evil.**<-

Thirty-six years ago, a certain wise and learned man raised an objection to me, which I judge to be worthy of consideration, along with our responses regarding its solution.

<margin>*Elohim applies to God, Angels, and Judges.*</margin>
<margin>*See Part 2, Chapter 6.*</margin>
<margin>Genesis 3:5</margin>
Before I recount that objection and its solution, I must first point out (as anyone learned in Hebrew knows) that the name **Elohim** <note>original Hebrew: אלהים</note> is "equivocal" <note>meaning a word that has several distinct meanings</note>. It applies to God, to Angels, and to the judges who govern provinces. Onkelos the Proselyte <note>A famous 2nd-century translator of the Torah into Aramaic</note>, of blessed memory, very truly explained that verse—"And you shall be as **Elohim**, knowing good and evil"—using this last meaning. He interpreted it as: "And you shall be as **Princes**."

<summary>Maimonides concludes that "likeness" to God refers to the human intellect rather than physical form, then begins Chapter 2 by explaining that the Hebrew word 'Elohim' can mean God, angels, or human rulers.</summary>
<keywords>Demuth, likeness, Intellectual Faculty, Image of God, Elohim, Onkelos, Genesis 3:5, Maimonides, linguistics, incorporeality</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues from Chapter 1, where the author defines "likeness" to clarify that God has no physical body. It now transitions into Chapter 2, addressing a theological challenge regarding the story of Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden.</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

<margin>The Objection.</margin> Having laid down this foundation concerning the multiple meanings of that word <note>Referring to "Likeness" or *Demuth* from the previous chapter</note>, I will now present the objection itself. An Opponent has said: It seems from the simple sense of Scripture that the "first intention" <note>The original purpose or design</note> in the creation of Man was for him to be like the other animals, without **Intellect** or thought, and not knowing how to discern between good and evil. However, when he rebelled, it was that very rebellion that produced in him this great perfection that is unique to him: namely, this Knowledge or Discernment that exists within us. This faculty is the most noble of all things within us; it is that by which we are sustained as a substance, and by which we are what we are. And it is a wondrous thing that the punishment for his disobedience should have given him a perfection he previously lacked, namely, **Intellect**. This is just as if someone were to say: "A certain man sinned and committed a most grave crime, and therefore they changed his creation for the better" (that is, *his condition was rendered better and more excellent*) "and he was set in the firmament like a star." This is the sum and sense of that objection. Now, hear also our response.

<margin>The Response.</margin> We respond: You are a man who speculates only at the very start of his meditations and thoughts (that is, *one who judges too lightly and precipitously*) and who thinks he can perfectly understand the Book (namely, *of our Law* <note>The Torah</note>), which is the Guide (of *life and actions*) for both those who came before and those who come after (namely, *for all people of all ages*). You treat it as if you were skimming it in your spare time, after a feast or lovemaking, as one usually reads a book of history or poetry. But pay attention and turn your mind to it; for the matter is not at all as you thought at the start of your speculation. Instead, it is as will now be explained to you with a more accurate consideration of the matter itself.

The **Intellect**, which the Creator first infused into Adam (which is his ultimate perfection), was bestowed upon him even before his rebellion (that is, *the Fall*). For it is because of that Intellect that it is said of Man that he was created in the **Image** and **likeness** of God. It is also because of this Intellect that God is said to have spoken with him and given him commandments, as it is written: <margin>Gen. 2:16.</margin> *And the* **LORD GOD** *commanded Adam.* For a commandment is not given to brute beasts or to those lacking intellect and reason. Through the **Intellect**, man is able to discern between **truth and falsehood**; and this was implanted in him for his perfection and completion. However, "Shameful" (or deformed) and "Beautiful" are terms used for things perceived by the senses, not for intellectual truths. For we do not say "it is beautiful" that the heavens are round, or "it is shameful" that the Earth is spread out,

<summary>This page addresses a theological objection: if Adam gained the knowledge of Good and Evil only after sinning, was sin a "promotion"? Maimonides responds that Intellect (the ability to discern Truth from Falsehood) was a gift given before the Fall, whereas "Good and Evil" are lower-level social or sensory perceptions.</summary>

<keywords>Intellect, Fall of Man, Adam, Image of God, Truth and Falsehood, Good and Evil, Maimonides, theological objection, discernment</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 2. The author is explaining that before the Fall, Adam possessed a pure "Intellect" focused on objective truth, and only after the Fall did he descend into the subjective world of "good and evil" (morality and social convention).</meta>

PART I. CHAPTER II.
A 3

...extended <note>original: "tensa," likely part of "extensa" from the previous page</note>, but rather **Truth** or **Falsehood** is predicated of them. And so, concerning the certainty or uncertainty of a thing, we speak in our language of **Truth** and **Falsehood**; but regarding a thing that is beautiful or deformed, we speak of **Good** or **Evil**. It is through the **Intellect** that man distinguishes truth from falsehood in intelligible things <note>Things that can be grasped by the mind rather than just the senses</note>. 

When man still existed in his integrity and perfection—specifically that of mind and Intellect, regarding which it was said of him, *And thou hast made him a little lower than the angels* <margin>Psalm 8:6</margin>—there was <margin>Psalm 8:6</margin> absolutely no capacity in him for using or grasping sensory things. For that which is most shameful among those things that clearly strike the senses—namely, the revelation of nakedness—was in no way considered shameful by him, and he did not recognize his own nakedness as shameful. 

But when he fell away, inclining toward vain desires and the lusts of the bodily senses, as it is said, *The woman saw that the tree was* <margin>Genesis 3:6</margin> *good for food, and that it was desirable to the eyes*, he was deservedly punished. For he rejected (that is, *cast aside and despised*) that **Intellectual Apprehension**. And because he sinned against that precept given to him by virtue of his Intellect, the **Apprehension of the Senses** succeeded in its place, and thus he was plunged into shameful and lustful passions. Only then did he recognize the value and worth of what he had lost, what he had been stripped of, and into what a miserable state he had fallen. 

For this reason it is said, *And you shall be like gods, knowing **good*** <margin>Genesis 3:5</margin> ***and evil***, but not, *knowing truth and falsehood*. Thus, observe that it is said, *Vattippakachna* <note>Hebrew: "And they were opened"</note> *Then the eyes of both were opened* <margin>Ibid. verse 7</margin>, and they recognized *that they were naked*, but it does not say, *And the eyes of both were opened and they saw*. For what they saw before, they also saw afterward; there were no blind spots <note>original: "scotomata," referring to visual obstructions</note> in their eyes that were later removed. Rather, it indicates that a new state arose in the man, by which he recognized as shameful in himself that which he previously did not consider shameful at all. 

For it must be known that the word for "Opening," *Pakach* <term>פקח</term> <note>meaning to open the eyes or ears to perception</note>, which is used <margin><term>פקח</term> (Pakach) What "To Open" properly signifies.</margin> there, is only spoken regarding the **revelation of cognition or knowledge**, not regarding the opening of physical or bodily sight. For example: *Vajiphkach* <note>Hebrew: "And he opened"</note> *And* <margin>Genesis 21:19</margin> *God opened her eyes*. Likewise, *Tippakachna* <note>Hebrew: "They shall be opened"</note> *Then the eyes of the* <margin>Isaiah 35:5</margin> *blind shall be opened*. Also, *Pakoach* <note>Hebrew: "Opening"</note> *Opening the ears, yet he hears not* <margin>Isaiah 42:20</margin>, just as it is <margin>Isaiah 42:20</margin> also read elsewhere, *Who have eyes to see and see not, etc.* <margin>Ezekiel 12:2</margin>

Regarding what is said of Adam, *When thou didst change his countenance, thou didst* <margin>Job 14:20</margin> *send him away*, it is to be explained thus: When he changed the object or the intention of his Countenance <note>His focus or mental direction</note>, then he was sent away or cast out (namely, *from Paradise*). For the word <term>פנים</term> (*Panim*), **Face** or **Countenance**, is derived from the verb <term>פנה</term> (*Panah*), which signifies "to turn," <term>פנים</term> (**Face**, "to turn toward") and "to behold" or "look back," as if one turns the **Face or Countenance toward something** <margin>The reason it is so called.</margin>.

<summary>This page explains that the "opening of eyes" in the Garden of Eden was a loss of intellectual clarity and a descent into subjective moral judgment rather than a gain of physical sight. Maimonides uses linguistic analysis of Hebrew terms for "opening" and "face" to argue that Adam's "fall" was a shift in his mental focus from eternal truths to sensory desires.</summary>
<keywords>Adam and Eve, Intellect, Truth vs. Falsehood, Good vs. Evil, Fall of Man, Hebrew etymology, Pakach, Panim, Garden of Eden, sensory perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Adam's fall from Chapter 2, transitioning into Chapter 3 which begins a linguistic analysis of terms for "form" and "likeness" in the Hebrew Bible.</meta>

...to turn the matter, because man is intent with his face toward that thing which his intention desires. He says, therefore, that when Adam <note>original: "Adamus"</note> changed his focus and directed it toward that thing concerning which he had previously been commanded not to turn his eyes, God cast him out of the garden of pleasure or Paradise. This was a punishment fitting the sin, according to the common saying, *Measure for measure* <note>original: "Mensura pro mensura"</note>. He had been permitted to eat and enjoy the sweet and most delicate fruits of the garden quietly and securely; but when his concupiscence <note>Strong sensory desire or lust</note> grew, and he followed the delights of his imagination <note>original: "phantasiæ"</note> (as we said before), and ate of that which he had been forbidden to eat, all those things were taken away from him. He was forced to eat much coarser food, which he would have never used before, <margin>Genesis 3:18, 19</margin> and that with the greatest labor and hardship, as it is said: *Thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to thee etc. In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat thy bread etc.* This is <margin>v. 23</margin> more clearly explained when it says, *And God sent him forth from the garden of pleasure to till the ground from which he was taken.* And so, in his food and many other things <margin>v. 18</margin>, He made him equal to the beasts, as it is said: *And thou shalt eat the herb of the field.* In this sense, Scripture says elsewhere: *Man did not abide in honor; he is likened to the beasts that perish.* Blessed be God, whose mind and wisdom is incomprehensible.

### Chapter III.

->The words **Temunah** <note>Hebrew: תְּמוּנָה, meaning Likeness or Image</note> and **Tabhnith** <note>Hebrew: תַּבְנִית, meaning Structure or Pattern</note> are explained.<-

The common people believe that these two words, *Temunah* and *Tabhnith*, signify one and the same thing in the Hebrew language. But the reality is otherwise.

<margin>**Tabhnith** (Structure), what it signifies.</margin> For **Tabhnith** <note>Hebrew: תַּבְנִית</note> is a verbal noun from the root **Banah** <note>Hebrew: בָּנָה, meaning "to build"</note>, and it signifies the *Structure* and *Arrangement* of any thing; that is, its form in terms of squareness, roundness, triangularity, or other similar <margin>Exodus 25:9</margin> figures. Thus it is said, *The **Tabhnith** (Structure or Pattern) of the Tabernacle, and* <margin>ibid. v. 40</margin> *the **Tabhnith** (Pattern) of all its instruments.* Likewise, *In their **Tabhnith**, according to the structure or form of those things which were shown to thee on the mountain.* Likewise <margin>Deuteronomy 4:17</margin>, *The **Tabhnith** (Form) of any bird.* Likewise, *The **Tabhnith** (Form) of a hand.* Likewise, <margin>Ezekiel 8:3</margin> *The **Tabhnith** (Structure) of a porch.* All these designate the Form or <margin>1 Chronicles 28:11</margin> external figure; and therefore, in the Hebrew language, this word is not attributed to those Forms that are attributed to God.

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of Adam’s exile from Paradise as a shift from intellectual bliss to animalistic labor and then begins a linguistic distinction between the Hebrew words for physical structure (Tabhnith) and divine likeness.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Paradise, Fall of Man, Measure for Measure, Tabhnith, Hebrew linguistics, Biblical Hebrew, divine form</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of Hebrew terms applied to God. It transitions from the word "Themunah" (form/likeness) in Chapter 3 to an exploration of verbs related to "seeing" (Raah, Chasah, Hibbit) in Chapter 4, specifically addressing how these sensory words describe intellectual or spiritual understanding when applied to the Divine.</meta>

Part I. Chapter III.

<margin>Concerning the word **Themunah** <note>Hebrew: תְּמוּנָה, meaning likeness, form, or image</note>, of which things it is spoken, and in what sense it is spoken of God.
Deuteronomy 4:16.
Ibid. verse 15.
Job 4:13, 16.
Numbers 12:8.</margin>
The word **Themunah** is a noun used in three different ways. First, it is used for the **Form** of something perceived by the senses outside of the Mind—that is, an external shape—as when it is said: *And you make for yourselves a carved image, the **themunah** [likeness] or figure of any idol.* Likewise: *Because you did not observe any **themunah** [likeness] or figure.* Second, it is used for an **Imaginary Form** that exists in the imagination and fantasy of a person caught up in an ecstasy <note>A state of being overwhelmed by emotion or religious fervor, often associated with prophetic visions</note>. In this sense, it is used in that passage: *Amid thoughts from visions of the night, etc.* At the end of that passage, it says: *While it stood still, I did not recognize its appearance; a **themunah** [likeness] was before my eyes*—that is, a **Dimjono** <note>Hebrew: דִּמְיוֹן, a likeness or resemblance</note>, an image of it appearing before my eyes. Third, it is used for a **true thing** which is apprehended by the Mind. And in this third meaning, the word **Themunah** is used of the Most High, as in the verse: *And the **temunato** [likeness] of the Lord shall he behold.* For the meaning is: *And he shall apprehend the truth of God*—that is, His very nature.

### Chapter IV.

### In which three Verbs of Seeing are explained:
### Raah, Chasah, Hibbit <note>Hebrew: רָאָה (to see), חָזָה (to gaze/prophesy), הִבִּיט (to look)</note>.

<margin>In what sense the Verbs of Seeing are attributed to God.</margin>
One should know that these three Verbs—*Raah, Chasah, Hibbit*—are indeed spoken of the vision of the eye; however, they are also transferred to the Apprehension of the Mind <note>This refers to intellectual understanding rather than physical sight</note>.

<margin>The Verb **Raah** [To See].
Genesis 29:2.
Ecclesiastes 1:16.
2 Chronicles 18:18.
Genesis 26:2.
Genesis 1.
Exodus 33:18.
Exodus 24:10.</margin>
Regarding the Verb **Raah**, it is commonly known, as in: *And he looked [Vaijar], and behold, a well in a field*; this is understood as the vision of the eye. But when it is said: *And my heart saw [raah] a multitude of wisdom and knowledge*, it is understood as the Apprehension of the Mind. In this sense, this verb **Raah** is always used when it is spoken of God, the Best and Greatest: such as, *I saw [Raithi] the Lord*; *And the Lord appeared [Vajera] to him*; Likewise, *And the Lord saw [Vajar] that it was good.* Likewise, *Please show [Hareni] me your glory.* Likewise, *And they saw [Vajiru] the God of Israel.* All these are to be understood as the Apprehension of the Mind, and in no way as bodily vision. For eyes apprehend only a body, and indeed only according to certain "accidents" <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are qualities like color or size that aren't the essential nature of a thing</note>—beauty, size, proportion, and similar things. Thus, God also does not apprehend through any bodily instrument, as will be declared elsewhere.

<margin>The Verb **Hibbit** [To Look/Aspire].
Genesis 19:17.
Ibid. verse 26.
Isaiah 5:30.</margin>
As for the verb **Hibbit**, it also is primarily taken for the gazing of the eye upon some thing: as in, *Do not look [tabbit] behind you.* Likewise, *But his wife looked back [Vattabbet] from behind him.* Likewise, *And he shall look [Venibbat] toward the land.*

<summary>This page analyzes the Hebrew word 'Themunah' (form) and the verb 'Raah' (to see), arguing that when applied to God, they refer to intellectual understanding rather than physical sight or shape. It emphasizes that God is non-physical and can only be "seen" through the mind's apprehension of truth.</summary>

<keywords>Themunah, form, Raah, vision, intellectual apprehension, incorporeality of God, Maimonides, Hebrew philology, biblical interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Hebrew verbs for "seeing" (Hibbit and Chazah), concluding Chapter 4. GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

Furthermore, the term is also applied to the Intuition or Vision of the Intellect, and the effort of the mind to grasp a certain thing. It is used in this sense when it is said: <margin>Numbers 23:21</margin> *Lo hibbit* <note>Hebrew: לֹא־הִבִּיט; original Latin: "Non aspexit" — He has not seen</note> *iniquity in Jacob*: for iniquity cannot be perceived by the eyes of the body. So it is also said: <margin>Exodus 33:8</margin> *Vehibbitu* <note>Hebrew: וְהִבִּיטוּ; original Latin: "Et respexerunt" — And they looked</note> *after Moses*. Indeed, our Masters <note>A reference to the Sages of the Talmud, often followed by "p.m." for *piae memoriae*, meaning "of blessed memory."</note> have taught that in this instance too, the word has the same meaning, indicating nothing other than that they diligently examined and considered the words and deeds of Moses. <margin>Genesis 15:5</margin> This same sense must be applied to the passage: *Habbet na* <note>Hebrew: הַבֶּט־נָא; original Latin: "Respice nunc" — Look now</note> *toward the heavens*. For this occurred in a Prophetic Vision. And according to this significance, the verb *Habat* <note>Hebrew: הִבִּיט; to look or regard</note> is always used when it occurs in relation to God, the Blessed: as in <margin>Exodus 3:6</margin> *Mehabbit* <note>Hebrew: מֵהַבִּיט; original Latin: "Respicere" — To look/beholding</note> *upon God*. Likewise: <margin>Numbers 12:8</margin> *And the similitude of the Lord shall he jabbit* <note>Hebrew: יַבִּיט; original Latin: "respiciet" — behold</note>. Likewise: <margin>Habakkuk 1:13</margin> *Vehabbit* <note>Hebrew: וְהַבִּיט; original Latin: "Et aspicere" — And to look</note> *on wickedness You cannot*.

<margin>The verb **Chazah** (To see).</margin> Finally, the verb *Chazah* <note>Hebrew: חָזָה; a root meaning to see, behold, or perceive</note> is similarly used both for the Vision of the Eye: as in <margin>Micah 4:11</margin> *Vattachas* <note>Hebrew: וְתַחַז; original Latin: "Et aspiciet" — And let look</note> *our eye upon Zion*; and for the Apprehension of the intellect: as in <margin>Isaiah 2:1</margin> *Which he chaza* <note>Hebrew: חָזָה; original Latin: "vidit" — saw</note> *concerning Judah and Jerusalem*. <margin>Genesis 15:1</margin> Likewise: *The word of the Lord came to Abraham bammachaseh* <note>Hebrew: בַּמַּחֲזֶה; original Latin: "in visione" — in a vision</note>. And in <margin>Exodus 24:11</margin> it is used in this sense: *Vajechezu* <note>Hebrew: וַיֶּחֱזוּ; original Latin: "Et viderunt" — And they saw</note> *God*, which is a point that must be carefully noted.

### Chapter V.

->*That one must not approach the contemplation of divine things without prior knowledge and understanding of other sciences, and without the amendment of one's life.*<-

<margin>See Aristotle, *On the Heavens*, book 2, chapter 12.</margin> When the Prince of Philosophers (*Aristotle*) began to investigate and construct demonstrations regarding matters of great depth, he wrote in his own defense to this effect: That the reader of his books should not ascribe his work to audacity or rashness, much less to ambition and arrogance, as if he were attempting a subject of which no knowledge could be had; but rather, it is fitting that the reader attribute it to a fervor and zeal for establishing certain and solid disciplines, as far as is possible within the infirmity of human affairs. <margin>How this Divine study is to be commenced.</margin> We wish the same to be said here. For a man ought not to rush precipitously into this grave and noble subject at the very beginning of his thinking, before he has properly exercised himself in other sciences and arts, thoroughly purified his morals, and [subdued] his passions and desires...

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of Hebrew verbs for "seeing" (Hibbit and Chazah), explaining that they denote intellectual perception when applied to God. It then introduces Chapter 5, emphasizing that the study of metaphysics requires a foundation in logic, science, and personal virtue, citing Aristotle's humility as a model.</summary>
<keywords>Habat, Chazah, intellectual perception, prophetic vision, Aristotle, moral purification, preparation for study, divine contemplation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Part I, Chapter 5 of the "Guide for the Perplexed." The previous page concluded a philological study of the Hebrew verbs for seeing, while this page transitions into the ethical and intellectual requirements for divine contemplation, specifically the need for humility and preparation before attempting to grasp the nature of God.</meta>

### Part I. Chapter V.

...[after he] has mortified imaginary desires. Once he has understood and known the true and real principles, as well as the methods of reasoning—namely, syllogisms <note>A form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note> and demonstrations—and the way in which he may guard himself against the seductions and fallacies of the intellect, only then may he rightly begin to investigate these matters as well. Truly, he should not immediately seize upon the first thing that offers itself or comes to his mind; nor should he allow his thoughts to wander too freely and securely from the beginning in his **Apprehension of the Creator**. Rather, he must proceed with modesty and slowness, until he has well-considered and weighed each point. For this reason it is written of Moses: <margin>Exodus 3:6</margin> *And he hid his face; for he was afraid to look upon God.* For while it is mentioned that this vision terrified him so much that he did not dare to look back at that created and visible light, it also teaches us that his eyes apprehended God, the Best and Greatest <note>original Latin: "Deum Opt. Max." (Optimum Maximum), a title often used to denote God's supreme perfection.</note>, as being free from all imperfection. This surely results in his greatest praise: that God, out of His goodness, poured out upon him as much as he could grasp, as it is said: <margin>Numbers 12:8</margin> *And the similitude of the <term>Lord</term> <note>original Latin: "Domini." Usually translating the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God.</note> shall he behold.*

Hence our Sages have also said that it was his reward for having hidden his face so that he would not see God. In contrast, the Nobles of the Israelites, because they rushed into this matter with "unwashed hands" <note>A metaphor for lack of spiritual and intellectual preparation.</note> and gave room to their own private thoughts, achieved only an imperfect apprehension. Hence it is said of them: <margin>Exodus 24:10</margin> *And they saw the God of Israel, and under his feet, etc.* It does not say simply, *And they saw the God of Israel*, for that would have declared generally or simply that they saw something, without explaining the manner and reason of how they saw. Instead, it explains specifically and accurately the measure of their apprehension: that it was only of **corporeality** <note>original Latin: "corporeitatis." The state of being material or having a physical body.</note>—that is, of material things and the works of God. This was the most just punishment for their rashness, because they broke through to this point before they were perfected. For this they deserved to be consumed, but Moses interceded for them until they were later consumed at Taberah. <margin>Numbers 11:3</margin> Nadab and Abihu, however, were consumed in the tent of meeting, according to the true tradition that has reached us on this matter.

If this, then, was the judgment upon them, how much greater will be ours, who are far inferior to them, and the judgment of those who are even lower than us? Therefore, it is necessary that every one of us should labor diligently in acquiring the foundations required for this matter, and in understanding those principles which purge our apprehension from all pollution—that is, from seduction and deception—and only then approach these holy and divine camps...

B

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

Next, it is also adapted to the Intuition or Sight of the Intellect, and its intention toward attaining some thing. It is used in this sense when it is said: <margin>Numbers 23:21</margin> *Lo hibbit* <note>original: "Lo hibbit" (Hebrew: לֹא־הִבִּיט)</note> *He has not beheld iniquity in Jacob*; for iniquity cannot be perceived by the eyes of the body. So it is also said: <margin>Exodus 33:8</margin> *Vehibbitu* <note>original: "Vehibbitu" (Hebrew: וְהִבִּיטוּ)</note> *And they looked after Moses*. Indeed, our Masters <note>A traditional reference to the Jewish Sages of the Talmudic era.</note> (of blessed memory) have taught that here too it has the same meaning, and nothing else is indicated than that they diligently examined and considered the words and deeds <margin>Genesis 15:5</margin> of Moses. In this same sense must be understood that passage, *Habbet na* <note>original: "Habbet na" (Hebrew: הַבֶּט־נָא)</note> *Look now toward heaven*. For this took place in a Prophetic Vision. And according to this meaning the word *hibbit* <note>original Hebrew: הביט</note> is always taken when it occurs regarding God, the Blessed: as in, <margin>Exodus 3:6; Numbers 12:8; Habakkuk 1:13</margin> *Mehabbit* <note>original: "Mehabbit" (Hebrew: מֵהַבִּיט)</note> *To behold God*. Also, *And the similitude of the Lord jabbit* <note>original: "jabbit" (Hebrew: יַבִּיט)</note> *he shall behold*. Also, *Vehabbit* <note>original: "Vehabbit" (Hebrew: וְהַבִּיט)</note> *And you cannot behold wickedness*.

<margin>The verb 'Chazah' To See.</margin> Finally, the verb *Chazah* <note>original Hebrew: חזה</note> is similarly used both for the Vision of the Eye: as in, <margin>Micah 4:11</margin> *Vattachas* <note>original: "Vattachas" (Hebrew: וְתַחַז)</note> *And let our eye look upon Zion*: and for the Apprehension of the intellect: as in, <margin>Isaiah 2:1</margin> *Which chasa* <note>original: "chasa" (Hebrew: חָזָה)</note> *he saw concerning Judah and Jerusalem*. <margin>Genesis 15:1</margin> Also, *The word of the Lord came to Abraham bammachaseh* <note>original: "bammachaseh" (Hebrew: בַּמַּחֲזֶה)</note> *in a vision*. And in <margin>Exodus 24:11</margin> it is said in this sense, *Vajechesu* <note>original: "Vajechesu" (Hebrew: וַיֶּחֱזוּ)</note> *And they saw God*, which should be carefully noted.

### CHAPTER V.

->*That one must not approach the contemplation of divine matters without prior knowledge and understanding of other sciences, and without the amendment of life.*<-

<margin>See book 2 of "On the Heavens" chapter 12.</margin> When the Prince of Philosophers (*Aristotle*) began to investigate and construct proofs in very profound matters, he wrote in this sense for his own excuse: that the reader of his books should not ascribe it to his audacity or rashness, much less to ambition and arrogance, as if he were attempting such a thing of which no knowledge could be had; but rather it is fitting that he attribute it to fervor and zeal for establishing certain and solid disciplines, as much as can be done in this weakness of human affairs. <margin>How this Divine study is to be begun.</margin> We wish the same to be said here. For a man ought not to rush headlong into this grave and noble material at the start of his thinking, before he has well-exercised himself in other sciences and arts, has thoroughly purified his character, and [subdued] his passions and desires...

# PART I. CHAPTER V.
<sig>B</sig>

...mortified imaginary cravings <note>The text likely completes the word "concupiscentias," referring to the mastery of physical or irrational desires.</note>. After he has understood and known the true and real Principles, as well as the methods of reasoning—namely Syllogisms and Demonstrations—and the way to guard himself against the seductions and fallacies of the intellect; then he can properly begin to investigate these matters as well. But he should not immediately seize upon the first thing that presents itself and comes to mind; nor should he allow his thoughts to wander too freely and carelessly from the beginning regarding the **Perception of the Creator** <note>original: "Apprehenſione Creatoris"</note>. Rather, he must proceed here with modesty and slowness until he has carefully considered and weighed each detail. For this reason, it is written of Moses: <margin>Exodus 3:6.</margin> *And he hid his face; for he was afraid to look upon God.* 

For since it is mentioned that this vision so terrified him that he did not dare look toward that created and visible light, we are simultaneously taught that his eyes perceived God, the Best and Greatest, who is free from all imperfection. This act certainly resulted in his highest praise, and because God, out of His goodness, poured upon him as much as he could contain, as it is said: <margin>Numbers 12:8.</margin> *And he shall behold the likeness of the LORD.* 

Hence our Sages <note>A traditional reference to the Jewish scholars of antiquity.</note> have also said that this was his reward: that because he hid his face so as not to see God, he was granted a higher vision later. On the contrary, the Nobles of the Israelites, because they rushed into this business with "unwashed hands" <note>A metaphor for being intellectually or spiritually unprepared.</note> and gave room to their own private thoughts, they did indeed perceive, but with an imperfect perception. Hence it is said of them: <margin>Exodus 24:10.</margin> *And they saw the God of Israel, and under his feet, etc.* It does not say only, *And they saw the God of Israel*; for that would have generally or simply declared that they saw something, but not the mode and manner in which they saw. 

Instead, it explains specifically and accurately the measure of their perception: namely, that it was only of corporeality (that is, *of corporeal things, the works of God*). This was the most just punishment for their rashness, that they burst forward before they were perfect. For this reason, they would have deserved to be consumed; but Moses interceded for them until they were later consumed at Taberah. <margin>Numbers 11:3.</margin> Nadab and Abihu, however, were consumed in the tent of meeting, according to the true tradition that has reached us on this matter. 

If this was the judgment upon them, how much greater will be ours, who are far inferior to them—and the judgment of those who are even inferior to us? Therefore, it is necessary that each of us labors diligently to acquire the foundations required for this matter, and to understand those principles which purge our perception <note>original: "Apprehenſionem"</note> from all pollution—that is, from seduction and deception—and only then approach these holy and divine camps...

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

<margin>Exodus 19:22</margin>
...should approach the contemplation of these things, as it is said: *Let the priests also, who come near the Lord, sanctify themselves, lest the Lord break forth against them.* Solomon also, the wisest of all mortals, carefully warned us that we must be extremely cautious when we attempt to reach this level,
<margin>Ecclesiastes 4:17</margin>
when he says: *Guard your foot when you go to the house of God.* <note>Maimonides uses this verse to emphasize the need for intellectual humility and careful preparation before engaging in metaphysics.</note> But I return now to complete that which we set out to explain, and I say that the Nobles of the children of Israel <note>A reference to the seventy elders who ascended Mount Sinai with Moses.</note>, among other obstacles and stumbling blocks that they encountered in their apprehension, also felt confusion due to their actions and physical pleasures.
<margin>Exodus 24:11</margin>
For this reason it is said: *And they saw God, and they ate and drank.* However, the end of that passage, where it is said, *And under His feet was like the work of a*
<margin>See below, Part 3, Ch. 4 and Part 1, Ch. 28</margin>
*sapphire stone,* etc., will be explained in other chapters of this book. Our entire intention here is that these words for "seeing"—<term>Raah, Chasah, and Hibbit</term> <note>original: "Raah, Chasah, Hibbit". These are different Hebrew verbs for sight or perception.</note>—when they occur in this context, are to be understood as the perception of the Intellect, not the vision of the eye. For the essence of God does not allow the physical eyes to perceive it. If, however, there is someone who, due to the slowness and thinness of their intellect, cannot reach this level to which we attempt to raise them, and insists that all these words refer to sensory perception—such as a "created light" (that is, a visible appearance of Divine Majesty) or to angels—there is no danger in this belief for the common person.

### CHAPTER VI.

->In which are explained איש *Ish* (Man) and אשה *Ishah*<-
->(Woman); likewise אח *Ach* (Brother) and<-
->אחות *Achoth* (Sister).<-

<margin>*Ish* and *Ishah* explained.</margin>
These two nouns, *Ish* and *Ishah* <note>original: "Isch & Ischah"</note>, are attributed first to the male and female among humans. Later, they are also adapted to every
<margin>Genesis 7:2</margin>
male and female among other species of living things; as in: *From every clean beast you shall take to yourself seven, Ish ve-ishto* <note>original: "Isch veischto"</note>, *a man and his woman*, that is, a male and its female. Furthermore, the term *Ishah* is also adapted to any thing that is fit and suitable to be joined with another,
<margin>Exodus 26:3</margin>
as in: *Five curtains shall be joined, Ishah el-achotah* <note>original: "Ischah el achotah"</note>, that is, *one to its sister* (or "one to the other"). From this it should also be manifest to you that these two words, *Ach* (brother) and *Achoth* (sister), are similarly used <term>equivocally</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, an "equivocal" term is one that has multiple distinct meanings depending on context.</note> in the same way as *Ish* and *Ishah*.

<summary>Maimonides concludes Chapter V by emphasizing that "seeing God" is a metaphor for intellectual understanding and warns against rushing into deep theology without preparation. Chapter VI begins an analysis of how Hebrew terms for "man," "woman," "brother," and "sister" are used metaphorically to describe pairs of objects or related concepts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and philosophical analysis of Hebrew terms used in the Torah. Having previously discussed terms for "seeing" and physical proximity to God, Maimonides now moves to Chapter VII, which examines the word "to beget" (Yalad) and its metaphorical applications to creation, time, and intellectual inheritance.</meta>

# Part I. Chapter VII.

<sig>B 2</sig>

### Chapter VII.

->In which the word *Yalad* [ילד], "to be born" or<-
->"to beget," is explained.<-

<margin>The word *Yalad* [ילד], "to beget."
Deut. 21:15.
Psal. 90:2.</margin>
It is well known what is understood by this word *Jalad* <note>original: *ילד* (*yalad*)</note>, namely, childbirth, as in: *And they shall have borne him sons.* <note>Deuteronomy 21:15</note> Furthermore, it is also transferred to the existence or creation of natural things, as in: *Before the mountains were brought forth* <note>Psalm 90:2; original Latin: *julledu nascentur*</note>—that is, before they were produced or existed. Moreover, it is applied to things germinating from the earth, which makes all things sprout as if it were giving birth to them, just as it is said: *And makes it bring forth and sprout.*

<margin>Isa. 55:10.</margin>
*And He makes it bring forth, and makes it sprout.* <note>Isaiah 55:10; original Latin: *Veholidah vehizmichah*</note> Beyond this, it is adapted to novelties—that is, the events of time—as if they were things being born; for instance: *For you do not know what a day may bring forth.* <note>Proverbs 27:1; original Latin: *teled paritura sit*</note> Furthermore, it is also adapted to thoughts, and the judgments and opinions that arise and are born from them, as it is said: *And he shall bring forth a lie.* <note>Psalm 7:15; original Latin: *Vejalad Et pariet mendacium*</note> It is in this sense that we find: *And they please themselves in the children of strangers* <note>Isaiah 2:6; original Latin: *Ubejalde Et in natis alienigenarum acquiescunt*</note>—that is, in their opinions, sciences, and doctrines. This is how Jonathan, son of Uzziel <note>The author of the Targum Jonathan, an ancient Aramaic translation and commentary on the Prophets.</note>, translated it: *And they walk in the laws of the nations.* And in this sense, one who teaches something to another, or who communicates a certain opinion to someone, is regarded as if he had begotten that person, insofar as he is the author of that opinion and judgment.

<margin>Prov. 27:1.
Psal. 7:15.
Isa. 2:6.</margin>
<margin>Why the disciples of the Prophets are called the "Sons of the Prophets."</margin>
Hence the disciples of the Prophets were called *Sons of the Prophets* <note>original: *Bene Filii Prophetarum*</note>, as I shall explain in the discussion on the homonymy <note>A word with multiple meanings.</note> of the word *Ben* [Son]. It is also in this sense that it was said of Adam: *And Adam lived a hundred and thirty years, and begot a son in his own likeness, after his image.* <note>Genesis 5:3; original Latin: *Vajoled & genuit in imagine sua*</note> What is meant by "in his image" and "in his likeness" has already been explained to you above. For all the other sons of Adam who preceded Seth <note>Seth is the son referenced in Genesis 5:3.</note> did not possess the true human form (properly so called)—namely, that form and image of man of which it was said: *In the image of God and His likeness.* <note>Genesis 1:26–27</note> But Seth, after Adam had educated and instructed him, stood forth perfected with human perfection, and thus it is said of him: *And he begat in his own image, in his own likeness.*

<margin>Gen. 5:3, ch. 1.</margin>
<margin>A man lacking intellect is not truly a man.</margin>
From this you can conclude that he who lacks this form is not truly a man, but a beast in human shape and appearance; except that he possesses a peculiar power for all kinds of evil and harm which other animals do not have (that is, he can cause more and deeper injury than they can). For when the Intellect and reason—which were given to him to attain perfection—do not achieve that goal, he then misuses them for all manner of destructive schemes, and...

<vocab>Jalad, Seth, Adam, Imago Dei, Intellectus, Prophetarum Filii, Jonathan Uzielis Filius</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the metaphorical meanings of the Hebrew word 'Yalad' (to beget), moving from biological birth to the creation of the world, the passing of time, and the transmission of ideas. Maimonides argues that "true" human birth refers to the passing on of intellectual and spiritual perfection, specifically citing Adam and Seth.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter VII on the metaphorical use of "begetting" and begins Chapter VIII, which examines the term "Place" (Makom) as applied to the divine essence.</meta>

...so it produces harms, as if it were only something similar to a human, expressing and reflecting him in some way. Such were the sons of Adam before Seth; hence it is said in the Midrash <note>A Midrash is a form of ancient Jewish commentary or storytelling that interprets biblical texts</note>: Through a full 130 years, Adam begot mere spirits—that is, demons—but when it pleased God, <margin>Genesis 5:3</margin> then he begot in his own likeness and image, as it is written: *And Adam lived one hundred and thirty years, and begot a son in his own likeness, after his image.*

### CHAPTER VIII.
### The word מקום (*Makom*), "Place," is explained.

<margin>The word *Makom* [מקום], meaning "Place": what it signifies and in what sense it is used regarding God.</margin> The principal and primary meaning of this word concerns "Place," both specific and general. However, this language later extended its use more broadly to denote the Dignity and Estimation of a person—that is, their perfection in a certain matter. We say, <margin>A similar phrase is found in Paul’s writing, 1 Corinthians 14:16: "he who fills the place of the uninformed." <note>original Greek: ὁ ἀναπληρῶν τὸν τόπον τοῦ ἰδιώτου</note></margin> for example, that "So-and-so is in such a place" regarding this or that matter. In this way, you know that those skilled in this language often use it, such as when they say: "He fills the place of his fathers in wisdom and the fear of the Lord"; or when they say, "The controversy remains in its place"—that is, in its current state or degree. It is in this sense that it was said: <margin>Ezekiel 3:12</margin> *Blessed be the glory of the Lord mimmekomo <note>original Hebrew: מִמְּקוֹמוֹ</note> from his place,* which means on account of the excellence and superiority of His portion among created things. Thus, whenever the word *Makom* <note>original Hebrew: מקום</note> is said of God, it truly refers to the Rank of His immense and incomparable Essence, as we shall demonstrate later.

It should be known, however, that in explaining the "homonyms" <note>words that are spelled the same but have different meanings</note> of terms in this book, our intention is not to explain every single notion of those words like grammarians or dictionary-writers do. Rather, it is to open a gate, as it were, and to alert you to the meanings that serve our purpose, so that you may navigate the writings of the Prophets and other Sages with understanding. By correctly understanding the vocabulary they use, you will be able to properly grasp each multi-meaning and equivocal word according to its genuine sense, fitting whichever passage or book it appears in. This book serves as a key to that matter. An example is this very word *Makom*, which we have explained to you so that you would know that when it is said, *Blessed be the glory of the Lord from His place*, by "Place" (*Makom*) His essence (God's) is understood. In a similar way, <margin>Exodus 33:21</margin> when it is said, *Behold, there is a place with me*, what is understood is a degree of speculation and the contemplation of the Intellect, not truly the physical eye, which was then directed toward the place shown to him on the mountain, where the Glory and the apprehension of perfection resided.

<summary>Maimonides explains that "begetting" only truly refers to humans when the offspring possesses the parent's intellectual essence, and then transitions to explaining "Place" (Makom) as a metaphor for God's rank or essence rather than a physical location.</summary>
<keywords>Makom, Place, Maimonides, More Nevochim, Essence of God, homonyms, Midrash, Adam, Seth, Ezekiel, Exodus, biblical interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the examination of anthropomorphic terms in the Hebrew Bible, moving from Chapter VIII's discussion of "Place" (Makom) to Chapter IX's discussion of "Throne" (Kisse).</meta>

Part I. Chapter IX.
B 3

### Chapter IX.

->In which the word *Kisse* [כסא]—meaning "Throne"—is explained.<-

<margin>*Kisse* [Throne]: what it is and how it is attributed to God.</margin>
It is well known what this word principally and primarily signifies in this language (*namely, a Royal Throne or Seat of Honor*). Because high-ranking and powerful people, such as Kings or Princes, are accustomed to sit upon a throne, the word *Kisse* (throne) teaches us about the Magnificence, Power, and Dignity of the one to whom it is attributed. In this way, the Sanctuary is called a *Kisse* or throne, to indicate the Power of Him who revealed Himself within it, and who placed His splendor and glory there, as it is said:
<margin>Why the Sanctuary is called a Throne.</margin>
> *A glorious throne, exalted from the beginning, is the place of our Sanctuary.* <note>original Latin: "Kisse solium gloriæ, altitudo à principio, locus Sanctuarii nostri." quoting Jeremiah 17:12.</note>

<margin>Jeremiah 17:12</margin>
<margin>Why the Heavens are called a Throne.</margin>
For the same reason, the Heavens are called *Kisse*—a throne—because they teach whoever observes and contemplates them about the Power of their Creator and Mover, and the Governor of this lower world, as He pours out His gifts from them abundantly; as in: *Thus says the Lord, Heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool.* <note>original Latin: "Sic dicit Dominus, Cœlum Kisse solium meum est, & terra scabellum pedum meorum." quoting Isaiah 66:1.</note> He means to say: "These things teach of my essence, strength, and power, just like a throne that is prepared for and befits a Prince." And this must necessarily be believed by all students and seekers of Truth: not as if there were some physical body upon which God (the Blessed) is carried. For it will be demonstrated to you that God is not a body or corporeal, and consequently does not have a "place" or a dwelling for a physical body. Therefore, these things must be understood as we have just explained. Every place that God has designated for the manifestation of His splendor and glory is called a *Kisse* [Throne]. However, the word is used much more broadly in the verse: *Because a hand is upon the throne of the Lord [Kes-jah].* <note>original Latin: "Quia manus super Kes-jah solium Domini." quoting Exodus 17:16.</note> For this refers to the very power of God, or His strength and magnificence, which must not be thought of as something outside His own essence, or as some created thing—as if God were sometimes without a throne and sometimes with one. To think such a thing is undoubtedly heresy. Jeremiah explained this to us when he said: *You, O Lord, remain forever; your throne [Kissacha] from generation to generation.* <note>original Latin: "Tu Domine in sæculum permanebis, Kissacha solium tuum in generationem & generationem." quoting Lamentations 5:19.</note> He teaches us that the Throne is something inseparable from God. Therefore, here and in all other similar places, the word *Kisse* signifies His Magnificence and Power, which are not something outside His essence, as will be declared in other chapters of this Book.

<margin>Isaiah 60:1 <note>This citation likely refers to Isaiah 66:1, though the original text cites chapter 60.</note></margin>
<margin>Exodus 17:16</margin>
<margin>Lamentations 5:19</margin>

<term>Kisse</term> [כסא]: Throne; <term>Solium</term>: Throne or seat of state; <term>Magnificentia</term>: Magnificence; <term>Potentia</term>: Power; <term>Sanctuarium</term>: Sanctuary; <term>Cœli</term>: Heavens; <term>Creatoris</term>: Of the Creator; <term>Motoris</term>: Of the Mover (the one who sets the spheres in motion); <term>Gubernatoris Mundi</term>: Of the Governor of the World; <term>Kes-jah</term>: Throne of the Lord; <term>Kissacha</term>: Your throne.

<summary>This page explains that the Hebrew word for "Throne" (Kisse) is a metaphor for God's power, magnificence, and essence rather than a physical seat. It argues that since God is incorporeal, references to His throne in the Sanctuary or the Heavens describe the places where His glory is most visible to humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Kisse, Throne, Divine Power, Incorporeality of God, Sanctuary, Heavens, Biblical metaphor, Maimonides</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of biblical terminology from the previous chapter, moving from the concept of God's "Throne" to the verbs used to describe Divine movement: descending and ascending.</meta>

# GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

### CHAPTER X.

### In which the words *Jarad* [ירד], "to descend," and *Alah* [עלה], "to ascend," are explained.

We have already stated beforehand that when we undertake to explain some <term>equivocal or homonymous</term> <note>These are terms that have multiple distinct meanings despite being the same word.</note> word (*specifically one attributed to God*) in this book, it is by no means our intention to recount every one of its meanings (for this book is not occupied with explaining language in general). Rather, we address only those meanings that seem necessary for the logic of our purpose, and nothing more. Therefore, among that number are these two words, *Jarad* [ירד] and *Alah* [עלה], whose sense and meaning are very well known.

<margin>*Jarad* (to descend) and *Alah* (to ascend): what they mean and in what sense they are attributed to God.</margin> For when a body is transferred or moved from a higher place to a lower place, it is said to *Jarad* (descend); but when it is transferred from a lower place to a taller and higher one, it is said to *Alah* (ascend).

Next, these words are transferred metaphorically to the concepts of *Magnificence* and *Power*. Hence, when someone's *Dignity* is depressed or diminished, it is said that he *Jarad* (descended); but when it is extolled and exalted, he *Alah* (ascended). Thus God says: <margin>Deuteronomy 28:43</margin> *The stranger who is in your midst shall rise* <note>original: "jaaleh" (shall ascend)</note> *higher and higher above you, and you shall sink lower and lower.* <note>original: "descendes" (shalt descend).</note> <margin>Ibid. verse 1.</margin> Likewise: *And the Lord your God will set you high* <note>original: "eljon," which is derived from the root "Alah"</note> *above all nations of the earth.* <margin>1 Chronicles 29:25</margin> Likewise: *And God magnified Solomon exceedingly* <note>original: "lemaaleh," to the highest degree or excellently</note>. You also know that our Rabbis often use this phrasing, as in the saying: *One should promote in matters of holiness, and not demote.* <note>original Latin: "Ascendere faciunt in sanctitate, & non faciunt descendere." This is a Jewish legal principle regarding the sanctity of ritual objects or the order of religious duties.</note>

Furthermore, these words are used regarding the *contemplation of the mind*. For when someone turns his thoughts to a vile and lowly thing, he is said to *Jarad* (descend); but when he turns his thoughts toward sublime and excellent things, he is said to *Alah* (ascend).

Moreover, since the human race is established in the lowest place and at the lowest degree in respect to God—and God Himself is at the highest degree, not indeed by reason of physical location, but by reason of His Essence, Majesty, and Power—therefore, whenever it pleases Him to bestow wisdom upon someone, or to pour out the gift of Prophecy upon certain ones among us, this resting of Prophecy upon someone, or the dwelling of the Majesty and Divine Presence <note>the *Shekhinah*</note> in a certain place, is called a "Descent." Conversely, the removal of Prophecy from a man, or the withdrawal of the Divine Majesty from a place, is called an "Ascent." It is almost always in this way that these words *Jarad* and *Alah* are to be accepted and understood when they are attributed to God, the Best and Greatest.

<meta>catchword: Ad-</meta>

<summary>This chapter explains that "descending" and "ascending" are used metaphorically when applied to God to describe the bestowal or withdrawal of divine inspiration and presence, rather than physical movement.</summary>
<keywords>Jarad, Alah, descent, ascent, prophecy, Divine Presence, Shekhinah, metaphors, linguistics, Maimonides</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Hebrew verbs *Jarad* (to descend) and *Alah* (to ascend), specifically focusing on how these movements represent Divine judgment or the elevation of the human intellect, rather than physical motion.</meta>

Furthermore, when a plague is about to descend upon a nation or a specific region of the earth according to the will of God—previously announced by the Prophets, who said that God would visit their works (unless they repent)—and when God later inflicts it, He is said to *Descend*. For if He did not first signify that He would do this, and thus make His will known to them, men would be too unworthy and lowly for Him to even visit and punish their works. This is declared to us in the books of the Prophets themselves, where it is said: <margin>Psalm 8:4</margin> *What is man, that you are mindful of him, and the son of man, that you visit him?* <note>The author uses this verse to show that God's "visitation" or judgment is an act of divine attention toward humans, who are otherwise insignificant.</note> For this is the sense implied in those words. And from this, such an event is called *Jeridah* <note>original: "Jeridah" (descension)</note> or "Descension": as in, <margin>Genesis 11:7</margin> *Come, let us go down* <note>original: "Neredah"</note> *and there confuse their lips.* Likewise, *And the Lord descended* <note>original: "Vajered"</note> *to see.* Likewise, *I will go down* <note>original: "Eredah"</note> *now and see.* <margin>Genesis 11:5, 18:21</margin> All of these signify the Punishment of men.

However, the first meaning mentioned earlier—namely, concerning Prophecy and Glory—is also not infrequent; as in, *And I will come down* <note>original: "Vejaradti"</note> *and speak with you there.* Likewise, <margin>Numbers 11:17, Exodus 19:20, Exodus 19:11</margin> *And the Lord had descended upon Mount Sinai.* Likewise, *And the Lord will descend in the sight of all the people upon Mount Sinai.* Likewise, <margin>Genesis 35:13</margin> *And God went up* <note>original: "Vajaal"</note> *from him*, that is, *And the Lord ascended from Abraham.* But that which is said, *And Moses went up* <note>original: "alah"</note> *to God*, belongs to the <margin>Exodus 19:3</margin> third meaning (namely, the Ascent of the Intellect and of contemplation). This is joined with the fact that he also physically ascended to the summit of the mountain, upon which the created Light or glory descended <note>Maimonides often refers to the "Shekhinah" or "Glory" as a "created light" to avoid the idea that God's actual essence occupies a physical space.</note>. In other words, it must be understood both as the physical ascent of Moses onto the mountain where the divine Majesty was visible, and as the Ascent of his own Intellect. It should by no means be understood as if the Blessed God Himself possessed a physical place into which one could ascend, or from which He Himself could descend, as the foolish and ignorant suppose.

### Chapter XI.

-> In which the word *Yashav* [ישב] is explained <-
-> To Sit. <-

<margin>What "To Sit" (*Yashav*) signifies.</margin> First of all, this word is used in our language <note>Referring to Hebrew, the original language of the Bible.</note> for the *Sitting* of a man in a certain place: as in, <margin>1 Samuel 1:9</margin> *But Eli the Priest was sitting* <note>original: "Joschebh"</note> *upon a throne.* And because a man who sits is in the most perfect state of rest and tranquility he can have, this word is therefore applied to any thing that remains tranquil, quiet, and does not change. Hence, when it is promised to Israel that they shall remain in the highest degree firm, quiet, and stable, it is said, <margin>Zechariah 14:11</margin> *And it shall be exalted, and shall sit* <note>original: "Vejaschebhah"</note> *or remain in its place.* So also it is said, <margin>Psalm 113:9</margin> *He who makes to dwell...* <note>original: "Moschibhi"</note>

<summary>This page explains that biblical terms like "descending" often refer to God's judgment or the manifestation of His glory rather than physical movement, and begins explaining "sitting" as a metaphor for divine stability and permanence.</summary>
<keywords>descending, ascending, intellect, divine judgment, Mount Sinai, Moses, sitting, tranquility, anthropomorphism, Hebrew grammar</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues Maimonides' linguistic analysis of divine attributes, specifically how the Hebrew verb "Jaschabh" (to sit) is applied to God, following the previous discussion of "Jarad" (to descend) and "Alah" (to ascend). These chapters aim to strip away physical or bodily interpretations of God’s actions.</meta>

# GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

*He who makes the barren woman to dwell in a family, so that she is a joyful mother of children.* <note>Psalm 113:9. The Latin text uses "facit sedere," to make sit, but interprets it as "to dwell."</note> The sense is: He who causes her to stay and persist. And it is in this latter sense that the word is used of God; as in, *You, O Lord, remain [teschebh] forever.* <margin>In what sense it is said of God. Psalm 102:12</margin> Likewise, *The one who sits [Hajoschebhi] in the heavens.* <margin>Psalm 123:1</margin> Likewise, *Sitting [Joschebh] in the heavens,* <margin>Psalm 2:4</margin> that is, He who remains for eternity, who is changed in no way at all. He does not change by a change of Substance, because He has nothing outside of His substance into which He could change. Nor is His <term>Attribution</term> <note>Attribution refers to how we describe God's relationship to the world through His actions or qualities.</note> changed: because there is no proportion between Him and all other things outside of Him into which that attribution could be changed, as will be explained elsewhere. <margin>Part 1, chapter 56</margin> Therefore, because He is entirely devoid of all change, He is most perfect, as He Himself expressly testifies: <margin>Malachi 3:6</margin> *I the Lord do not change.* For in Him there is no change of anything. And it is in this sense that the word *Jaschabh* <note>original Hebrew: ישב, meaning to sit, dwell, or remain.</note> is used when speaking of the Blessed God.

<margin>
In what sense the word *Jaschabh* (to sit/remain) is said of the Heavens.
</margin>

This word is also attributed in many places to the Heavens, because they too are not subject to change and alteration; that is, their individual and singular parts do not change, just as the individual things of the Earth change, which are subject to <term>generation and corruption</term>. <note>In medieval science, "generation" is the coming-into-being of a thing and "corruption" is its passing away or decay. The heavens were thought to be eternal and unchanging compared to the earth.</note>

Thus, when God—Blessed be He—is related (which is an <term>equivocal attribution</term> <note>An "equivocal" term is one word that has multiple, distinct meanings. Here, "sitting" means something different for God than it does for a human.</note>) to the species of beings subject to generation and corruption, He is also said to *Sit*; because those species do not perish, but always remain, just like the individual parts of the heavens. Thus it is said, <margin>Isaiah 40:22</margin> *He who sits in the circle of the earth,* that is, He who perpetually preserves things subject to change upon the globe of the earth—that is, within its hollow—which are contained in that hollow. So it is said, <margin>Psalm 29:10</margin> *The Lord sits at the flood [or confusion].* <note>The Latin *confusio* translates the Hebrew *mabbul* (flood). Maimonides interprets this as God's stability even amidst the chaos of the changing world.</note> The sense is: although earthly things change and decay, there is no change in God; because that attribution, by which God is related to those things that are born and decay, is a firm and stable attribution, referring to the **species**, not to individuals. <note>While individual animals die, the "species" or type of animal continues; Maimonides attributes this stability to God "sitting" or remaining constant.</note>

Consider these things and you will find that the word *Jaschabh* is always used in this way when it is spoken of the Blessed God.

### CHAPTER XII.
### ->In which the word *Kum* [קום] is explained<-
### ->*To Stand, To Rise.*<-

<margin>
What *Kum* (to stand/rise) signifies and in what sense it is said of God.
Esther 5:9.
</margin>

*KUM* is an equivocal term, one meaning of which is *to Stand* or *to Rise*, the opposite of *to Sit* <note>original Greek: τῷ Jaschabh Sedere</note>: as in, *And he did not rise [Kam], nor did he move for him.*

<meta>catchword: Furthermore (Præterea)</meta>

<summary>Maimonides explains that the Hebrew verb "Jaschabh" (to sit) describes God's absolute immutability and the permanence of universal species, rather than physical posture. He then begins an analysis of the verb "Kum" (to stand/rise) as another potentially misleading term for divine action.</summary>

Part I. Chapter XII.
<sig>C</sig>

Furthermore, it also signifies Firmness and Truth—that is, the true existence of a thing. For example: *And the Lord established [Vajakem] his word.* <note>1 Kings 8:20</note> Likewise: *And the field of Ephron stood [Vajakom], and was confirmed.* <note>Genesis 23:17</note> Likewise: *And the house that is in the city shall stand [Vekam] (shall remain).* <note>Leviticus 25:30</note> Likewise: *And the Kingdom of Israel shall stand [Vekam] in your hand.* <note>1 Samuel 24:20</note> In this sense, the word *Rise* <note>Hebrew: *Kum*</note> is always used when it occurs regarding God, the Best and Greatest. For example: *Now I will arise [akum], says the Lord*—that is, now I will confirm and establish my word, my promises, and my warnings (translated word-for-word as: my promises for good and for evil). <note>Psalm 12:6</note> Thus: *You shall arise [Takum] and have mercy on Zion*—that is, you will fulfill what you promised her: namely, that you would have mercy on her. <note>Psalm 102:13</note>

<margin>
1 Kings 8:20.
Genesis 23:17.
Leviticus 25:30.
1 Samuel 24:20.
Psalm 12:5.
Psalm 102:13.
</margin>

Moreover, since someone who has decided and decreed that it is necessary to act or do something prepares himself for the task by "rising up," it is therefore said of anyone who undertakes a matter—whatever it may be—that he "rises." For example: *That my son has made my servant rise [suscitavit] against me.* <note>1 Samuel 22:8</note> And in this sense, it is also applied to the Decree of God regarding the destruction of a nation that has deserved punishment; as in: *And I will rise [Vekamti] against the house of Jeroboam.* <note>Amos 7:9</note> Likewise: *And he will rise against the house of the wicked.* <note>Isaiah 31:2</note> It is possible that the previously cited passages should also be understood this way: *Now I will rise,* and *You shall arise and have mercy on Zion*—as if to say: "You will rise against her enemies."

<margin>
1 Samuel 22:8.
Amos 7:9.
Isaiah 31:2.
Psalm 12:5.
Psalm 102:13.
</margin>

And in this sense, many places occur; not truly, as if God were actually standing or sitting. Heaven forbid! Hence our <term>Rabbis</term> <note>Jewish sages and scholars of the law.</note> of blessed memory say: *On high (in heaven) there is neither sitting [Jeschibhah] nor standing [Amidah].* For the word *Stood* <note>Hebrew: *Amad*</note> is very often used in place of *Rose* <note>Hebrew: *Kam*</note>.

### Chapter XIII.
### ->In which the word *Amad* is explained<-
### ->[Stood]<-

<margin>
The word *Amad* [Stood].
Genesis 41:46.
Jeremiah 15:1.
Genesis 18:8.
Job 32:16.
Genesis 29:35.
</margin>

The word *Amad* is <term>equivocal</term> <note>A term that can have several different meanings depending on context.</note>. It signifies, first, the actual standing of a person: as in, *When he stood [Beomdo] before Pharaoh.* Likewise: *If Moses and Samuel should stand [jaamod] before me.* Likewise: *And he himself was standing [omed] beside them.*

Next, it is used for the *Cessation* of some action; as in, *And they stood still [Veamedu], and answered no more.* Likewise: *And she stood [Vattaamod] (ceased) from bearing children.*

Furthermore, it denotes Stability and Permanence, or Duration: as in, *That they may stand [Vejaamdu] (last) for many days.* Likewise: *You will be able to stand [amod] (to endure).* Likewise: *Its taste stood [Amad] in it*—that is, it remained, it persisted, and was not changed.

Jeremiah 32:14.
Exodus 18:23.
Jeremiah 48:11.
</margin>

<summary>This page analyzes the Hebrew verbs "Kum" (to rise) and "Amad" (to stand), arguing that when applied to God, they signify the fulfillment of a divine decree or the stability of His existence rather than a physical posture. It emphasizes the Rabbinic principle that physical positions like sitting or standing do not apply to the incorporeal Divine.</summary>

<margin>Psalm 111:3</margin>
Also: *His justice stands [omedet] for eternity*—that is, it is stable, it remains, and it endures.

<margin>In what sense "standing" is said of God.</margin>
And this latter sense of the word *Amad* [to stand] is always to be understood whenever it occurs in reference to God. For example: *And his feet shall stand [Ve-amedu] upon the Mount of Olives* <note>Zechariah 14:4</note>—that is, his causes will be established (meaning the effects or results he brings about), just as I am

### CHAPTER XIV.
### Explanation of the word אדם Adam (Man).

<margin>Concerning the word Adam (Man).</margin>
ADAM is an equivocal term <term>equivocal: a word with multiple distinct meanings</term>. First, it is the proper name of the first human being and, as Scripture teaches, it is derived from the word *Adamah* [Earth].

<margin>Genesis 6:3; Ecclesiastes 3:21; Ibid. verse 19.</margin>
Secondly, it is a general name for the whole species. For example: *My spirit shall not contend with man [ba-adam].* Also: *Who knows the spirit of the sons of Adam [men]?* Also: *And the excellence of man [ha-adam] over the beasts is nothing.*

Thirdly, in a more specific sense, it signifies the common folk and the multitude of people—that is, the ordinary people <note>original Latin: *idiotas*, referring to private citizens or the unlearned</note>, rather than distinguished individuals who are, as it were, set apart from the common lot of mortals. For example: 
<margin>Psalm 49:3; Genesis 6:3; Psalm 82:7.</margin>
*Both the sons of Adam [common men] and the sons of a man [high-born men].* And in this third sense it is said: *And the sons of God saw the daughters of men [ha-adam].* Also: *But like men [ke-adam], you shall die.*

### CHAPTER XV.
### The terms נצב Nizzabh and יצב Jazabh (To Stand) are explained.

<margin>Nizzabh and Jazabh are explained.</margin>
NIZZABH and *Jazabh*, although they are from different roots, have one and the same meaning, as you will recognize from their general usage. They are, however, equivocal terms.

First, they signify nothing other than *Kum* [to stand]. For example: *And 
<margin>Exodus 2:4; Psalm 2:2; Numbers 16:27.</margin>
his sister stood [va-tetazzebh] from a distance.* Also: *The kings of the earth set themselves [yityazzebu].* Also: *They came out and stood [nizzabhim].*

Secondly, they also signify Stability, Firmness, and Perpetuity. 
<margin>Psalm 119:89; Genesis 28:13.</margin>
For example: *Your word stands [nizzabh] in heaven*—that is, it remains stable and firm. They are used in this way whenever they occur in reference to God. For example: *And behold...*

<summary>Maimonides explains that verbs like 'stand' refer to divine stability rather than physical posture, and clarifies that 'Adam' can refer to the first man, the human species, or the common people.</summary>
<keywords>Amad, Adam, Nizzabh, Jazabh, biblical Hebrew, etymology, divine attributes, stability, human species, equivocal terms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Hebrew terms applied to God. It transitions from discussing "standing" (nizzabh and amad) in Chapter 15 to the various meanings of "rock" (Tzur) in Chapter 16, reinforcing the argument that these words describe God's stability and role as a First Cause rather than physical attributes.</meta>

PART I. CHAPTER XV.
C 2
<meta>catchword: Blessed</meta>

The Lord *nizzabh* [stood] over it—that is, he remains firm and stable over it, namely, the ladder, one end of which is in heaven and the other on earth. By this ladder, anyone who desires to ascend must do so in order to reach him who stands at its top, since he persists firmly at the highest point of the ladder. For what I am saying is clear: those words, *Behold, the Lord stood over it*, must be understood according to this parable or metaphor, as seen from what follows: *And the Angels of God were ascending*. By these <margin>Prophets are called Angels.</margin> "angels," nothing else is meant but the Prophets, as is expressly said of them: *And he sent an Angel* <note>Numbers 20:16; usually interpreted as a messenger or prophet like Moses</note>. Likewise: *And an Angel of the Lord came up from Gilgal to Bochim* <note>Judges 2:1</note>. How appropriate it is that he says *They were ascending and descending*, placing the ascent before the descent! For after the Ascent and the attainment of the degrees of knowledge (namely, of Divine things) on that ladder, there follows the Descent to civil matters, for the purpose of governing and teaching mankind. We have already taught in Chapter 10 that this is what the term "Descent" signifies. But I return to our purpose: that *He stood above it* signifies nothing other than that he is stable and firm; it does not indicate any physical posture. This is also the sense of: *And you shall stand [Venizzabhta] upon the rock*. And observe here that *Nizzabh* and *Amad* <margin>Exodus 33:21.</margin> denote the same thing. For in the same way, it is read elsewhere: *Behold, I will stand [omed] before you there upon the rock in Horeb*. <margin>Exodus 17:6.</margin>

### CHAPTER XVI.
### Explanation of the word צור [Tzur]: Rock, Cliff.

<margin>Tzur: Rock, Cliff. Exodus 17:6.</margin> THE NAME *Tzur* is equivocal <term>equivocal: a word with multiple meanings</term>. First, it is the name of a Mountain: as in, *And you shall strike the Tzur [rock]* <note>Exodus 17:6</note>.
<margin>Joshua 5:2.</margin> Secondly, it is also the name of a certain hard stone: as in, *Knives of tzurim [flint]* <note>Joshua 5:2</note>, meaning stones or made of stone.
Thirdly, it signifies an Origin, a Fountainhead, or a Vein from which stones are quarried; as in, *Look to the tzur [rock] from which you were hewn*. From this, it is trans- <margin>Isaiah 51:1.</margin> ferred to the origin and principle of any thing. Therefore, after the Prophet says, *Look to the tzur*, he adds: *Look to Abraham your father, etc.* It is as if he had said: Since that fountainhead or vein from which you were quarried is Abraham your father, walk therefore in his ways, imitate his character and virtues, and follow in his footsteps. For it is fitting that the nature of the vein and fountainhead should exist in the thing that was cut from it. And according to this sense, the <margin>Why God is called Tzur [Rock].</margin> Blessed God is called *Tzur*; because he is the principle and efficient cause of all things...

<summary>This page explains that God "standing" on Jacob's ladder or a rock signifies His eternal stability, and interprets "Angels" as prophets who must gain divine knowledge before teaching. It also introduces the word Tzur (Rock), explaining its transition from a physical stone to a metaphor for God as the Origin of all existence.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob's ladder, nizzabh, standing, stability, prophets, angels, Tzur, Rock, Abraham, First Cause, etymology</keywords>

GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

<margin>
Deuteronomy 32:4.
Deuteronomy 32:18.
Deuteronomy 32:30.
1 Samuel 2:2.
Isaiah 26:4.
Exodus 33:21.
See Chapter 8 above.
</margin>
of things outside of Himself; as in: *The Rock <note>original: "Hazzur"</note> of the Source—that is, God—His work is perfect*. Also: *The Rock of the Source who begot you, you have forgotten*. Likewise: *Their Source—God—sold them because of their Rock*. Again: *There is no Rock like our God*. Also: *The Source is the Rock of ages*. And: *And you shall stand upon the Rock*. Pause here, and consider carefully what has been said: that God is the first principle of all things. For this is the gate through which one arrives at Him—that is, at God or the knowledge of Him—just as we indicated in the explanation of that passage: *Behold, there is a place by me*.

### CHAPTER XVII.

### *Why the Prophets spoke obscurely and, as it were, through riddles, regarding both the Work of Creation and the Work of the Chariot—that is, concerning natural and spiritual wisdom.*

<margin>
Divine matters must be spoken of covertly.
</margin>
Do not think that Divine Wisdom must be hidden only from the common people and the masses. The same judgment applies to natural wisdom <note>Maimonides equates "The Work of Creation" (Ma'aseh Bereshit) with Physics or natural science</note>, as we showed you in the Preface of this book, based on that well-known saying: *And regarding the Work of Creation, one does not expound even to two people* <note>A reference to the Mishnah, Hagigah 2:1, which limits the teaching of esoteric subjects to avoid confusion or heresy</note>.

<margin>
Philosophers and Gentiles taught their principles covertly and enigmatically.
</margin>
And this practice is not found only among our Doctors of the Law; it was also observed long ago by the Philosophers and Wise Men among the Gentiles, who hid their first principles and spoke of them through riddles. Thus Plato and others before him called Matter the "Female" and Form the "Male." For you know that there are three principles of things that are born and decay: Matter, Form, and Privation <note>term: Privation <gloss>The absence or lack of a specific Form in Matter, which allows for the possibility of change</gloss></note>, which is perpetually joined to Matter. For unless Privation accompanied Matter in this way, Matter itself could not receive a new Form. In this respect, Privation is also counted among the principles. However, when a Form arrives, then that specific privation ceases; I mean the privation of that Form which has arrived, and another privation is then introduced, and so on to infinity, as is explained in Physics.

If, therefore, these philosophers used metaphorical words and similarities in teaching such matters—the publication of which brings no harm or danger—how much more fitting is it that we, who are Teachers of the Divine Law, do not openly reveal those things which are too difficult for the common understanding. If we did, the people might imagine something else—indeed, the exact opposite of what we intend.

Take careful note of this.
