PART I. CHAPTER XVIII.
<sig>C 3</sig>

### Chapter XVIII.

->In which three words are explained: *Karabh* (To approach), *Nagasch* (To draw near), and *Nagah* (To touch or attain).<-

<margin>The words *Karabh*, *Nagasch*, and *Nagah*, meaning to draw near or approach—what they signify and how they are said of God.</margin>
These three words, *Karabh*, *Nagasch*, and *Nagah*, are used primarily to describe something that is close or approaching in terms of physical space. Secondarily, they are used to describe the union between Knowledge and the thing being known.

When one body is seen to approach another, the word *Karabh* <note>original: "קרב"</note> is primarily used to express this; as in, *When he had approached <note>original: "karabh"</note> the camp*. Likewise, *And Pharaoh caused [the camp] to approach <note>original: "hikribh"; this is the causative form of the verb</note>*. Next, *Nagah* <note>original: "נגע"</note> is similarly used for the physical union of one body with another: as in, *And she touched <note>original: "Vattaggah"</note> his feet*. Also, *And it touched <note>original: "Vajaggah"</note> my mouth*. Finally, *Nagasch* <note>original: "נגש"</note> is used in the same way for local movement, as when one man approaches and moves toward another: as in, *And Judah approached <note>original: "Vajiggasch"</note> him*.

<margin>
Exodus 32:19.
Exodus 14:10.
Exodus 4:25.
Jeremiah 1:9.
Genesis 44:18.
</margin>

The second meaning of these words refers to the connection of the mind and the "contact" of intellectual apprehension, rather than physical location. The verb *Nagah* is used in this sense when it is said: *Because its judgment has reached <note>original: "nagah"</note> to the heavens*. Likewise *Karabh* is used where it is written: *The matter that is too difficult for you, you shall bring near <note>original: "takribhun"</note> to me*, which means, "you shall make it known to me." In the same way, the word *Nagasch* is found used as follows: *And Abraham approached <note>original: "Vajiggasch"</note> and said*. For this occurred in a prophetic vision and a prophetic dream, as will be explained elsewhere. Also: *Because this people draws near <note>original: "nagasch"</note> with its mouth and with its lips*.

These words, *Karabh* and *Nagasch*, are to be understood in this way whenever they are found in the books of the Prophets concerning God and His creatures, whatever they may be (<insert>that is, whether the creatures are said to approach and draw near to God, or He to them</insert>). For God—Blessed be He—is not a body (as is demonstrated extensively in this book), and He does not approach or draw near to any thing; nor can any other thing approach Him (<insert>in a physical sense</insert>). This is because when corporeality is removed, physical Place and Location are also removed, as are all forms of approaching, distancing, contact, union, separation, vicinity, and so on.

I do not think you have any doubt that in those passages such as: *The Lord is near <note>original: "Karobh"</note> to all who call upon Him*; or, *They desire the approach <note>original: "Kirbhat"</note> of God*; or, *The approach <note>original: "Kirbhat"</note> of God is good for me*—I say, I do not think you have any doubt that these passages...

<margin>
Jeremiah 51:9.
Deuteronomy 1:17.
Genesis 18:23.
Isaiah 29:13.
Psalm 145:18.
Isaiah 58:2.
Psalm 73:28.
</margin>

<vocab>Approach, Draw Near, Touch, Proximity, Intellect, Prophecy, Corporeality, Place</vocab>

<summary>This page analyzes the Hebrew verbs for 'approaching' and 'touching,' arguing that when applied to God, they signify intellectual or spiritual connection rather than physical movement or proximity. Maimonides emphasizes that because God is incorporeal, He exists outside of space and cannot 'move' in a literal sense.</summary>

...let all these things be understood as the **Approaching of the mind**, that is, as **mental Apprehension**, and not as movement in space. Thus it is also said, *Kerobhim*
<margin>
Joshua 9:16.
Deuteronomy 5:27.
Exodus 24:2.
</margin>
*They are near*. <note>original: "קרובים" (kerobhim)</note> Likewise, *Kerabh: Approach, you, and hear*. <note>original: "קרב" (kerabh)</note> Likewise, *Veniggasch: And Moses alone shall approach, but they shall not approach*. <note>original: "ונגש" (veniggasch)</note> But if it pleases you to take that which was said of Moses—*And he shall approach*, etc.—as referring to a physical Approaching to the mountain upon which the **Light** or **Glory of God** <note>This refers to the *Shekhinah* or the created light that represents the Divine Presence in Maimonides' philosophy</note> then dwelled, you are free to do so: provided that you hold fast to this fundamental principle: that whether a man be at the center of the earth or at the summit of the **ninth sphere** <note>In medieval cosmology, the ninth sphere was the outermost moving sphere of the universe, representing the furthest possible physical distance from the center</note> (if such a thing were possible), he would not be any further from God in the former place, nor any closer to Him in the latter. Rather, **Approaching God** consists in the **Apprehension** of Him, while **Distance** consists in being ignorant of Him or failing to understand Him. There are many degrees of this Approaching and Distance: one person is found in one degree, another in another. But what the excellence and preeminence of this Apprehension is, and what its distinctions are, will be explained in another 
<margin>
Psalm 144:5.
Job 2:5.
</margin>
chapter of this book. As for that which is said, *Touch the mountains and they shall smoke*, it is **Metaphorical**, and means nothing other than: "Make Your Word touch them," or "Make Your Word reach them," just as it is said in Job, *Touch his very self*, that is: "Bring Your plague upon him."

In this way, therefore, the word *Nagah* <note>original: "נגע" (to touch or reach)</note> in this Language must be wisely discerned according to its sense in every place. For sometimes it intends **physical Touch**; at other times it intends the **Contact and Union of the mind**, and the apprehension of some thing—just as if he who apprehends something which he had not apprehended before has "approached" it, having previously been "far" from it. Observe this well.

->In which is explained what *Male* signifies<-

<margin>
*Plenus* (Full), what it signifies, and how it is said of God.
Genesis 24:16.

Genesis 25:24.
Genesis 50:3.
</margin>
**MALE** <note>original: "מלא"</note> is an **equivocal term** <note>A term that has multiple, distinct meanings depending on context</note> which is used first of a body that enters into another body and fills it: as in, *And she filled her jar*. Likewise, *A full omer*. Likewise, *An omer for each*. And so very often.

Secondly, it is used for the **Completion and Perfection** of a certain and defined time: as in, *And her days were fulfilled <note>or "completed"</note> that she should give birth*. Likewise, *And forty days were fulfilled for him*.

Thirdly, it signifies a thing which is perfect and absolute in all its parts...
solu- <note>Catchword for "absolutum" (absolute/complete)</note>

# Part I. Chapter XIX.

<margin>
Deuteronomy 33:23.

...fulfilled: as in, *And full with the blessing of the Lord*. <note>original: "Umale Et plene benedictione Domini"</note> Likewise, *And he filled them with wisdom of heart*. <note>original: "Umille Et implevit eos sapientiâ cordis"</note> Likewise, *And he was filled with wisdom and understanding and knowledge*. <note>original: "Vajimmale Et impletus erat sapientiâ & intelligentiâ & scientiâ"</note> And it is in this sense that we find, *The whole earth is full of his glory*. <note>original: "Melo Plena est tota terra gloriâ ejus"</note> For the meaning is: the whole earth bears witness to His perfection and to Him (so that His glory and praise may be perfect in this earth).

<margin>Exodus 40:34.</margin>
Likewise, *And the glory of the Lord filled the tabernacle*. <note>original: "Et gloria Domini mille replevit tabernaculum"</note> And so the word <term>Male <note>original: "מלא" (male)</note></term>, meaning "Full," is always to be understood in this way when attributed to God: not indeed as if God had a body with which He might fill some physical place. But if you prefer to understand the "Glory of the Lord" as the **Created Light** <note>This refers to the *Shekhinah*, a visible manifestation of divine presence used to avoid attributing a physical body to God</note> (that is, some visible sign of God’s Majesty) which filled the tabernacle, there will be nothing absurd in that.

### Chapter XX.

#### In which is explained the meaning of <term>Ram <note>original: "רם" (ram)</note></term> (High or Exalted) and <term>Nasa <note>original: "נשא" (nasa)</note></term> (Lifted).

<margin>Two words, <term>Ram</term> and <term>Nasa</term>, are explained, and how they are used in relation to God.</margin>
*Ram* is an equivocal term <note>An "equivocal term" is a word that has two or more entirely different meanings. Maimonides uses this to distinguish between physical height and metaphorical status</note>, signifying height of place, and height of degree—that is, of dignity, honor, and glory.

From the first meaning comes what is said: *And the ark was lifted up from the earth*. <note>original: "Vattarom Et exaltata est arca à terra"</note>

<margin>
Genesis 7:17.
Psalms 89:20.
1 Kings 16:2.
1 Kings 14:7.
</margin>
From the second meaning are: *I have exalted one chosen from the people*. <note>original: "Harimoti Exaltavi electum è populo"</note> Likewise, *Because I exalted you from the dust*. <note>original: "Propterea quòd harimoticha exaltavi te è pulvere"</note> Likewise, *Because I exalted you from among the people*. <note>original: "Propterea quòd harimoricha exaltavi te de medio populi"</note> And this word *Ram* must always be referred to this second meaning when it occurs regarding the Creator: such as, *Be exalted, O God, above the heavens*. <note>original: "Rumah Exaltare supra cælos Deus"</note>

<margin>Psalms 57:6, 12.</margin>
Similarly, the word *Nasah* signifies height of place, and height of degree, or Excellence and Eminence.

<margin>Genesis 42:26.</margin>
From the first meaning is: *And they lifted their grain onto their donkeys*. <note>original: "Vajissu Et exaltârunt, elevârunt frumentum suum super asinos suos"</note> And many instances are found in this sense of physical lifting and moving from place to place.

<margin>
Numbers 24:7.
Isaiah 63:9.
Numbers 16:3.
</margin>
From the second meaning: *And his kingdom shall be exalted*. <note>original: "Vetinnaffe Et exaltabitur regnum ejus"</note> Likewise, *And he carried them and exalted them*. <note>original: "Et portavit eos, vajenaffem & exaltavit eos"</note> Likewise, *Why do you lift yourselves up?* <note>original: "Quare tithnaffeu effertis vos?"</note> The word *Nasah* should always be understood this way when attributed to the Creator: as in, *Lift yourself up, judge of the earth*. <note>original: "Hinnafe Exaltato te judex terræ"</note> Likewise, *Thus says the High and Exalted One* <note>original: "Sic dicit excelsus veniffa & altus"</note>—namely, in terms of the height and magnificence of degree or excellence, not of place.

<margin>
Psalms 94:2.
Isaiah 57:15.
</margin>
But perhaps someone will raise an objection here based on what I say regarding "Magnificence" and "Height" of degree and excellence: asking, how can I attribute so many things to a single and most simple Being (God)?

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 20, which discusses the Unity of God’s attributes, and begins Chapter 21, analyzing the multiple meanings of the Hebrew verb "Abhar" (to pass). The catchword "Deus" (God) links to the theological conclusion that all divine attributes refer back to God's singular essence.</meta>

But as far as this matter is concerned, it will be explained to you elsewhere that among those who perfectly apprehend God, He is not called by many names and attributes. All those denominations or attributes which speak of His *Power, Magnificence, Strength, Perfection, Goodness*, and so on, return to and refer to one single thing: namely, His *Essence*. They do not refer to anything outside of it <note>original: "non ad aliquid extra eam"</note> (that is to say, all those descriptions are nothing other than the very essence of God itself). We shall deal with the Names or Attributes of God in other chapters. Our purpose in this chapter was only to demonstrate that these two words, <term>Ram <note>original: "רם" (High)</note></term> and <term>Nissa <note>original: "נשא" (Exalted)</note></term>, are not to be understood as referring to height of physical place, but rather to excellence and dignity when they are attributed to God.

### CHAPTER XXI.
### In which is explained the word <term>Abhar <note>original: "עבר" (Abhar)</note></term>, meaning "To Pass."

<margin>The word <term>Abhar</term>, "To Pass."</margin>
<margin>Genesis 33:3. Exodus 17:5.</margin>
This word *Abhar* signifies, in its first sense, the *local transition of a body*. It properly and principally refers to the movement of living creatures along a straight and level path. For example: *And he himself passed over before them* <note>original: "Et ipse abhar transivit ante eos"</note>. Or, *Pass on before this people* <note>original: "Abhor Transi ante populum hunc"</note>.

<margin>Exodus 36:6. 1 Samuel 2:24.</margin>
Secondly, it is adapted to describe the *path or carrying of a voice through the air*. For example: *And they caused a voice to pass through the camp* <note>original: "Vajaabhiru Et transire fecerunt vocem per castra," referring to a proclamation being made</note>. Likewise: *It is no good report that I hear, which the Lord’s people cause to pass* <note>original: "quam maabhirim transire faciunt," meaning a rumor spreading among the people</note>.

<margin>Genesis 15:17.</margin>
Thirdly, it is said of the *arrival of splendor and visible divine Majesty*, which the Prophets saw in prophetic visions. For example: *And behold, a smoking furnace and a burning lamp that passed between those pieces* <note>original: "quæ abhar transibat inter divisiones ipsas"</note>. This indeed happened in a prophetic vision, as is gathered from the beginning of that history where it is said, *And a deep sleep fell upon Abraham* <note>original: "Et sopor irruit super Abraham"</note>. It is also used in this sense when it is said: *And I will pass through the land of Egypt* <note>original: "Veabharti Et transibo terram Aegypti," referring to the night of the Passover</note>. There are other similar instances.
<margin>Genesis 15:12. Exodus 12:12.</margin>

<margin>Jeremiah 23:9.</margin>
*Fourthly*, the term is transferred to one who performs an action and exceeds the proper limit or transgresses boundaries. For example: *Like a man whom wine has passed over* <note>original: "Sicut vir abharo qui transivit vinum," meaning one who has exceeded the limit in drinking and is overcome by wine</note>.

*Fifthly*, it is said of one who pretends to have one intention but purposefully deviates from it toward another he has set for himself. (Or, *of one who leads another away from their intention, causes them to stray, or makes them pay attention to something else.*) For example: *But he shot arrows as if to make them pass him* <note>original: "ut transire faceret eum," referring to Jonathan's signal to David</note>. According to this metaphorical sense, it is said in my judgment: *And the Lord passed over his face* <note>original: "Vajaabhor Et transivit Dominus super faciem suam"</note>, (or, *And he caused to pass*...
<margin>1 Samuel 20:36. Exodus 34:6.</margin>

<summary>This page concludes the argument that divine attributes like "High" refer to God's essence rather than physical height, and begins a linguistic analysis of the word "Abhar" (to pass), categorizing its physical, auditory, and metaphorical meanings in Scripture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 21 regarding the word "Abhar" (to pass). The previous page established the literal meaning of physical movement; this page applies the term to the specific biblical event where God "passes" before Moses, navigating the theological challenge of describing a non-physical God in human language.</meta>

### PART I. CHAPTER XXI.
D

...God [caused to pass] over his [Moses'] face): so that the suffix *vav* <note>the grammatical "his" ending</note> in <term>Panav <note>original Hebrew: פָּנָיו, "His face"</note></term> refers to God, the Blessed One. For in this way our Rabbis of pious memory also wrote that *Panav* refers to God, the Blessed One, as can be seen in the *Haggadoth* <note>traditional Rabbinic stories or homilies that explain the Bible</note>. But this is not the place to cite those; it is enough for us that our opinion is strengthened by them in some way.

The explanation of that passage, then, seems to me to be this: Moses (of pious memory) requested from God that <term>Apprehension <note>intellectual grasp or direct perception</note></term> which God calls the "Vision of the Face" when He says: *And my face shall not be seen*. But God, having passed over that request, <margin>Exodus 33:23</margin> promised him another kind of Apprehension, which He labels the "Vision of the back," when He says: *And you shall see my back*.

We have also already written about this very matter <margin>Ibid.</margin> in our *Deuteronomy* <note>referring to the *Mishneh Torah*, Maimonides' great code of law</note> (that is, in the book <term>Jad <note>literally "Hand," representing the 14 volumes of Maimonides' legal code</note></term>) and stated <margin>part 1. c. 38.</margin> that God, the Best and Greatest, hid from Moses that Apprehension called the "Vision of the Face," and caused him to pass on to another: namely, the knowledge of the works or actions attributed to Him. These are those Names or Attributes of God (*those thirteen properties of God, according to the Hebrew counting, which God ascribes to Himself in that place*).

When I said that God "hid" one form of Apprehension from him, I want it to be understood in this way: that such a grasp is impossible and imperceptible by its very nature. Every perfected man, when he busies himself trying to grasp something more sublime than what his intellect is naturally capable of catching, becomes confused in his perception or certainly dies (as we shall explain in another chapter of this book), <margin>part 1. c. 32.</margin> unless special help from God is present, just as it is said of Moses: *And I will <margin>Exodus 33:22</margin> protect you with my hand until I have passed, or have caused to pass* (meaning, I have caused you to pass away from the intention you had, which far exceeded your capacity).

The <term>Targum <note>the ancient Aramaic translation and commentary of the Hebrew Bible</note></term> treats these expressions in its usual manner. For whenever anything occurs that seems to ascribe and attribute <term>corporeality <note>having a physical body</note></term> to God, <margin>*How the Targum is accustomed to remove corporeality from God.*</margin> it explains it through the omission of a related term and attributes it to some other thing that refers to God, but falls short of His essence. For example: *And behold, the Lord stood above it*. The Targum explains: *The Glory of the Lord <margin>Genesis 28:13</margin> stood above it*. Likewise: *May the Lord watch between me and you*. The <margin>Gen. 31:49</margin> Targum translates: *May the Word of the Lord watch*.

This is the constant habit of that Interpreter <note>referring to Onkelos</note> of pious memory. Hence, in the present passage, where it is said, *And the Lord passed over His face*, he himself has: *And the Lord caused His Majesty to pass before him*. According to him, therefore, the thing that passed was undoubtedly something created, and the pronominal suffix *vav* in *Panav* must refer to Moses, so that "over his face" is the same as "before him," just as in the verse: *And the gift passed over before him*. And <margin>Gen. 32:21</margin> this exposition of <term>Onkelos <note>the traditional author of the Aramaic Targum of the Torah</note></term> is also elegant and good, which the Holy Scripture itself seems to confirm when it says: *And it shall be, while [my glory] passes by* <margin>Exodus 33:22</margin>

<summary>Maimonides explains that God's "passing" before Moses refers to the human limit of understanding God's essence; we can only know God's "back" (His actions and attributes) rather than His "face" (His true nature).</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Divine Attributes, Corporeality, Onkelos, Targum, Vision of the Face, Vision of the Back, Apprehension, Glory of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine names "Ram" and "Nissa" (High and Exalted) from page 23, concluding that these refer to God’s excellence rather than physical height. It then transitions into Chapter 21, which begins an analysis of the Hebrew verb "Abhar" (to pass).</meta>

<header>GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED</header>

But as far as this matter is concerned, it will be explained to you elsewhere that among those who understand God perfectly, He is not called by many names and attributes. All those denominations or attributes which teach of His Power, Magnificence, Fortitude, Perfection, Goodness, and so on, return and refer to one single thing: namely, to His very Essence, and not to anything outside of it <note>This reflects Maimonides' core belief in "Divine Simplicity"—the idea that God is not composed of different parts or traits, but is a single, unified essence.</note> (*that is, that all those things are nothing other than the essence of God itself*). We shall deal with the Names or Attributes of God in other chapters. Our purpose in this chapter was only to demonstrate that these two words *Ram* <note>Hebrew: רָם, "High"</note> and *Nissa* <note>Hebrew: נִשָּׂא, "Exalted"</note> are not to be understood as height of place, but of Excellence and dignity (*specifically when they are attributed to God*).

### CHAPTER XXI.
### *In which the word עבר Abhar (To Pass) is explained.*

<margin>The word עבר (Abhar) "To Pass."</margin>
<margin>Genesis 33:3; Exodus 17:5.</margin>
This word *Abhar* signifies, first, the *physical movement of a body from place to place*, and specifically and principally the movement of living beings in a straight and level direction: as in, *And he himself passed [abhar] before them.* Likewise, *Pass [Abhor] before this people.*

<margin>Exodus 36:6; 1 Samuel 2:24.</margin>
Secondly, it is also adapted to the *passage or carrying of a voice through the air*: as in, *And they caused a voice to pass [Vajaabhiru] through the camp.* Likewise, *It is no good report that I hear, which the Lord's people cause to pass [maabhirim] <note>Meaning a rumor or report spreading among the people.</note>.*

<margin>Genesis 15:17.</margin>
*Thirdly*, it is said of the *arrival of a visible divine splendor and Majesty*, which the Prophets saw in a prophetic vision: as in, *And behold, a smoking furnace and a flaming torch that passed [abhar] between those pieces.* Indeed, this happened in a prophetic vision, as is gathered from the beginning of that story where it is said, *And a deep sleep fell upon Abraham.* It is in this same sense that it is said, *And I will pass [Veabharti] through the land of Egypt.* and other similar passages.
<margin>Ibid. verse 12; Exodus 12:12.</margin>

<margin>Jeremiah 23:9.</margin>
*Fourthly*, it is also transferred to one who does something and exceeds the proper limit, overstepping the boundaries: as in, *Like a man who is overcome [abhar] by wine (that is, one who has exceeded the limit in drinking it).*

*Fifthly*, it is also said of one who pretends to have one intention but purposefully deviates from it toward another which he has set for himself: (or of one who leads another away from his intention, *causes him to deviate*, and makes him pay attention to something else:) as in, *But he shot the arrows to make them pass [lehaabhiro] him.* It is according to this metaphorical sense, in my judgment, that it is said, *And the Lord passed [Vajaabhor] before his face*, (or, *And He caused...*
<margin>1 Samuel 20:36; Exodus 34:6.</margin>

<meta>The text breaks off here with the catchword "Deus" (God), which will begin the next page as part of the phrase "caused God to pass."</meta>

<summary>Maimonides argues that divine attributes describe God's single essence rather than distinct traits. He then begins a philological study of the verb "Abhar" (to pass), moving from physical movement to the "passing" of God's presence in prophetic visions.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Essence, Divine Attributes, Abhar, To Pass, Prophetic Vision, Biblical Philology, Moses, Abraham, Exodus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Hebrew verb "Abhar" (to pass), specifically investigating how it is used in the context of Moses’ encounter with God in the Book of Exodus.</meta>

Part I. Chapter XXI.

...God [passed Moses, namely] *before His face* <note>original: "super faciem suam"</note>): so that the suffix וֹ in פָּנָיו *Panav* <note>Hebrew: "His face"</note> refers to the Blessed God. For in this way our Rabbis <note>The ancient Jewish sages</note> (of blessed memory) also wrote that *Panav* refers to the Blessed God, as can be seen in the *Haggadoth* <note>The non-legal, homiletic, and storytelling traditions of the Talmud</note>. But this is not the place to cite those; it is enough for us that it lends some strength to our opinion. Therefore, the explanation of that passage will be, as it seems to me: that Moses (of blessed memory) sought from God that Perception <note>original: "Apprehensionem"</note> which God calls the *Vision of the face*, when He says; <margin>Exodus 33:23</margin> *And my face shall not be seen*: but God, having denied this, promised him another Perception, which He names the *Vision of the back* <note>original: "Visionem posteriorum"</note>, while He says, <margin>Ibid.</margin> *And you shall see my back*. Concerning this very matter <margin>Part 1, chapter 38.</margin> we have already written in our *Deuterosis* <note>literally "Repetition," referring to Maimonides' great legal code, the Mishneh Torah</note> (that is, in the book *Yad*), and we said that God, the Best and Greatest, hid from Moses that Perception which is called the *Vision of the face*, and caused him to pass to another: namely, the knowledge of the works or actions attributed to Him, which are those Names or Attributes of God (those thirteen properties of God, according to the calculation of the Hebrews, which God ascribes to Himself in that place).

But what I said—*that God hid from him one Perception*—I wish to be understood in this way: that such Perception is by its nature impossible and imperceptible. Every perfected man, when he busies himself to perceive something more sublime than what his intellect can naturally grasp, becomes confounded in his perception, or indeed dies (as we shall explain in another chapter of this book), <margin>Part 1, chapter 32.</margin> unless a special help from God is present for him, as it is said of Moses, <margin>Exodus 33:22</margin> *And I will protect you with my hand until I have passed* (that is, protecting you from the intent you had, which far exceeded your capacity).

The *Targum* <note>The ancient Aramaic translation of the Bible</note> acts in its usual manner regarding these expressions. For whatever occurs that seems to ascribe and attribute a physical body to God, it <margin>*How the Targum is accustomed to remove corporeality from God.*</margin> explains by an ellipsis of the relative term and attributes it to some other thing that refers to God, and yet is distinct: as in, *And behold, the Lord stood above it*. <margin>Genesis 28:13</margin> The Targum explains: *The Glory of the Lord stood above it*. Likewise, *The Lord watch between me and you*. <margin>Genesis 31:49</margin> The Targum translates: *The Word of the Lord watch*. And thus that Interpreter <note>referring to Onkelos, the traditional author of the Targum</note> (of blessed memory) is perpetually accustomed to do. Whence in the present place also, where it is said, *And the Lord passed before his face*, he has: *And the Lord caused His Majesty to pass before him*. According to him, therefore, that thing which passed will undoubtedly be something created, and the Suffix Pronoun וֹ *vov* in *Panav* [His face] must be referred to Moses, so that *al panav* <note>before his face</note> is the same as *lephanav* <note>in front of him</note>, just as in that passage: *And the gift passed [lephanav] before him*. And <margin>Genesis 32:21</margin> this exposition of Onkelos is also elegant and good, which the Scripture itself seems to confirm when it says, *And it shall come to pass, while my glory passes by*. <margin>Exodus 33:22</margin>

D

<summary>Maimonides explains that Moses' request to see God's "face" represents a desire for impossible intellectual perception of God's essence, which God redirects toward His "back"—the understanding of His actions and attributes. He also highlights how the Targum (Aramaic translation) avoids physical descriptions of God by substituting terms like "Glory" or "Majesty."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Hebrew verb "Abhar" (to pass) and its application to God's presence. # MORE NEVOCHIM

...my glory. For it declares that the thing which passed was something attributed to God, not His very essence itself. And concerning this Glory (which is mentioned in the words just cited), it must be understood what He immediately adds: *Until I have passed*. Likewise, that phrase: *And the Lord passed before his face* <note>original: "al panav"</note>. But if it could not be done otherwise, and a grammatical omission <note>original: "ellipsis"</note> of some relative term had to be assumed—just as Onkelos <note>A famous 2nd-century scholar known for his Aramaic translation of the Torah, which avoids describing God in human terms</note> always did, placing in its stead sometimes the word *Glory* <note>Aramaic: "Jekara"</note>, sometimes the *Word* <note>Aramaic: "Memra"</note>, and occasionally the *Majesty* <note>Hebrew/Aramaic: "Schechinah"</note>, depending on the context of each passage—then we also could provide such a missing related term, namely a *Voice* <note>Hebrew: "Kol"</note>, and explain that passage thus: *And the voice of the Lord passed before him and cried out*.

<margin>Exodus 36:6</margin>
For we showed before that this verb *to pass* <term>Abhar: the Hebrew root for passing or crossing over</term> is used regarding a voice, from that passage: *And they caused a voice to pass through the camp*. And thus

<margin>Crying out is attributed to the Voice.</margin>
the Voice will be that which cried out. Nor should this seem absurd to you, that *Shouting* or *the act of shouting* <note>original Greek: "τὸ Clamare"</note> is attributed to a *Voice*. For in a clearly similar way, and with the same words, Speech is attributed to the voice of God

<margin>Numbers 7:89</margin>
the Blessed; as in, *He heard the voice speaking to him*. For by the same reasoning that Speech and Utterance are attributed to a voice, so too can Shouting be attributed to it. Furthermore, we have an explicit passage in which both *to speak* <note>original Greek/Hebrew: "τὸ Amar"</note> and *to cry out* <note>original Greek/Hebrew: "τὸ Kara"</note> are attributed to a voice, where it is

<margin>Isaiah 40:6</margin>
read: *A voice* [Kol] *says, "Cry out!"* And it said (namely, that voice), *What shall I cry?* So then, according to this exposition, the sense of the present passage will be this: *And the voice of the Lord passed before him, and it cried out, "Lord, Lord, God mighty, merciful, and gracious,"* and that doubling, *Lord, Lord*, will be the act of calling or the person

<margin>Exodus 3:4. Genesis 22:11.</margin>
calling, as in that instance, *Moses, Moses*. Likewise, *Abraham, Abraham*. And this explanation is also quite elegant and suitable.

However, there is no reason for you to be troubled that this matter, being so profound and difficult, admits several interpretations. For these in no way hinder our purpose (or: *For they bring no detriment to our purpose, namely, of removing corporeality* <note>The false belief that God has a physical body or human form</note> *from God*). You are free to choose from these and follow whichever you wish: namely, either that this whole affair was a mere Prophetic Vision; and that by that which Moses sought, as well as by that which was denied to him, and by that which he perceived, only Intellectual Perceptions are to be understood; that all these things, I say, are intellectual, and there is nothing sensible <note>perceivable by the physical senses</note> in them, as we explained from the beginning; Or, that there was also a sensory perception—namely, a Vision—but of some created thing, through the vision of which he attained the perfection of Intellectual Perception, as Onkelos explained—provided, however, that the perception of that Vision is not established to have occurred in a Prophetic Vision (that is, in the phantasy and imagi-

<summary>Maimonides offers two interpretations of Moses' encounter with God: either the "passing" refers to a created voice or light (the Glory) rather than God Himself, or the entire event was a purely intellectual, prophetic vision intended to remove any physical attributes from the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the Hebrew verb "Abhar" (to pass) and begins the analysis of the verb "Bo" (to come), continuing the project of interpreting physical descriptions of God as metaphors for His power, word, or the created light known as the "Glory."</meta>

# PART I. CHAPTER XXI.
<sig>D 2</sig>

<margin>Genesis 15:17</margin>
...vision) <note>Completing the word "imaginatione" from the previous page</note> just as it is said of Abraham: *And behold, a smoking furnace and a flaming torch, which passed between those divisions* <note>This refers to the "Covenant of the Pieces," where God's presence is represented by fire</note>. Or finally, it may be understood that an "apprehension of hearing" <note>An intellectual or sensory perception of sound</note> was joined to it; that is to say, some voice—and indeed one created by God without doubt—was said to be that which passed before his [Moses'] face. From these opinions, I say, choose whichever you prefer. For they all tend toward this single goal: that you do not believe the phrase, *And He passed* <gloss>Hebrew: lephanav—before him</gloss>, should be understood like the phrase, *Pass before this people* <margin>Exodus 17:5</margin> (which refers to a physical movement or transition of a body). For God, the Blessed One, is not a body, nor does motion apply to Him; therefore, the verb *Abhar* <term>Abhar: "to pass" or "cross over"</term> cannot be attributed to Him according to its primary, literal meaning.

---

### CHAPTER XXII.
->In which the word *Bo* is explained<-
->*To Come*<-

<margin>בוא [Bo] To Come.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 27:35</margin>
*Bo* signifies, in its first sense, the arrival of living beings at a certain place, or to a specific individual: as in, *Thy brother came [Ba] with guile*.

<margin>Genesis 43:24</margin>
<margin>Leviticus 25:2</margin>
Next, it also signifies the entry or "going in" of the same into a place: as in, *And Joseph entered [Vajabho] the house*. Likewise, *When you shall come [tabhou] into the land*.

<margin>Judges 13:17</margin>
<margin>Isaiah 47:13</margin>
*Thirdly*, it signifies the arrival of an incorporeal thing <note>Something without a physical body, such as a word or an event</note>: as in, *When thy word shall come [jabho] to pass, that we may honor thee*. Likewise, *From those things which are to come upon thee*.

<margin>Job 30:26</margin>
<margin>How it is attributed to the Creator.</margin>
<margin>Exodus 19:9</margin>
<margin>Ezekiel 44:2</margin>
<margin>Zechariah 14:5</margin>
*Fourthly*, it is transferred <note>used metaphorically</note> even to certain "privations" <note>The absence of something, like light or peace</note>: as in, *And evil came [Vajabho]. And darkness came [Vajabho]*. And according to this metaphor, by which the word is transferred to that which is incorporeal, it is also applied to the Creator—specifically to both the coming of His Word and of His Majesty <note>Latin: "Majestas," often referring to the Divine Presence or Shekhinah</note>. In this sense it is said: *Behold, I come [ba] to thee in a thick cloud*. Likewise, *Because the Lord God of Israel has entered [ba] into it*. In this way, all similar passages are to be understood as referring to some visible and corporeal Majesty of His <note>Referring to a created sign of God's presence, like a cloud or light, rather than God's essence</note>: as in, *And the Lord my God shall come [Uba]*, which means, *His Word shall come*—as if to say, He will fulfill the promises which He pledged by the hands of His prophets. For this is what it means when it further says, *And all the Saints with thee*; it is as if to say: *And the Word of the Lord God shall come by the hands of all the Saints who are with thee, who have spoken to Israel*.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the verb "to pass" (Abhar) and begins explaining "to come" (Bo), arguing that when these verbs describe God, they refer to His Word, His promises, or a created prophetic vision rather than physical movement.</summary>
<keywords>Abhar, Bo, anthropomorphism, Divine Majesty, Shekhinah, prophecy, Moses, Abraham, biblical metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Hebrew verbs used in the Bible to describe God's actions. Having previously discussed "Abhar" (to pass) and "Bo" (to come), Maimonides now moves to "Yatza" (to go out) and "Shuv" (to return), further stripping away literal, physical interpretations of the Divine.</meta>

### CHAPTER XXIII.
### *In which the words יצא (Yatza), meaning "To Go Out" or "To Exit," and שוב (Shuv), meaning "To Return," are explained.*

<margin>יצא (Yatza): To go out, to exit.</margin>
The word *Yatza* <note>original: "Jaza"</note> is the opposite of the preceding word *Bo* [to come], and it primarily signifies the physical exit of a body from a place where it was resting to another place—whether that body is animate <note>living</note> or inanimate <note>non-living</note>: as in,
<margin>Genesis 44:4; Exodus 22:6.</margin>
*They had gone out [yatz'u] from the city.* Also, *When a fire has gone out [tetze].* <note>Here, "gone out" refers to a fire spreading or breaking out.</note>

<margin>Esther 7:8; Esther 1:17; Isaiah 2:3; Genesis 19:23.</margin>
Next, the term is transferred metaphorically to the showing or manifestation of an incorporeal <note>non-physical</note> thing: as in, *The word went out [yatza] from the King’s mouth.* Also, *When the word of the Queen shall go out [yetze] to all women,* which refers to the news of her transgression of the King's command, which she did not obey. Likewise, *For the Law shall go out [tetze] from Zion.* Also, *The sun had gone out [yatza] over the earth,* which is to say, the vision or appearance of its light.

<margin>"To go out": in what sense it is used regarding God.</margin>
And according to this second meaning <note>manifestation</note>, the word *Yatza* must always be understood when it is attributed to God, the Blessed One: as in, *Behold, the Lord*
<margin>Isaiah 26:21.</margin>
*comes out [yotze] from His place,* that is to say, He makes seen, shows, or manifests His word, which until now was hidden from us; it is as if to say: something shall happen that was not there before. For whatever is created by God is attributed to His "word": as in,
<margin>Psalm 33:6.</margin>
*By the word of the Lord the heavens were created, and by the breath of His mouth all their host.* This imagery is taken from the works of earthly kings, whose instruments for perfecting and executing their will are their spoken words. God, the Blessed One, however, clearly requires no instrument to act, but perfects His work by His will alone. Furthermore, He possesses no literal speech, as will be explained later. <note>Maimonides argues later in the book that "Divine Speech" is a metaphor for the overflow of His will into creation.</note>

<margin>שוב (Shuv): To return.</margin>
Just as the word *Yatza* is adapted to signify the manifestation of some divine work—as we showed
<margin>Isaiah 26:21.</margin>
from the verse, *Behold, the Lord comes out from His place*—so, on the contrary, the word *Shuv* refers to the withdrawal or hiding 
<margin>Hosea 5:15.</margin>
of that work, according to His will alone: as in, *I will go and return [ve-ashuv] to my place.* For this signifies the withdrawal of the Majesty or the visible presence of God <term>the Shekhinah: the dwelling presence of God on earth</term> which was among us. This is followed by the "Privation of the Providence of God" <note>the removal of His protective care</note> 
<margin>Deuteronomy 31:17.</margin>
toward us, just as in the threat of punishment God says: *And I will hide my face from them, and they shall be consumed.* For when something is deprived of the Providence of God, it is then exposed and left vulnerable to all the "accidents" <note>chance events or natural misfortunes</note> that can happen to it. Because this threat is so harsh, God uses a "cognomination" <note>a substitute name or figure of speech</note> in expressing it, saying, *I will return to my place.*

<vocab>Yatza, Shuv, More Nevochim, Maimonides, Providence of God, Zion</vocab>

<summary>Maimonides explains that biblical terms like "going out" or "returning" applied to God do not imply physical movement, but rather the manifestation or withdrawal of His divine influence and protection (Providence).</summary>
<keywords>metaphor, incorporeality, divine manifestation, Providence, Yatza, Shuv, Shekhinah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Maimonides' project of interpreting biblical terms that appear to describe God in physical, human ways. After discussing "Yatza" (to go out) on the previous page, he now turns to "Halach" (to walk or go), following the same pattern of moving from literal physical motion to metaphorical divine action.</meta>

Part I. Chapter XXIV.
D 3

### Chapter XXIV.

->In which the word הלך *Halach* is explained<-
->*He went.*<-

<margin>הלך (*Halach*): He walked, He went.</margin>
The word *Halach* <note>original: "Halach"</note> also belongs to that category of terms that signify the specific motions of living beings or animate things: as in, *And Jacob went [halach] on his way.* This usage is found very frequently.
<margin>Genesis 32:1.</margin>

Next, it is applied to the movement <note>or "traction"</note> of bodies that are more subtle than the bodies of living beings: as in, *The waters continued to go [haloch] and decrease.* Likewise, *And the fire ran [utehallech] along the ground.*
<margin>Genesis 8:5; Exodus 9:23.</margin>

Thirdly, it is transferred to the spreading, extension, and manifestation of something, even if it is entirely incorporeal <note>non-physical</note>: as in, *Its voice shall go [jelech] like a serpent.* Similarly, *The voice of the Lord God walking [mithallech] in the garden.* For the "voice" <note>or sound</note> is the thing of which it is said that it "walked" in the garden.
<margin>Jeremiah 46:22; Genesis 3:8.</margin>

<margin>In what sense it is used regarding God.</margin>
And according to this concept, every instance of *Halach* that occurs in relation to the Creator is understood: insofar as it is transferred to an incorporeal thing, signifying either the "spreading" of something, or the "withdrawal" of God’s Providence. This withdrawal is clearly similar to the departure or removal of a living being from a certain thing or place, which occurs through "going" [*halichah*]. For just as He expresses the removal of His Providence by the "hiding of His face," saying, *I will surely hide [haster astir] my face in that day,* so He also sometimes expresses it through "going" [*Halicha*], which is nothing other than a withdrawal from a certain thing: as in, *I will go [elecha] and return to my place.* Furthermore, in that passage where it says, *And the anger of the Lord was kindled against them, and He departed [vajelech],* both meanings are present at once: namely, the removal of God's Providence (indicated by the word "went"), and the spreading, revelation, or manifestation of something—specifically God’s anger—which "went" and followed them, on account of which Miriam is said to have become leprous, white as snow.
<margin>Deuteronomy 31:18; Hosea 5:15; Numbers 12:9; Ibid. v. 10.</margin>

Finally, this word is adapted to describe conduct and customs, which are performed by the mind alone without physical motion: as in, *And you shall walk [vehalachta] in His ways.* Likewise, *You shall walk [telechu] after the Lord your God.* Likewise, *And let us walk [venelecha] in the light of the Lord.*
<margin>Deuteronomy 28:9; Deuteronomy 13:4; Isaiah 2:5.</margin>

<vocab>Halach (to go/walk), Motion, Creator, Providence of God, Halichah (the act of going), Hiding of the face, Conduct/Customs</vocab>

<summary>This chapter explains that the Hebrew verb "Halach" (to walk) is used metaphorically when applied to God to describe the manifestation of His will or the withdrawal of His divine protection (Providence).</summary>
<keywords>Halach, divine motion, metaphor, Providence, anthropomorphism, Miriam, walking in God's ways, biblical philology</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of divine "movement" (Halach) on page 29 to the concept of divine "dwelling" (Shachan). Maimonides continues to deconstruct physical descriptions of God into metaphysical concepts of Providence and Majesty. This leads into Chapter 26, which introduces the fundamental interpretive principle that "the Law speaks in human language."</meta>

### CHAPTER XXV.
### *In which the word שכן (Shachan), meaning "To Dwell," is explained.*

<margin>שכן (*Shachan*): He Dwelled. Genesis 14:13. Genesis 35:22.</margin>
This word, as is well known, signifies the continuation of a stay, that is, remaining in a specific place. For example: *But he [shochen] dwelled in the oaks of Mamre.* <note>original: "Ipse autem schochen habitabat in quercubus Mamre"</note> Also: *And it happened [bishkon] when Israel dwelled.* <note>original: "Et fuit bischkon cùm habitaret Israël"</note> This is clear and manifest. For to dwell in a certain place is nothing other than to continuously stand or remain there. For when living beings stay for a long time in a place, whether it be a public space or their own property, they are said to "dwell" in that place, despite the fact that they may move about within it from time to time. As in: <margin>Genesis 14:13. Genesis 35:22.</margin> *Abraham [shochen] dwelled in the oaks of Mamre.* Also: *And it happened when Israel dwelled [bishkon].*

Furthermore, the word is adapted to describe inanimate things, and indeed anything that constantly and steadily remains in a certain thing, even if that thing in which it remains is not a physical place, nor is the thing remaining a living being. For example: <margin>Job 3:5.</margin> *Let a cloud [tishkon] dwell upon it* (meaning, upon that day). In this case, it is obvious that a cloud is not a living thing, nor is a "day" a body or a physical object, but rather a segment of time.

<margin>*In what sense it is said of God.*</margin>
And according to this usage, the term is also attributed to the Creator: specifically to express the Dwelling or Abiding of His Majesty <note>The "Majesty" refers to the *Shekhinah*, the manifest presence of God.</note> and His Providence in a certain place. For wherever He has placed the signs of His Majesty and Presence, or wherever He causes His Providence to dwell, <margin>Exodus 24:16. Exodus 29:48. Deuteronomy 33:16.</margin> He is said to "Dwell." For example: *And [vajishkon] the glory of the Lord dwelled.* Also: *And [veshachanti] I will dwell in the midst of the children of Israel.* Also: *By the will of the [shocheni] dweller in the bush.* Therefore, in whatever place this action (Dwelling) is found attributed to the Creator, it signifies the Abiding of His Majesty—that is, of the created Glory in a certain place—or Divine Providence acting upon something, according to the specific nature of each location.

---

### CHAPTER XXVI.
### *Why the Law used Anthropomorphisms.*

<margin>*The Law speaks through Anthropomorphisms.*</margin>
That general Rule is well known which serves to explain all the modes of speaking that occur in this subject: *The Law speaks according to the tongue of the sons of men.* <note>A famous rabbinic maxim: "Dibrah Torah ki-lashon bnei adam."</note> Its meaning is this: whatever human beings are able to grasp through their intellect and imagination from the very beginning of their thought is attributed to the Creator in the Scripture.

<summary>Maimonides explains that the Hebrew word for "dwelling" (Shachan) refers to the localization of God's Presence (Shekhinah) or Providence rather than a physical location. He then introduces the pivotal rule that the Torah uses human-like language (anthropomorphism) to make divine concepts accessible to human understanding.</summary>
<keywords>Shachan, Dwelling, Shekhinah, Divine Majesty, Providence, Anthropomorphism, Language of Men, Maimonides, Torah interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 25, which dealt with the term "dwelling" (Shachan). It concludes the previous argument and transitions into Chapter 26. The text begins mid-word with "ri," likely the end of the Latin word "dici" (to be said) or "appelleri" (to be called), referring to how God is described.</meta>

### Part I. Chapter XXVI.

[...to be called.] <note>The text begins with the suffix "ri," the end of a word from the previous page.</note> From this, He is described through physical attributes and names, so that it may be taught that He is a Being <term>Ens; a philosophical term for something that exists</term>. For the common people do not grasp the concept of Being <note>original: "Entitatem"</note> or Essence <note>original: "Essentiam"</note> from the start of their thinking, except in physical things. Hence, whatever is not a body, or a physical thing existing within a body, is not believed by them to exist at all.

<margin>
*Why physical attributes and names are ascribed to God.*
</margin>

So also, whatever we consider to be a "perfection" is attributed to the Blessed God, to teach that He is most perfect in every way and degree, and that there is no defect or lack <note>original: "privationem"</note> in Him. On the other hand, we do not read of anything being attributed to God in which common people perceive any imperfection. Therefore, God is not described as eating, drinking, sleeping, being sick, and so on; instead, He is described only by those things which humans hold to be perfections.

<margin>
*Why human perfections are attributed to God.*
</margin>

<margin>
*Why Eating, Drinking, Sleeping, etc., are not attributed to God.*
</margin>

However, even though these traits are the highest perfections from our human perspective, they are, in relation to God's nature, in the highest degree of imperfection. But people assume that if these human perfections were separated from the Creator, it would imply a great imperfection in His nature.

<margin>
*paragraph 2. in the preface.*
</margin>

Thus, you know that "Motion" is counted among the perfections of living creatures, and is even necessary for their perfection. For just as they constantly have need of food and drink to compensate for what they lose daily, so too they have need of Motion to pursue what is good for them (or what they are used to) and to flee from evil. Hence, in itself, there is no difference whether you attribute food and drink or motion to the Creator; but it is done only according to "the language of the sons of men," <note>original: "linguam filiorum hominum"; this is a translation of the Hebrew principle "Dibra Torah k’lashon bnei adam," meaning the Bible uses human metaphors so we can understand it.</note> that is, according to the imagination of the common people. To them, Eating and Drinking are imperfections, but Motion is not—even though Motion itself necessarily presupposes imperfection. For it has been demonstrated that whatever moves is physical and divisible. Later, it will be explained to you that because God is incorporeal <note>not made of a physical body</note>, neither motion nor rest applies to Him. For "Rest" applies to nothing except that which is capable of moving. Therefore, all those names which signify the motion of living beings are attributed to the Creator in the manner we have described: for example, when "Life" is attributed to Him, or when He is said to live. For Motion is an accidental property belonging specifically to living things.

When, therefore, corporeality <note>having a physical body</note> is removed from God, then all these other things will also be removed from Him: namely, Descending, Ascending, Walking, Standing, Going Around, Sitting, Dwelling, Going Out, Coming, Passing By, and the like. It is useful and necessary to explain these things a little more fully, as we have done, not only for the sake of the common people who are filled with such opinions, but also for those who strive for human perfection, so that they may rid themselves of these thoughts that began in the years of their youth.

### CHAPTER [XXVI] <note>The sig "CAP." acts as a catchword for the next section.</note>

<sig>C A P.</sig>
<meta>catchword: CAP.</meta>

<summary>Maimonides explains that the Bible uses physical metaphors for God because humans can only conceive of "existence" through physical things. He introduces the principle that the Law speaks in human language, attributing "perfections" like motion to God while excluding "imperfections" like eating, even though both are technically inapplicable to a non-physical Being.</summary>
<keywords>Anthropomorphism, Attributes of God, Divine Perfection, Motion, Incorporeality, Language of Man, Maimonides, Divine Essence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how language is used to describe God. While the previous chapter explained why the Bible uses human terms generally, Chapter 27 focuses on Onkelos, the famous translator of the Torah into Aramaic, and his specific techniques for protecting God's immaterial nature by avoiding physical descriptions.</meta>

### CHAPTER XXVII.

### *How careful and diligent Onkelos was in removing corporeality from God. The difference between a prophecy of a dream, a Vision, and when it is said: "And the word of the Lord came to me, saying," etc.*

<margin>Onkelos was diligent in removing physicality from God.</margin>
Onkelos the Proselyte <term>Onkelos; a famous 2nd-century convert to Judaism and scholar who translated the Torah into Aramaic</term>, a man most accomplished in the Hebrew and Chaldean <note>original: "Chaldaica"; referring here to the Aramaic language</note> languages, proved his diligence and industry very energetically in—among other things—removing the concept of corporeality <term>corporeitas; the state of having a physical body</term> from God. For all the Attributes or Names attributed to God in Scripture that seem to imply a physical body, he explained according to their true and genuine sense. Hence, whenever he encountered a word that signifies some kind of *Motion*, he replaced *Motion* with *Apparition* or the *Manifestation* of some created splendor—that is, the Divine Majesty or Providence. Thus, he interprets *He descended* <note>original: "Jarad"; Hebrew for 'descended'</note> *the Lord* as *The Lord will reveal Himself* <note>original: "Jitgele"; Aramaic for 'He will be revealed'</note>. Likewise, *And the Lord descended* <note>original: "Vajered"</note> becomes *And He appeared* <note>original: "Vajitgele"</note>, but certainly not *And He went down* <note>original: "Unechat"; a more literal Aramaic word for physical descent</note>. Similarly, *I will go down now and see* <note>original: "Eredah na"</note> is translated as *I will reveal myself and see*. And this is a constant rule in his translation.

<margin>Exodus 19:18. Genesis 11:5. Genesis 18:21.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 46:4.</margin>
But truly, that passage, *I will go down with you into Egypt* <note>original: "Ego eredah"</note>, he translated word-for-word as *I will go down with you* <note>original: "Ego echut"</note>, which is remarkable. But we learn from this the great perfection of that man and the excellence of his interpretation—how he understood each thing exactly as it is to be understood and grasped. For through this Translation <term>Targum; the Aramaic translation of the Hebrew Bible</term>, he revealed to us a great matter regarding the nature of Prophecy: namely, that we should pay attention to the beginning of that narrative, which says: *God spoke to Israel in visions of the night, and said, Jacob, Jacob; and he answered etc. And He said, I am etc. I will go down etc.* For after he concluded from the beginning of that story that all those things happened in "Visions of the night," he did not hesitate to report every single thing said in the Vision using the same literal words. And such a narration is true. For it only reports what was said to him (*Jacob*), not what actually happened in physical reality—unlike the passage *And the Lord descended upon Mount Sinai* <note>Exodus 19:20</note>, which is a narrative of what actually occurred. Therefore, in that case, he translated it as an "Apparition" or "Revelation," and thus removed from God anything that pertains to the essence of Motion.

<margin>Ibid. verse 2. <note>Refers to Genesis 46:2</note></margin>
<margin>Exodus 19:20.</margin>

<summary>Maimonides praises the translator Onkelos for his systematic efforts to avoid describing God in physical terms, explaining that Onkelos only used literal language for God's movements when they occurred within a prophetic dream or vision.</summary>
<keywords>Onkelos, Targum, corporeality, divine motion, prophecy, Jacob, Mount Sinai, Aramaic translation, anthropomorphism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Onkelos the Proselyte’s translation techniques. The previous page established that Onkelos typically avoids attributing physical motion to God by substituting terms like "revelation" for "descending." Chapter 27 now concludes by discussing how he treats prophetic visions and the role of angels as divine messengers.</meta>

# PART I. CHAPTER XXVII.
# E

Regarding "imaginary" matters—such as a narrative describing what was said to someone in a vision—Onkelos left those as they were, which is surprising. You are reminded by this that there is a great difference between that which is said to happen *in a dream* <note>original: "In somnio"; Hebrew: "Bachalôm"</note> or *in visions of the night*, and that which is said to happen *in a vision* <note>original: "Bemachase ubemareh"</note>, and that which is said simply and absolutely, such as: *And the word of the Lord came to me, saying*, or, *And the Lord said to me, saying*. 

Furthermore, in my judgment, it is possible that Onkelos understood the word *Elohim* <term>Elohim; a Hebrew name for God that can also refer to angels, judges, or powers</term> to mean an "Angel" in certain contexts, and therefore did not hesitate to translate the phrase as: *I will go down with you* <note>Genesis 46:4</note>. It should not seem incredible to you that *Elohim* there referred to an Angel, even though the text says, *I am God, the God of your father*. For it is possible that those words were uttered by God through an Angel. 

Do you not see that the very same one of whom it is said, *And the Angel of the Lord said to me in a dream, "Jacob," and I answered, "Here I am,"*—that same one, I say, says at the end of that narrative, *I am the God of Bethel, where you anointed a pillar and where you made a vow to me* <note>Genesis 31:11-13</note>? There is no doubt that Jacob made his vow to God, not to an Angel. But there is nothing new in the books of the Prophets <margin>*Words spoken by Angels are often narrated as if God Himself had spoken them.*</margin> about narrating words spoken by an Angel in God's name as if God Himself were uttering them. Therefore, all such instances are to be understood through the "omission of a relative term" <term>ellipsis relativi; a grammatical term meaning a word like "messenger" is implied but not written</term>, as if it were said: *I am the messenger of the God of your father*; *I am the messenger of the God who appeared to you in Bethel*, and so on. Many such things will be encountered when we discuss Prophecy and its levels, as well as Angels, according to the purpose of this book.

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

->*In which the Name <term>רגל</term> Regel (Foot) is explained.*<-

<margin><term>רגל</term> Regel (Foot). Exodus 21:24.</margin> **R**EGEL is an "equivocal" word <term>vox æquivoca; a term with multiple distinct meanings</term>. It signifies, first, the feet of living creatures: as in, *Foot for foot* <note>original: "Regel tahat regel"</note>.

<margin>Exodus 11:8.</margin> Secondly, it is transferred to mean something that follows after another: as in, *Go out, you and all the people who are at your feet* <note>original: "beraglecha"; usually translated as "who follow you"</note>, that is, those who follow you.

<margin>Genesis 30:30.</margin> Thirdly, it also signifies "Cause": as in, *And the Lord has blessed you at my foot* <note>original: "leragli"; Hebrew idiom for "on my account"</note>, that is, for my sake, or because of me. For when something exists because of another thing, or by the power of some other thing, then 그 thing is its cause. This usage occurs often, such as: *According to the foot of the work, and according to the foot of the children* <margin>Genesis 33:14.</margin> <note>original: "Leregel hammelacha, uleregel hajeladim"</note>, which means because of the flock and because of the children. 

Likewise, *And his feet shall stand in that day upon the Mount of Olives* <margin>Zechariah 14:4.</margin> <note>The text cites Zach. 14. 3, but the quote is from verse 4</note>, which means his "causes" shall stand, or rather his "effects"—that is, the miracles which will then be seen in that place, of which God (Blessed be He) is the efficient cause. Jonathan son of Uziel <term>Jonathan F. Uziel; the traditional author of the Aramaic translation (Targum) of the Prophets</term>, of blessed memory, leans toward this interpretation: *And he shall reveal himself with his power, in that day, upon...*

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on why some biblical passages attribute physical actions to God by explaining that angels often speak in the first person for God. It then begins a linguistic analysis of the word "Regel" (foot), showing how it can mean a physical foot, a follower, or a cause/reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Onkelos the Proselyte, focusing on his specific translation choices in the Aramaic Targum. ### CHAPTER XXVII.

### On the caution and diligence shown by Onkelos in removing physical characteristics from God. The distinction between a prophecy received in a dream, a vision, and when the text states: "And the word of the Lord came to me, saying," etc.

<margin>Onkelos was diligent in removing physical attributes from God.</margin>
Onkelos the Proselyte, a man of great perfection in the Hebrew and Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldaica"</note> languages, demonstrated his diligence and hard work most effectively by removing all suggestions of a physical body from God. For all the attributes or names assigned to God in Scripture that seem to imply a physical form, he interpreted according to their true and original meaning. Thus, whenever he encounters a word designating some kind of **Motion**, he substitutes it with **Appearance** or the **Manifestation of some created glory**—that is, the Divine Majesty or Providence. For instance, he translates *Jarad* <note>original Hebrew: "Descended"</note> as *Jitgele* <note>original Aramaic: "He will reveal Himself"</note>. Similarly, "And the Lord descended" <margin>Exodus 19:18. Genesis 11:5. Genesis 18:21.</margin> is translated as "And the Lord revealed Himself," and not "And He went down." Likewise, "I will go down now and see" is rendered as "I will reveal myself and see." This is a consistent rule throughout his translation.

<margin>Genesis 46:4.</margin>
However, the phrase "I will go down with you into Egypt" was translated by him word-for-word as "I will go down with you," <note>original: "Ego echut descendam tecum"</note> which is quite surprising. But from this, we learn of this man’s great perfection and the excellence of his interpretation—how he understood each thing exactly as it should be understood. Through this translation <term>Targum; the traditional Aramaic translation of the Hebrew Bible</term>, he revealed a great principle regarding the nature of prophecy: namely, that we must pay attention to the beginning of the narrative. In this case, it begins: <margin>Genesis 46:2.</margin> "God spoke to Israel in visions of the night, and said: 'Jacob, Jacob,' and he answered... and then He said... I will go down," etc. Since Onkelos concluded from the start of the story that all these things happened in **Visions of the night**, he did not hesitate to report the individual words spoken in the vision exactly as they were. Such a narrative is true to the experience; it merely records what was said to Jacob, not what actually happened in physical reality. This differs from the passage, <margin>Exodus 19:20.</margin> "And the Lord descended upon Mount Sinai," which is a narrative of an event that actually took place in the world. For that reason, he translated it as an Appearance or Revelation, thereby removing from God anything relating to the physical essence of Motion.

<summary>This page explains how the translator Onkelos handled descriptions of God "moving," showing that he used literal language only when the movement occurred within a prophetic dream or vision. For actual physical events, Onkelos substituted "descending" with "revealing" to ensure God was not seen as a physical being.</summary>

<keywords>Onkelos, incorporeality of God, Targum, prophecy, visions of the night, Jacob, Mount Sinai, divine motion, translation theory</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page concludes the analysis of Onkelos the Proselyte’s translation methods regarding prophetic visions and begins a new chapter on the linguistic analysis of the Hebrew word "Regel" (Foot).</meta>

Part I. Chapter XXVII.

E

As for those things belonging to the imagination—such as the narration of what was said to someone in a prophetic state—Onkelos left them exactly as they are, which is a matter of wonder. From this, you are reminded that there is a great difference between that which is said to happen *in a dream*, or *in visions of the night*, and that which is said to be *in a vision* <note>original Hebrew: "Bemachase ubemareh"</note>, and between that which is said simply and absolutely, such as, *And the word of the Lord came to me, saying*, or, *And the Lord said to me, saying*. 

Furthermore, in my judgment, it is also possible that Onkelos understood the word *Elohim* <note>a Hebrew name for God that can also refer to angels or judges</note> to mean an "Angel," and therefore he did not hesitate to translate the phrase as, *I will go down with you*. It should not seem incredible to you that *Elohim* there referred to an Angel, even though it says, *I am God, the God of your father*. For it is possible that those words were brought forth (from God) through the medium of an Angel. 

Do you not see that the very same one of whom it is said, *And the Angel of the Lord said to me in a dream, Jacob; and I answered, here I am*, is the same one who says at the end of that narration, *I am the God of Bethel, where you anointed the pillar, and where you vowed a vow unto me*? There is no doubt that Jacob made his vow to God, and not to an Angel. But this is nothing new in the books of the Prophets <margin>*Words brought forth by Angels are often narrated as if God Himself had spoken them.*</margin>—that those words which an Angel speaks in the name of God are narrated as if God himself uttered them. Therefore, all such instances are to be understood through the "omission of a relative term" <note>the linguistic term 'ellipsis'; assuming a missing connecting word</note>, as if it were said: *I am the envoy of the God of your father: I am the messenger of the God who appeared to you in Bethel, etc.* Many such things will be encountered when we discuss Prophecy and its degrees, as well as Angels, according to the purpose of this book.

### Chapter XXVIII.
### ->In which the name "Regel" (Foot) is explained.<-

<margin>Regel (Foot).</margin>
<margin>Exodus 21:24.</margin>
The word *Regel* <note>Hebrew: רגל</note> is an equivocal term <note>a word with multiple meanings depending on context</note>. Primarily, it signifies the **Feet** of living creatures: as in, *Foot for foot* <note>original: "Regel tahat regel"</note>.
<margin>Exodus 11:8.</margin>
Secondly, it is transferred to mean something that follows after another: as in, *Go out, you and all the people who are at your feet* <note>original: "beraglecha"</note>, that is, who follow you.
<margin>Genesis 30:30.</margin>
Thirdly, it also signifies a **Cause**: as in, *And the Lord blessed you at my foot* <note>original: "leragli"</note>, that is, for my sake, or because of me. For when something exists because of another thing, or by the power of some other thing, then that thing is its cause. This occurs often, such as: *According to the foot of the work, and according to the foot of the children* <note>original: "Leregel hammelacha, uleregel hajeladim"</note>, which means because of the flock <margin>Genesis 33:14.</margin> and because of the children. Likewise: *And his feet shall stand in that day upon the mount of Olives* <margin>Zechariah 14:3.</margin>, which means his "causes" shall stand, or rather his "effects"—that is, the miracles which will then be seen in that place, of which God (Blessed be He) is the efficient cause. Jonathan ben Uzziel <note>an early translator of the Bible into Aramaic</note> (of blessed memory) inclines toward this interpretation: *And he shall reveal himself with his power, in that day, upon...*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Hebrew word "Regel" (Foot) and how the translator Onkelos handles anthropomorphic descriptions of God. Specifically, it addresses the vision of the seventy elders of Israel in Exodus 24:10, where they "saw" God and what was "under His feet."</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

...the Mount of Olives. And in this way he is accustomed to translate other things of this kind.

<margin>Exodus 24:10.</margin>
Concerning that passage where it is said, *And under His feet* <note>original Hebrew: "V’thachat raglav"</note> as if it were *a work of the whiteness of Sapphire stone*, there Onkelos explained the word *feet* as if it were a title for the Throne. For he says, *And under the throne of His glory*. Observe here again and marvel at how diligently and carefully Onkelos removes "corporeity" <term>corporeity: the state of having a physical body</term> from God, and anything that even from a distance seems to introduce or confirm it. For he does not say simply *And under His throne*: because if he had attributed the Throne to God Himself, it might have seemed at first glance to follow that God is supported by some throne, and thus that there is corporeity in Him. Instead, he attributes the Throne to His Glory—that is, to the Majesty, which is a created light. So in his Targum <note>the Aramaic translation/paraphrase</note> of that passage:

<margin>Exodus 17:16.</margin>
*Because a hand is upon the throne of Jah* <note>a name for God</note>, he says: *Before God, whose Majesty is upon the throne of glory*.

<margin>Jeremiah 17:12.</margin>
And thus by common consent that phrase *throne of glory* must also be understood in the same way.

But these things are beyond our current scope; therefore I return to our purpose. I say, then, that Onkelos's aim in translating the cited passage was to remove corporeity from God, not to declare or explain what they <note>namely, the "Nobles of the children of Israel"</note> actually perceived, or what that parable intended. This is his custom almost everywhere: he does not enter into or involve himself in explaining the meaning of parables and allegories, but remains focused on removing corporeity. This is because the removal of corporeity is a matter of demonstration and necessary for faith; hence he freely pronounces his opinion there as the matter seems to require. 

However, the explanation of parables is a matter of uncertainty and doubt; for it is possible they have this intention or another. Also, the things within them are usually very obscure. Understanding them is not among the articles of Faith, nor is their comprehension suited for the common people. Therefore, Onkelos did not wish to involve himself in this task.

For us, however, according to the plan of our work in this book, these things must necessarily be explained. I say, therefore, that *Under His feet* <note>Hebrew: "Thachat raglav"</note> is the same as saying "because of Him" or "through Him" <note>Hebrew: "Missibbato ubiglalos"</note>, as we have explained. What they perceived was the *Prime Matter* <term>Prime Matter: the most basic, formless substance from which all physical things are made</term>, which comes from God the Blessed—that is, immediately—and of which He Himself is the efficient cause. For it should be noted that it says, *like the work of the whiteness of Sapphire*: for if it had intended to denote the color or external appearance, it would have said simply, *Like the whiteness of Sapphire*. But it adds, *Like the work*, because Matter, as you know, by its nature always receives forms and is acted upon; it never acts itself, except by accident. Conversely, Form always acts by itself, and is never acted upon, except by accident, as these things are explained in the books of Physics. This...

<summary>Maimonides explains how the translator Onkelos avoids physical descriptions of God by replacing "under His feet" with "under His throne of glory." He then offers his own philosophical interpretation, identifying the "sapphire work" in the elders' vision as a representation of Prime Matter created by God.</summary>

<keywords>Onkelos, Targum, Prime Matter, corporeity, Throne of Glory, Exodus 24:10, anthropomorphism, Maimonides, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the "whiteness of sapphire" from Exodus 24:10, interpreting it as a metaphor for Prime Matter rather than a physical object. It then begins Chapter 29, which explores the multiple meanings of the Hebrew word "Azabh" (to grieve or rebel) to explain anthropomorphic descriptions of God’s emotions.</meta>

Part I. Chapter XXVIII.
<sig>E 2</sig>

for this reason he says, *Like a work* <note>original: "Kemaaseh Sicut opus"</note>. Furthermore, by the phrase *the whiteness of sapphire* <note>original: "libhnat haffapphir"</note>, he signifies brilliance or transparency, rather than the color white itself. For the "whiteness" of a sapphire is not a white pigment, but merely its transparency or luster. Indeed, brilliance is not a color, as is taught in the *Physics* <note>Referring to Aristotle’s works on natural philosophy</note>. For if it were a color, other colors could not be seen through it, nor would it receive other colors. Because a brilliant or lucid body is devoid of all colors, it is able to receive all other colors in turn—much like Prime Matter <term>Prime Matter: in ancient philosophy, the fundamental substance that has no form of its own but can take on any physical form</term>, which in itself is deprived of all forms, yet receives them all one after another.

Thus, that which they perceived was Prime Matter, which is attributed to God because it is the first and most excellent of the creatures subject to "generation and corruption" <note>the process of being born/created and dying/decaying</note>, and because He created it from nothing. However, this matter will be discussed elsewhere. I will only add this much: our explanation—namely, that the Nobles of the children of Israel perceived Prime Matter—is also supported by the paraphrase of Onkelos <note>a famous 2nd-century translator of the Torah into Aramaic</note>. For he says: *And under the throne of His glory*. Truly, in reality, Prime Matter is "under the Heaven," which is called the "Throne," as we saw in the preceding chapters. I was moved toward this wonderful explanation by a certain saying of *Rabbi Eliezer, son of Hyrcanus*, which you will hear in another chapter of this book. 

<margin>Part 2, Chapter 26.</margin>
The general intention of everyone (that is, both Onkelos’s view and my own) is to remove "corporeity" <term>corporeity: the quality of having a physical body</term> from God, and to establish that all these perceptions were intellectual, not sensory (meaning they refer to the Mind, not the physical senses). Therefore, observe and take note of this.

### Chapter XXIX.

->*The word <term>עצב</term> "Azabh" (To Grieve) is explained.*<-

<margin>To Grieve.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 3:16.</margin>
<margin>1 Kings 1:6.</margin>
<margin>1 Samuel 20:34.</margin>
The word *Azabh* is "equivocal" <term>equivocal: a term that has multiple distinct meanings</term>. First and most properly, it signifies Pain or Sorrow: as in, *In pain* <note>original Hebrew: "Beezebh"</note> *you shall bring forth children*. 

Secondly, it also signifies Anger or Indignation: as in, *And his father had not grieved him* <gloss>that is, made him indignant or provoked him</gloss>. Likewise: *Because he was grieved* <gloss>indignant</gloss> *for David's sake*.

<margin>Isaiah 63:10.</margin>
<margin>Psalm 78:40.</margin>
<margin>Psalm 139:24.</margin>
<margin>Psalm 56:6.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 6:6.</margin>
Thirdly, it signifies To Rebel or Rebellion: as in, *But they rebelled and grieved* <gloss>were rebellious toward</gloss> *His Holy Spirit*. Also, *How often they rebelled against Him* <note>original: "Jaazibhuhu"</note> *in the wilderness*. Likewise, *If there be any way of rebellion* <note>original: "ozebh"</note> *in me*. And, *All day long they wrest* <gloss>rebel against</gloss> *my words*. 

According to the second or third meaning, it is said: *And it grieved Him at His heart* <note>original: "Vajitazzebh belibbo"</note>. Following the second meaning, the sense would be that God was indignant against them because of the wickedness of their deeds. As for the phrase *At His heart*...
