<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 29 regarding the word "grieved" (Hebrew: *Vajitazzebh*) and the phrase "in his heart" (Hebrew: *belibbo*). It then transitions into Chapter 30, exploring the metaphorical uses of the word "to eat" (Hebrew: *Achal*).</meta>

<header>GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED</header>

<margin>Genesis 8:21.</margin>
in his [heart], just as it is also said in the Story of Noah: *And the Lord said in his heart* <note>original: "belibbo"</note>, take this explanation for it. When it is said of a man concerning a certain matter that he said it <margin>How it is said of God, that he says something "In his heart."</margin> *In his heart*, or, *To his heart*, it is understood as something which he never spoke or revealed to any man. Similarly, when God wills and decrees something within Himself that He has not revealed to any Prophet, then, when that thing (*which He willed and decreed*) has passed and been done, it is said of it, *And the Lord said in his heart*, in human fashion, according to that well-worn saying: *The Law speaks in the language of the sons of men* <note>A fundamental principle in Jewish thought: the Bible uses human metaphors to describe God so that people can understand Him</note>. And these things are clear and manifest. Because, therefore, in the Rebellion of the Flood, it is not explained that anyone was sent to them (by God) at that time to either warn them or threaten them with death, <margin>Genesis 6:6.</margin> it is therefore said of them, *that He was angry against them in His heart* <note>original: "belibbo"</note>. Similarly, when He willed that there should no longer be a Flood, <margin>Genesis 8:21.</margin> He said to no Prophet, *Go and announce this*: therefore it is also said there, *The Lord said in his heart*. The explanation of that passage *And He was grieved at His heart* <note>original: "Vajitazzebh el libbo"</note>, according to the third meaning of this word, is that man rebelled against the will of God. For *Heart* <margin>Part 1, Chapter 39.</margin> sometimes also signifies *Will*, as will be explained in the discussion on the equivocal meanings <term>Equivocal: a word with multiple distinct meanings depending on context</term> of the word *Heart* <note>original: "Lebh"</note>. [*Thus these words, according to this opinion, should be translated: And he rebelled against His heart (that is, His will).*]

### CHAPTER XXX.

->In which the word *Achal* "To Eat" is explained.<-

<margin>Achal "To Eat".</margin> This word *Achal* from its first application signifies the consumption of food and nourishment by animals, which is so well known that it is not necessary to illustrate it with examples.

Next, because two things primarily occur in eating—first, the destruction of the thing being eaten, that is, the destruction of its primary form; and second, the growth of living beings, the preservation of their constitution and essence, and the restoration of all the body's powers and faculties—it therefore has two additional meanings in this language [Hebrew]. For according to the first property [destruction], this word *Achal* is applied to any Perishing or Destruction, and so generally to the desolation of any <margin>Leviticus 26:38, Numbers 13:32, Isaiah 1:20, 2 Samuel 2:26, Numbers 11:1, Deuteronomy 4:24.</margin> form: such as, *And the land of your enemies shall eat you*. Also, *A land that eats up its inhabitants*. Also, *By the sword you shall be eaten*. Also, *The sword shall eat*. Also, *The fire of God burned among them and ate the outermost part of the camp*. Also, *The Lord is a consuming fire*, that is, He will destroy all His rebels with the destruction of fire, over which He Himself rules. And in this way it occurs very frequently. According to the second—

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of Chapter 30, specifically exploring the metaphorical use of "eating" and "drinking" to represent intellectual apprehension and spiritual wisdom. It concludes the chapter with an interpretation from the Targum and begins Chapter 31 on the inherent limits of human understanding.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Part I. Chapter XXX.</header>
<sig>E 3</sig>

[the natu]re of eating, it is also applied to Wisdom and Doctrine, and universally to Intellectual Apprehensions. Through these, the human Form <note>In medieval philosophy, the "Form" refers to the essential intellectual nature of a human being</note> is preserved because of the perfection inherent in them, just as the body is preserved by food because of its goodness. As it is written: *Come, buy... and eat* <note>original: "veichlu"</note>. <margin>Isaiah 55:1.</margin> Likewise, a little later: *Listen diligently to me, and eat* <note>original: "veichlu"</note> *what is good*. <margin>Ibid. v. 2.</margin> Likewise: *To eat* <note>original: "Achol"</note> *much honey is not good*. <margin>Proverbs 25:27.</margin> Item: *My son, eat* <note>original: "Achol"</note> *honey, because it is good, and the honeycomb, because it is sweet to your palate*. <margin>Proverbs 24:13.</margin> And so it often happens in the words of our Rabbis of pious memory that they call Wisdom "eating," as when they say: *Come, eat meat in which there is much fat*. They also say: *Every instance of "Eating and Drinking" mentioned in this book* (meaning the Book of Proverbs) *is nothing other than Wisdom, or the Law* <note>original: "Lex," referring to the Torah</note>, as other manuscripts have it. For in this same way, <margin>Wisdom is called Water.</margin> Wisdom is also frequently called Water: as in, *All you who thirst, come to the waters*. <margin>Isaiah 55:1.</margin> And because this usage occurs so frequently, and is so manifest and clear—as if it were the first and proper meaning of this word—therefore "Hunger" and "Thirst" are also used to signify the <margin>The deprivation of Wisdom is called Hunger and Thirst.</margin> Deprivation of Wisdom and Apprehension: as in, *And I will send a famine into the land, not a famine of bread, nor a thirst for water, but of hearing the Word of the LORD*. <margin>Amos 8:11.</margin> Item: *My soul thirsts for God, for the living God*. <margin>Psalm 42:3.</margin> And so on throughout. Thus Jonathan <note>Jonathan ben Uzziel, the traditional author of the Aramaic translation (Targum) of the Prophets</note> interprets that passage, *And you shall draw waters from the fountains of salvation*, in this sense: *And you shall receive new doctrine with joy from the chosen of the righteous*. Here, observe how he explains "Water" as the Wisdom to come in those days; however, he took the word *Mimmaajene* <note>Hebrew: מעייני</note> "From the fountains," as if it were the same as *Meene* <note>Hebrew: מעיני</note> in that passage of Moses: *Meene haedah* <margin>Numbers 15:24.</margin>, that is, "From the heads" or the leaders of the assembly. These leaders are indeed the Wise. Furthermore, when he says, *From the chosen of the Righteous*, he does so because true Justice is ultimately salvation. You see, therefore, how he has adapted each individual word to Wisdom and Doctrine.

<margin>The apprehension of the Intellect is imperfect.</margin>

<meta>This page continues from Chapter 31, which discusses the inherent limitations of human knowledge. The previous page established that the human mind has a specific structure and capacity, and this page expands on the idea that there are boundaries to what we can know, comparing intellectual limits to physical and sensory limits.</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

...certain things which, by its own nature, [the intellect] can in no way grasp, not even through a cause (that is, through singular grace and Divine influence) <note>Maimonides suggests that some mysteries are inherently beyond human capacity, while others might only be revealed by God</note>, but the gates to grasping them are closed and barred from every side. There are also certain things of which it grasps and attains a part, while remaining ignorant of another part. For although it possesses the faculty of Understanding, it does not follow that it can understand everything; just as the senses indeed perceive, but not at every distance. So it is with the rest of the bodily faculties: for example, if someone can carry two talents <note>A talent was a heavy ancient unit of weight; Maimonides uses this to illustrate that every power has a maximum capacity</note>, it does not follow that he can carry ten.

Just as there is such a difference among individuals that one surpasses another in these sensory perceptions and other bodily faculties—as is known to everyone—yet they still have a certain limit and do not proceed into the immense or the infinite; so too the judgment of human intellectual understandings is the same. For here also there is great difference and inequality, and one person is much more excellent than another, as can be seen especially among the learned. For there is one who, by himself, discovers a certain thing through his own contemplation, which another can never understand; so that even if the other person wishes to teach him with every effort, illustrating it with whatever examples, and over a very long time, his intellect can never reach it, but the "hand of his intellect" is too short <note>original: "abbreviata sit manus intellectus," a biblical idiom meaning one's power or reach is limited</note> to understand it. Truly, even this excellence does not proceed to infinity: the human intellect also has its barriers <note>original: "cancellos," literally lattice-work or railings that enclose a space</note>, within which it must remain.

<margin>
*Individuals differ in their capacity for understanding.*
</margin>

For there are certain things which a man well knows are impossible for him to grasp, and thus his soul does not even long to know and recognize them, because he judges the matter impossible for himself and knows there is no gate through which he might enter into the knowledge of them. Thus we do not know the number of the stars in the Heaven, whether it be even or odd; likewise the number of species of animals, minerals, plants, and similar things. Then there are certain things for which the appetite and desire of man is very intense, in the investigation and examination of which his intellect strains all its nerves. Hence among all nations and at all times, a great crowd of men is found devoted to such contemplations. And here their opinions are very diverse and disagreeing, from which many controversies and questions arise: because the intellect hangs upon the grasping of them (that is, in the desire to grasp them), and so each one thinks he has found the way through which he may know their nature and truth. Nor is it even in the power of the intellect...

<term>The Guide for the Perplexed</term> <term>Maimonides</term> <term>Apprehension</term> <term>Human Intellect</term> <term>Individuals</term> <term>Contemplation</term>

<summary>This page argues that human intelligence has fixed boundaries, much like physical strength or eyesight, and that some truths are permanently inaccessible to us. Maimonides notes that while individuals vary in their intellectual reach, everyone eventually hits a "wall" regarding certain celestial or natural mysteries.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 31 of Part I. The previous page discussed how the human intellect has inherent limits, just as physical strength or the senses do. It ended with the observation that even with great effort and examples, some truths remain beyond certain individuals. This page picks up mid-word with the conclusion that the intellect’s role is to provide proof for what can be known, and explores why people disagree even on provable truths.</meta>

### Part I. Chapter XXXI.

...of the human [intellect] <note>The Latin text picks up mid-word with "ctus," likely completing "intellectus" (intellect) from the previous page.</note> to provide a demonstration of those things. For regarding anything that can be proven by demonstration, there is no controversy or disagreement; no one opposes or contradicts such things except the ignorant and the unskilled. Such opposition is called "demonstrative contradiction" (that is, acting against things that are capable of being proven). Thus, one finds men who stir up controversy regarding the roundness of the Earth, the Heavens, and similar matters. Truly, such people have no entry or standing in this business. This type of confusion, however, happens most especially and most frequently in Divine matters; it happens more rarely in the Natural Sciences, and in Mathematics it is almost entirely absent.

<margin>The causes of disagreements and controversies.</margin>
**Alexander of Aphrodisias** <note>A famous Greek commentator on Aristotle who lived around 200 AD; Maimonides frequently cites him.</note> wrote that there are three causes of Disagreements and Controversies: **first**, the Love of dominance and victory—which he called <term>philonikeia</term> <note>original: "φιλονεικίαν," meaning a love of quarreling or a desire to be right at any cost</note>—for this prevents a man from grasping a thing as it truly is; **second**, the Subtlety, depth, and difficulty of the thing to be grasped; **third**, the Ignorance and lack of skill of the one trying to understand, as well as his own Imperfection and Weakness in grasping a matter that is otherwise possible to understand.

Alexander indeed recorded these causes. But in these times of ours, we can add a **fourth** cause which he omitted: namely, Habit or Education. For men by nature love <margin>How great is the force of Habit.</margin> that to which they have been accustomed, and they lean toward it. Hence you see that those who live in rural villages—because they are not used to washing their heads or bodies, nor indulging in pleasures or fine foods—despise the cities. They do not desire to enjoy the city's pleasures, but rather choose the bad things they are used to over the good things they are not. They have no great desire to live in magnificent palaces, to be clothed in silk and precious garments, or to delight themselves with baths, ointments, or spices. 

Plainly, the same thing happens to a person regarding the thoughts and opinions in which he was raised; out of love for them, he cannot be moved away from them. This is the reason why a man often cannot grasp the truth: because he follows those things to which he is accustomed. This happens to the common people regarding <term>corporeality</term> <note>The mistaken belief that God has a physical body or human-like form.</note> and other Divine matters, as we shall declare elsewhere. For all these errors happen to them chiefly because they have been trained and educated in writings that are held in high esteem among them, which teach about corporeality and other imaginings according to the literal sense—things in which there is no truth. However, these texts must be understood <term>parabolically</term> <note>As metaphors or allegories rather than literal facts.</note>, for reasons I shall provide elsewhere. Furthermore, you should not think that these things we have said regarding the weakness and imperfection of the human Intellect...

<meta>The text ends with the catchword "nobis" (by us), indicating the start of the next page.</meta>

<summary>Maimonides examines why people disagree on truth, citing Alexander of Aphrodisias's three causes (ego, difficulty of subject, and lack of skill) and adding a fourth: the powerful influence of habit and traditional education.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 31 and begins Chapter 32 of Part I. The previous page discussed the causes of disagreement in human knowledge, particularly in divine matters, noting that the intellect has limits similar to physical senses.</meta>

...was said by us, and that a certain and defined limit has been prescribed for [the intellect] (or, *concerning its limitation*), is not said by our Law alone. For the Philosophers also established and grasped this very point with perfect understanding, without any hesitation or doubt; for indeed, the matter is placed beyond any risk of doubt, except perhaps for one who is plainly ignorant of those things for which evident proofs <note>original: "demonstrationes," referring to logical or mathematical certainties</note> are held. Regarding this chapter, we have provided this as a sort of foundation for what follows.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

### *That failure and exhaustion occur in Intellectual matters just as they do in Sensual ones. It is demonstrated by reason and the testimony of Scripture that matters higher than what one's intellect can bear should not be investigated.*

<margin>Exhaustion happens to the Intellect just as to the senses.</margin>
Let the reader know that in Intellectual Apprehensions <note>The mental act of "grasping" or understanding a concept.</note> (because they depend upon matter), something similar happens to that which occurs in sensory perceptions. For when you see with your eyes, you grasp that which is within the power of your sight to perceive. But if you force your eyes and strain your vision too much—to see across a greater distance than your power of sight allows, or when you gaze upon writing or a carving too fine for your visual faculty to see—then your sight will be weakened, not only regarding what you cannot see at all, but even regarding that which you could otherwise see. Thus, you will no longer be able to see what you were able to see before you strained and exhausted your sight in this way. 

Anyone engaged in the contemplation of some science or art that consists of thought will experience the same thing. For when they have strained and exhausted their thoughts too much upon it, they will become confused and will not understand that which they could have understood otherwise. Indeed, the rule for all bodily faculties and powers in this matter is one and the same. Something similar happens to you in Intellectual Apprehensions. For if you stop at the doubt you have encountered, and do not afflict your soul by trying to persuade it that there is a proof <note>original: "demonstratio"</note> for a thing where no proof exists, nor attempt to reject or prove as false that which cannot be logically refuted <note>The sentence ends abruptly due to the page break; the catchword "mon-" indicates the word "demonstrari" (demonstrated/proven) follows.</note>

<summary>Maimonides concludes that the intellect's limits are recognized by both religious law and philosophy, then begins a new chapter comparing mental strain to physical eye strain—arguing that overextending the mind's capacity can damage one's ability to understand even simple truths.</summary>
<keywords>Intellectual Apprehension, mental exhaustion, limits of reason, visual analogy, Maimonides, logic, philosophy vs law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 32 of Part I regarding the limitations of the human intellect. It contrasts the success of Rabbi Akiva, who respected these limits, with the failure of Elisha ben Abuyah, who overstepped them.</meta>

### Part I. Chapter XXXII.

...can be shown, nor do you desire to grasp that which exceeds your capacity: when, I say, you have observed all these things, you have reached the summit of human perfection, and you will be at the rank of Rabbi Akiva <note>original: "R. Akibhæ"</note> of blessed memory <note>original: "p. m." (piae memoriae)</note>, who "entered and exited in peace" <note>A famous Talmudic reference to Akiva's safe journey into mystical speculation ("the Orchard") compared to his colleagues.</note> regarding these Divine matters in his contemplation. 

But if you attempt to grasp that which exceeds your comprehension, or begin to refute as false those things for which there is no proof <note>original: "demonstratio," meaning a logical or certain certainty</note> of their opposites—even if they are in no way probable or possible—then you will become like a certain Elisha <note>Refers to Elisha ben Abuyah, a sage who became an apostate after engaging in mystical speculation beyond his capacity.</note>: that is, not only will you not be perfect, but you will be more imperfect than all imperfection. And it will happen that imaginary things will dominate you, and you will incline toward vices and all manner of base and evil things, because of the excessive distress and exhaustion with which you afflict the Intellect in this way, and so you extinguish its light. 

This happens in no other way than occurs in the sense of Sight, which often perceives many false appearances when it is weakened—as can be seen in the sick—or when the sharp focus of the eyes is directed toward things too bright and shining, or too subtle.

<margin>*The Intellect must not be exhausted by those things which exceed its grasp.*</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 25:16</margin>
In this sense Solomon says, *If you have found honey, eat only what is sufficient for you, lest you be filled with it and vomit it up*, an example of which they relate concerning a certain Elisha. This is a truly excellent comparison, in which he compares Wisdom to food (as we have also warned before), and indeed to the sweetest food, namely honey. Its nature is such that when eaten in great quantity, it disturbs the stomach and produces vomiting. It is as if Solomon wished to say: this Apprehension <note>The act of the mind "grasping" a concept.</note> is indeed excellent, remarkable, and a most perfect thing, but if one does not stay within its limits, and if one does not move within it cautiously and prudently, it degenerates into imperfection, just as happens in the eating of honey. For if it is taken moderately and temperately, it nourishes and is sweet; but if one exceeds the limit and eats too much, the opposite happens. 

Observe, however, that he does not say, *Lest you be filled with it and loathing of it take you*, or, *and you despise it*, but, *And you vomit it up*. In the same sense he says elsewhere, *To eat much honey is not good*.

<margin>*Wisdom is compared with honey.*</margin>
<margin>Ibid. verse 27</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiastes 7:16 <note>The Latin text cites verse 17, but it is 7:16 in modern bibles.</note></margin>
<margin>Ecclesiastes 5:1 <note>The Latin text cites 4:17.</note></margin>
<margin>Psalm 131:1</margin>
He also refers to this when he says: *Do not be overly wise; why should you destroy yourself?* So also when he says, *Guard your foot when you go to the house of God*. Hence David also says, *I have not walked in great matters, nor in wonders above me*. 

Our Sages mean the same thing when they say, *Do not seek into things that are too high for you, and do not search into things hidden from you.* Likewise, *Think on those things which are commanded to you; for you have no need of those things which are secret.* That is: Do not send forth your intellect except to that which a human can grasp and attain; for to exercise oneself in those things which exceed one's intellect is extremely harmful, as we have shown. They also hint at the same when they say: *Whoever looks into four things, etc.* <note>A reference to Mishnah Hagigah 2:1, which warns against speculating on what is "above, below, before, or after" the world.</note> Especially...

<margin>*These sayings are also read in Sirach, chapter 3, verses 20 and 21.*</margin>

<summary>This page warns that the human intellect, like physical sight, has limits; pushing beyond these boundaries leads to mental exhaustion, error, and moral decay. It uses the biblical metaphor of honey to explain that while wisdom is sweet, an excess of it beyond one's capacity is harmful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the limits of the human intellect from Chapter 32. It addresses the ethical and practical consequences of intellectual overreach and begins Chapter 33, which outlines the proper sequence of study for those approaching divine science.</meta>

# GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

...but truly in that saying: *Whoever does not have regard for the glory of his Creator, it would be better for him had he not come into the world* <note>original: "Quicunq; non parcit gloriæ Creatoris sui, (expediret ei, ut non venisset in mundum...)" — This is a well-known passage from the Mishnah (Chagigah 2:1) cautioning against improper mystical speculation.</note> <insert>one should add: as is clear from the Commentary of Rabbi Shem Tov</insert> <note>Shem Tov ibn Shem Tov (c. 1380–1441) was a Spanish rabbi and kabbalist known for his defense of traditional Jewish thought against radical philosophy.</note>. For they intended nothing else than what we have said: that a human being should not rush forward with his thoughts toward those matters that bring him ruin. Indeed, when certain doubts arise within him, or he does not immediately find a logical proof for the thing he seeks, he should not at once abandon or reject it, or pronounce it false; rather, he should wait and have regard for the glory of his Creator, and restrain himself, just as this has already been explained. 

However, it is not the mind or intention of those Prophets and Sages of ours to close the gates of all speculation, or to bar the intellect from grasping those things which it *is* capable of grasping—as certain foolish and lazy men believe. Such people think they can quite handsomely hide their own unskillfulness and ignorance under the veil of "perfection and wisdom." On the contrary, they brand true perfection and knowledge with the mark of imperfection, 

<margin>Isaiah 5:20.</margin> and so they wander and stray from the path of the Law, *putting darkness for light and light for darkness.* Indeed, the intention is to show that the human intellect has a certain limit at which it must stop. However, it is not necessary for you to examine our words, which we have spoken in this chapter about the human intellect, with too much subtlety or precision; for we only wished to direct them toward our specific goal, and not to explain the nature of the Intellect <term>precisely <note>original Greek: ἀκριβῶς (akribōs), meaning "accurately" or "to the letter."</note></term>.

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

->*That the course of studies should not begin with Theology itself, but that the mind must first be exercised in other arts and disciplines. Why they speak of Divine matters through riddles, and why they are likewise called Secrets.*<-

<margin>One must approach the study of Theology through certain stages.</margin> I would have you know that in teaching or learning, it is extremely dangerous and harmful to begin immediately with Divine Wisdom <note>Theology or Metaphysics.</note>, and with the exposition of the Prophetic Parables, Figures, and Metaphors. For it is necessary that younger students or beginners, and those who are of a slower disposition, be first instructed in the primary elements according to the measure of their own understanding. But if someone is observed to be of a more perfect Intellect, and has reached that sublime and lofty rank...

<summary>Maimonides warns against using religious warnings as an excuse for ignorance and asserts that the human intellect has boundaries. He then begins Chapter 33, arguing that theology must be studied only after a rigorous foundation in other disciplines.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 33 regarding the gradual education required to understand divine matters. It uses the analogy of feeding an infant to explain why the deepest truths are hidden from those not yet ready for them.</meta>

->### PART I. CHAPTER XXXIII.<-

->F 2<-

...until he is fit and suitable for that level, I say, of demonstrative speculation and of truly intellectual doctrines; they will make him ascend little by little until he reaches his perfection, either by his own industry or by the help of some teacher. For if someone were to begin with this Divine Wisdom <note>original: "Divina Sapientia" — referring here to Metaphysics, the study of the highest causes and God.</note> immediately, there would arise in him not only a confusion of the things to be believed, but a total destruction of them. Nor does such a person seem to me <margin>An analogy.</margin> to act any differently than if one wished to feed an infant with wheaten bread and meat, and to give it wine to drink. For without doubt, he would kill it—not because those foods are bad, or contrary or unsuitable to human nature, but because the one receiving them is too weak to digest them in a manner that would allow him to perceive any benefit from them. Thus, as far as those true and real sciences and opinions are concerned, <margin>Why the Sages speak of Divine matters secretly and through riddles.</margin> the Sages have not spoken of them so secretly, enigmatically, and obscurely because some evil lies hidden beneath them, or because they destroy the foundations of our Law (as foolish men imagine); rather, they hid them because our natural talent and intellect are unable to grasp them immediately from the beginning. However, they revealed certain parts so that those who are more perfect might learn them, and for this reason they are called the *Secrets of the Law*, as we shall explain.

<margin>Why the Law speaks through anthropomorphisms. Chapters 26 and 47.</margin> And this is also the reason why the Law speaks in the "Language of the sons of men," <note>original: "Lingua filiorum hominum" — a translation of the Hebrew principle *Dibra torah kileshon bnei adam*, meaning the Torah uses human-like descriptions of God so the average person can understand.</note> as we have explained, because this method is convenient and easy, so that children, women, and the common folk—in whose power it is not to apprehend things according to their actual truth—may begin and learn from it. For this reason, all things that must be believed were sufficiently handed down to them in this manner, as was every concept that can direct the intellect toward knowing that God is and exists, even if not to the truth of His <term>quiddity</term> <note>The "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing; its definition.</note>. But if someone is more perfect and of a loftier talent, so that he has knowledge of the Secrets of the Law—whether he learned them from another or from himself, being stirred from one point to another—he will reach that level where he believes those true and real meanings (concerning God) through the paths of true faith (not like the common people through analogies drawn from men). That is, he will believe either through demonstration in those matters where a demonstration can be had, or through other firm and solid arguments where that is possible; and thus he will formally know and understand the truth and substance of those things which were handed down to him through analogies and parables. Furthermore, we have already cited many times in this book that well-worn saying of our Rabbis: *And in the Chariot <note>original: "Mercava" — referring to Ma'aseh Merkabah, the "Work of the Chariot," the highest and most restricted form of Jewish mystical and metaphysical speculation based on Ezekiel's vision.</note> they shall not instruct even a single person, unless he be wise and understanding from his own mind*, and even then they only hand over to him the main chapter headings. Therefore, one must not begin—

<summary>Maimonides explains that divine truths are hidden in metaphors and "secrets" because the human intellect requires gradual training, just as an infant cannot digest solid food. He defends the use of anthropomorphisms in the Law as a necessary educational tool for the common person.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 32, which discusses the boundaries of human reason, and begins Chapter 33. The previous page transition mentions the pedagogical dangers of teaching metaphysics to the unprepared; here, Maimonides emphasizes that while the intellect has limits, this should not be used as an excuse for ignorance.</meta>

...but indeed regarding that saying: *Whoever does not spare the glory of his Creator, (it would be better for him if he had not come into the world; this should be added, as is clear from the Commentary of Rabbi Shem Tov).* <note>original: "Quicunq; non parcit gloriæ Creatoris sui..." — This is a reference to a passage in the Mishnah (Hagigah 2:1) cautioning against speculating on matters beyond human comprehension.</note> For they mean nothing else by this than what we have said: that a man must not break forth with his thoughts toward those kinds of things which bring destruction upon him. But when certain doubts arise in him, or if he does not immediately find the demonstration of the thing he seeks, he should not then instantly abandon or reject it, or pronounce it false; rather, he should wait, spare the glory of his Creator, and restrain himself, just as this has already been explained. 

However, the mind and meaning of those sayings of the Prophets and our Sages is not to close the gates of all speculation, or to keep the intellect away from the apprehension of those things which it *can* grasp—as certain foolish and lazy men think. These people very cleverly think they can cover their own unskillfulness and ignorance with the veil of "perfection" and "wisdom" in this way; on the contrary, they brand perfection and knowledge as if they were imperfections, and they step off and wander away from the path of the Law, *putting darkness for light and light for darkness.* <margin>Isaiah 5:20.</margin> 

But the intention is to indicate that the human intellect has a certain limit at which it must stop. However, you should not examine our words which we have spoken in this chapter about the human intellect too subtly or accurately; for we only wanted to direct them toward our goal, and not to explain the nature of the Intellect with precision <note>original Greek: "ἀκριβῶς" (akribōs) — meaning with exactness or mathematical rigor.</note>.

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

->*That the course of studies must not begin with Theology itself, but the mind must first be exercised in other arts and disciplines. Why they speak of Divine matters through riddles, and why they are likewise called Secrets.*<-

<margin>That the study of Theology must be approached through certain steps.</margin>
I would have you know that in teaching or learning, to begin immediately with Divine Wisdom <note>original: "Sapientia Divina" — referring to Metaphysics, the highest branch of philosophy dealing with the nature of God and existence.</note>, and with the exposition of the Prophetic Parables, Tropes, and Metaphors, is extremely dangerous and harmful. For it is necessary that first the youths or beginners <note>original: "Tyrones" — a Latin term for recruits or novices.</note>, and those who are of a slower disposition, be instructed in the first principles, according to the measure of their own understanding. But if anyone is observed to be of a more perfect intellect, and [fit] for that sublime and exalted level—

<summary>Maimonides warns that the limitations of human reason should not be used as a shield for ignorance, but as a guide for restraint. He begins Chapter 33 by arguing that students must study preparatory disciplines before attempting the "Secrets of the Law" or Metaphysics.</summary>

### PART I. CHAPTER XXXIII.

F 2

...while fit and suitable, to that level, I say, of demonstrative speculation and of truly intellectual doctrines, they will make him ascend little by little, until he reaches his perfection either by his own industry or by the help of some teacher. For without doubt he would kill him, not because those foods are bad, or contrary or unsuitable to the nature of man, but because the one receiving them is too weak to digest them in such a way that he might derive benefit from them.

<margin>An Analogy.</margin>
<margin>Why the Sages speak secretly and enigmatically about Divine matters.</margin>
Thus, regarding those sciences and true and real opinions, the <term>Sages</term> <note>The ancient Jewish scholars and teachers of the Talmud.</note> have not spoken so secretly, enigmatically, and obscurely about them because something evil lies hidden under them, or because they destroy the foundations of our Law (as foolish men think); rather, they have hidden them because our natural talent and intellect are unable to grasp them immediately from the beginning. However, they revealed certain things so that those who are more perfected might learn them, and hence these are called the **Secrets of the Law**, just as we shall explain.

<margin>Why the Law speaks through anthropopathisms. Chapters 26 and 47.</margin>
And this is also the reason why the Law speaks in the **Language of the children of men** <note>original: "Linguâ filiorum hominum" — This translates the Hebrew principle "Dibrah Torah ki-leshon benei adam," meaning the Bible uses human metaphors so everyone can understand it.</note>, as we have explained, because this method is convenient and easy, so that children, women, and the common populace may begin and learn from it, for it is not within their power to apprehend things according to their actual truth. That is, he reaches it either through demonstration in those matters where demonstration can be had, or through other firm and solid arguments where that is possible, and thus he formally knows and understands the truth and substance of those things which were handed down to him through similes and parables. Furthermore, we have many times already in this book cited that well-worn saying of our Rabbis: *And concerning the <term>Mercava</term>, they shall not instruct even one person, unless he be wise and understanding from his own mind,* <note>original: "Et in Mercava..." — This refers to the "Work of the Chariot" (Ma'aseh Merkavah), the mystical and philosophical study of Ezekiel's vision, which Maimonides equates with Metaphysics.</note> and then they shall hand over to him the main and principal chapters. ...be dealt with by a person in this business, except according to the measure of his grasp, and with these two conditions: *first*, if he is wise, that is, if he has been imbued with those sciences from which the principles of <term>speculation</term> <note>original: "speculationis" — In this context, it refers to rigorous philosophical investigation or logical reasoning.</note> are drawn; *second*, if he is intelligent, of a clear and sharp mind, who can perceive a matter even when lightly indicated—which is what they <note>The "they" refers to the Sages of the Talmudic tradition.</note> mean when they say, *And understanding from his own mind*. ### CHAPTER XXXIV.
### *Concerning the causes for which one should not begin with Divine Wisdom in learning or teaching.*

<margin>Reasons why the course of studies should not begin with Theology.</margin>There are FIVE causes that hinder and prevent studies from beginning with <term>Divine matters</term> <note>original: "rebus Divinis" — Referring to Metaphysics, the study of God and the first causes.</note>, and prevent anyone from showing or revealing to the common people or the masses those things that can be known and understood about them.

<margin>1. Depth and difficulty. Ecclesiastes 7:24. Job 28:12.</margin>The first cause is the difficulty, subtlety, and depth of the Matter itself, about which Solomon speaks thus: *That which is far off and exceeding deep, who can find it out?* <note>Ecclesiastes 7:24</note> Likewise Job: *But where shall wisdom be found?* <note>Job 28:12</note> Now, it is indeed backwards in learning to begin <margin>One must not begin with the more difficult things.</margin>from the more difficult and more hidden things. Hence we also find in the <term>Parables of our Law</term> <note>The allegorical sections of the Torah or the Hebrew Bible.</note> that Wisdom is compared to Water, which our Sages, among other things, also explained in this way: He who is skilled in the art of swimming can bring forth pearls from the depth of the sea; but he who is unskilled in it remains submerged in the deep. Therefore, no one should attempt it except one who is practiced in that art.

<margin>2. The incapacity of the human mind.</margin>The second cause is the briefness and narrowness of the knowledge of all men from the beginning: because the ultimate perfection (<note>original: "Intellectus sc." — namely, of the Intellect.</note>) is not given to a man from the start, but is first in him only as a <term>potential</term> <note>original: "potentiâ" — A philosophical term for the capacity to become something that is not yet realized.</note>, deprived of all <margin>Job 11:12.</margin>action. For, *man is born like a wild ass's colt*. <note>Job 11:12</note> Nor does it follow, if something exists in someone as a potential, that it must necessarily be brought into action. For it is possible that it remains in its imperfection, and this either because of certain external obstacles, or because of the scarcity and interruption of study and effort in those matters through which that potential could...

<summary>Maimonides begins Chapter 34 by listing why metaphysics (Divine Wisdom) should not be taught to beginners, focusing on the subject's inherent difficulty and the fact that human intellectual perfection is a potential that must be developed over time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 34 of Part I, which details the reasons why metaphysical study (the "Divine Wisdom") must be delayed until a student is sufficiently prepared. ...be brought into <term>actualization</term> <note>original: "in actum deduci" — The philosophical transition from a potential ability to a realized, active state of being.</note>. Hence it is expressly said: *Not many are wise.* And from the book of Job (32:9), the saying of our <term>Rabbis</term> <note>original: "Rabbinorum nostrorum" — Referring to the Sages of the Jewish Talmudic tradition.</note> is relevant: *I saw the sons of the upper chamber, and they were few.* <note>original: "Vidi filios cœnaculi, & erant pauci" — A reference to a Talmudic statement (Sukkah 45b) regarding the rare individuals who attain the highest level of spiritual and intellectual clarity.</note> For there are very many things that keep us away and hold us back from perfection. Furthermore, when will a person have prepared themselves sufficiently, and when will they have enough leisure to learn everything that must be learned, so that those capacities which exist within them as potential may be brought into action?

<margin>3. The vast number of preliminary requirements.</margin>
The third cause is the lengthiness and the multitude of the preliminary subjects with which a person must be equipped. For although humans are naturally held by a desire to know ultimate purposes, it often happens that weariness overcomes them, and they abandon the required preliminary knowledge. However, if anyone were able to reach such a goal or perfection without the preceding knowledge of those subjects, then they would not be "preliminary" at all, but rather empty exhaustion and mere superfluity. 

For every person, no matter how simple or foolish, is so constituted that if you wake them from sleep and ask them: do they not desire to know the nature, the number, the form and shape of the heavens? What are the angels? By what means was the world created? For what purpose were its individual parts so ordered and arranged? What is the soul? From where does it draw its origin? Is it separated from the body? And if it is separated, how is it separated, and where does it go? and similar questions—they will undoubtedly answer you that they naturally desire a true and solid knowledge of these things. 

Yet, they will want this desire to be satisfied peacefully, without labor or trouble, and for this knowledge to be instilled in them in just one or two words. For if you wanted to require of them that they set aside their business even for a single week so that they could learn and understand all these things, they would not do it for the world; rather, they would rest in their own false imaginings and scorn what is told to them about the vast number of things that must be known beforehand, and likewise about the length of time required for them. 

But you know that all these things are most closely connected and bound together. For all things that exist are either God Himself or the works of God—which include everything other than God. Now, there is no other way of knowing God except through His works. These teach that God exists, and they provide the basis for everything that must be believed concerning Him—that is, what ought to be affirmed or denied about Him. Therefore, it necessarily follows that one must properly understand all <term>Beings</term> <note>original: "Entia" — Refers to all existing things or entities in the created order.</note> and their nature, so that from every species of thing we can derive certain and necessary principles that may serve us in investigating the nature of God. For think how many principles are derived from the nature of Number (that is, from...

<summary>Maimonides explains that the third reason metaphysical study is delayed is the immense amount of prerequisite knowledge required. He notes that while everyone naturally desires answers to deep questions about the universe and the soul, few are willing to undergo the long, rigorous scientific study necessary to reach those truths.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Part I, Chapter 34 of the "Guide for the Perplexed." Having established that metaphysical study is difficult and that human capacity is limited, the author is now explaining the third reason: the necessity of mastering several preliminary sciences—such as mathematics, logic, and physics—before one can safely approach divine knowledge.</meta>

-> **GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED** <-
<meta>catchword: God</meta>

(*Arithmetic*) and from the properties of Shapes in Geometry, which instruct us about many things that must be removed from the Creator <note>original: "removeri debent à Creatore" — This refers to 'negative theology,' the idea that we understand God better by realizing what He is not (e.g., God is not physical, not finite).</note>, and whose removal further teaches us about other things? As far as the science of Astronomy and natural things are concerned, I do not see how they can be doubted: for these are necessary matters for understanding the arrangement and structure of the World, and thus they serve to help us understand how God truly—not according to our vain imaginations and conceptions—rules and governs the World. There are besides certain other Theoretical or Speculative Sciences, (*such as Logic and Metaphysics*), from which, although the principles for this Science or Wisdom are not sought, they nevertheless exercise the Intellect, and prepare it with the habit of making demonstrations, and of understanding the nature of those things which are demonstrated from the truth of the matter; and furthermore, they remove many errors found in the opinions of those who speculate, so that **accidental** <note>secondary or non-essential qualities</note> things are not confused with **essential** <note>the core nature of a thing</note> things, along with other absurd opinions that arise from such confusion. To all these it must yet be added that they lead a person to a formal knowledge of things as they actually are. And although these are not the foundations of Divine Wisdom: nevertheless, they exhibit a necessary use in other matters which make us approach that Wisdom. Therefore, since these things are so, everyone who wishes to achieve human perfection must first be instructed in the art of Logic, then in order in the Mathematical disciplines, (*namely Arithmetic, Geometry, Astronomy, etc.* for here he understands these by the term <term>propedeutic sciences</term> <note>original: "Chochmoth limmudijoth" — Hebrew for the introductory or mathematical sciences required before higher study.</note>), afterward in Natural science, and finally in Divine matters. Now, however, we find many whose intellect grows weary and fails in learning these arts and disciplines: and even if it does not fail, it can happen that they are consumed by death before they have a perfect knowledge of them. For this reason, unless some Science were given to us through <term>Tradition</term> <note>original: "Kabbalam" — Used here in the literal sense of 'received tradition' or transmission of knowledge, rather than the later specific school of Jewish mysticism.</note>, and if we were not led to this matter by examples and parables—but if instead everything had to be understood and learned by us formally through the essential definitions of things, and nothing were to be believed except through demonstrations—then surely, because this can only be done through long preparations, the greater part of humanity would meet their end before they knew whether God exists or not; to say nothing of what should be affirmed, denied, or removed [as a quality] regarding Him. Nor would they be rescued from such a premature death [of the soul], except perhaps *one from a city, and two from a family*, that is, very few (*a proverbial expression common in the writings of the Rabbis*). As for others, whom...

<summary>This page argues that a rigorous education in logic, mathematics, and natural sciences is a prerequisite for understanding God, though such a path is so long that most people rely on tradition and parables to grasp these truths.</summary>

# Part I. Chapter XXXIV.

<margin>Jeremiah 3:14</margin>
God calls them the **Remnants** <note>original: "Seridim" — survivors or those left behind</note>, that is, the **Survivors** or the **Remaining**, who are, as it were, exempt from the common lot of men; the highest and ultimate perfection does not belong to them except after the knowledge of these prerequisites. Solomon himself, the wisest of all mortals, recognized how necessary these principles are and showed that it is impossible for anyone to reach this wisdom without first undertaking legitimate training in them, when he said: <margin>Ecclesiastes 10:10</margin> *If the iron be blunt, and its edge not polished, how shall it overcome armies or strengths?* (That is: If the edge of some knife or sword is not sharp, and its surface is not well-polished, it cannot overcome or cut through hard things; rather, it is necessary that it first be sharpened and polished.) *And rectification* (that is, industry or preparation) *is much more required for wisdom* (specifically, for acquiring it). Likewise: <margin>Proverbs 19:20</margin> *Accept discipline, so that you may become wise in your latter end.*

Furthermore, there is yet another necessity for which these principles must be known and learned, namely this: while a man is studying, many doubts constantly arise within him; he even learns the contradictions to a certain opinion and those things which refute it more quickly than he learns its confirmation—just as it is easier to destroy a house than to build one. The confirmation of assertions and the solutions to doubts mostly flow from various principles which are drawn from those prerequisites. Therefore, he who speculates without them is like a man <margin>An analogy.</margin> walking and striving toward a certain place on his own feet who falls into an extremely deep pit, from which he has no power or plan to escape until he dies. For such a man, it would have been far better if he had abandoned that journey and remained quietly in his place. Solomon, in the Proverbs, described these characteristics of the lazy and their laziness with great elegance and in vivid colors. For the whole parable is about the lazy man seeking wisdom. He speaks there of the desire of him who longs for knowledge of the ultimate goals, and only desires it, spending no effort on learning the prerequisites that lead there: <margin>Proverbs 21:25-26</margin> *The desire of the sluggard kills him, because his hands refuse to do anything. All day long he craves with craving; but the righteous gives and does not hold back.* The reason why his desire kills him, he says, is that he is not concerned with that which could satisfy his desire; but that he only indulges his longing and does nothing else besides, hoping that he will reach a place where no means or instruments remain for him to reach. From this, if he had abandoned his desire entirely, it would have been much better for him. But observe how

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

the end of this parable declares its beginning, when it is said: *The righteous man truly gives and does not withhold.* <note>original: "Justus verò tribuit, nec cohibet" — referring to Proverbs 21:26.</note> For the righteous man is contrasted with the lazy man for no other reason than that the righteous man—as we have explained elsewhere—gives to every matter what is its own. That is, he spends his whole time on his studies and does not take anything away from them to give to other business. It is as if he had said: the righteous man gives all his days to wisdom and does not subtract from them; just as it says in a similar <margin>Proverbs 31:3</margin> sense elsewhere: *Do not give your strength to women* <note>original: "Ne des mulieribus robur tuum"</note> (continually, that is, and all your strength). Yet many very famous and distinguished men suffer from this disease: they wish to first inquire into and investigate the ultimate goals, and to discourse upon them, without a preliminary study <note>original: "protheoria"</note> and a prior knowledge of the required principles. Among them are some who, out of ignorance or pride, disparage those principles (either because they cannot understand them, or because they are too lazy and sluggish to learn them), and they attempt to demonstrate and prove that those principles are highly dangerous and useless. But the truth is clear to the person of understanding.

<margin>4. Natural Disposition or Constitution.</margin> The fourth reason is *Natural Disposition*. Regarding this, it has been demonstrated that moral qualities <note>the character and temperament of a person</note> are preparations for the rational qualities. It is impossible for rational things to be true—that is, for the intelligible faculties (the active powers of the <margin>Not a few things he says here seem to taste of Pelagianism, and therefore should be read with judgment. <note>The marginal commentator is warning the reader about Pelagianism, a 5th-century heresy that emphasized human free will over divine grace. This suggests the Latin translator or editor was a Christian scholar.</note></margin> **Intellect**) to be perfect—except in one who is endowed with good, gentle, and constant morals. However, there are some who from the beginning of their birth have acquired a temperament <note>term: temperament — in medieval medicine, the balance of the four "humors" (fluids) in the body which determined personality and health</note> with which perfection can in no way coexist. For example: someone who has a violently hot and strong heart cannot place a bridle on his anger, even if he applies the greatest practice. So too, one who is of a hot and moist nature and of a robust constitution will very rarely fear sin, whatever remedy he may wish to apply to this evil. Thus you will find some who are fickle and restless, whose disordered and disturbed movements testify clearly to a corruption of their composition and a malice of temperament that cannot be corrected. In such men, the **Intellect** never appears perfect; and therefore, to wish to attempt anything in this business with them is pure folly. Indeed, this **Wisdom** is prepared in a far different way than **Medicine**, **Arithmetic**, or **Geometry**. Not everyone is fit for it, for the reasons already stated; and it also necessarily requires a preceding preparation in good morals, through which a man may be established in the highest degree of rectitude and perfection. <margin>Proverbs 3:32</margin> *For the perverse man is an abomination to the Lord, but his secret is with the righteous.* And for this reason, they do not teach it to children and youths, because on account of the heat of their nature they are not fit to receive it, and because of their juvenile flames, which [incite?] their minds <insert>as they grow</insert>...

<summary>This page concludes the argument that spiritual and metaphysical study requires total dedication of time and a specific "natural disposition." Maimonides (via the Latin translator) argues that biological temperament and moral character are necessary prerequisites for intellectual perfection, noting that young people or those with volatile "humors" are unsuited for such deep wisdom.</summary>

### Part I. Chapter XXXIV.

occupy those who are growing and reaching puberty, until that immoderate flame is extinguished and they themselves become more steady and calm, knowing how to humble their minds and how to correct and improve their temperament. For only then, at last, should they be admitted and promoted to that rank—namely, the Apprehension of God, the Best and Greatest, or Divine Wisdom, which is called the <term>Work of the Chariot</term> <note>original: *Opus Mercavah*; this refers to the most profound and secret parts of Jewish mysticism and metaphysics, based on Ezekiel’s vision of the Divine Chariot.</note>. As it is said, <margin>Psalm 34:18</margin> *The Lord is near to those with a broken heart.* Also, *I dwell in the high and holy place, and with him who has a contrite and humble spirit,* etc. <margin>Isaiah 57:15</margin> Hence they write in the <term>Talmud</term> <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism, containing legal and philosophical discussions.</note> regarding the instruction: *The principal chapters are only handed over to the Father of the court* <note>The *Av Beit Din*, a high-ranking judicial official.</note> *whose heart is affected by grief and sorrow,* implying that meekness, humility, and the fear of sin are required alongside wisdom. And it is further said in the same place: *The secrets of the Law are not handed over except to a Counselor* <note>original: *lejoez*</note>, *to a Wise Craftsman* <note>original: *vachacham charafchim*</note>, *and to one Skilled in Eloquence* <note>original: *unevon lachafch*</note>. These traits, however, cannot exist in anyone without a natural disposition and inclination. For some men are completely lacking in counsel, even though they are very intelligent and ingenious. But there are others who are well-equipped with good and healthy counsel in political matters, pertaining to the governance of men and their minds—those who are designated by the name *Counselors*—yet meanwhile, in intelligible matters <note>Abstract or metaphysical concepts.</note>, even the most basic and easy ones, they understand nothing at all. Rather, they are ignorant, unskilled, and destitute of all talent for them. <margin>Proverbs 17:16</margin> *Why is there a price in the hand of a fool to buy wisdom, when he has no heart for it?* Others are intelligent, with a clear and piercing wit, who know how to encompass matters in brief and fitting words. These are included under the term *Skilled in Eloquence*. But because they expend no effort, they also do not attain the sciences and arts. However, he who has the actual knowledge of the arts and the habit of them is called a *Wise Craftsman* <note>original: *Chacham charafchim*</note>, or a Wise, Skilled Artisan, of whom they say, *When he speaks* (namely, regarding the words of the Law), *everyone becomes deaf* (because, of course, he discourses upon them so learnedly, so solidly, and so profoundly). From these things you see how much our Sages required of a man before they would hand over to him the Secrets of the Law: namely, that he be perfect both in political and practical affairs and in theoretical sciences, and furthermore that he be of a good nature, ingenious, and eloquent, knowing how to present matters accurately and properly. Hence we read in the same place that when Rabbi Jochanan said to Rabbi Eliezer, "Come and I will teach you the Work of the Chariot," Rabbi Eliezer replied, "I have not yet grown old, and I still feel the heat of nature and juvenile impulses." Therefore, since they required not only a proper age,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the qualifications for metaphysical study. The previous page ended by noting that certain natural dispositions and inclinations are required before a student can be admitted to the "Apprehension of God." **GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED**

...but also honest morals, or virtue, how could all people indiscriminately—the uneducated, the common folk, women, children, and so on—be admitted to this study?

<margin>5. Preoccupations or the care of household and domestic affairs.</margin>
The fifth cause is the **Preoccupation** with the necessities of the body, which are the "first perfection" (such as food, clothing, etc.), especially if this is joined with the care of a wife and children. This is to say nothing of a man’s depraved and perverse zeal, which he often spends on acquiring superfluous things—a frequent and common vice. For if even a "perfect man" <note>Maimonides distinguishes between "perfection" of the body (basic needs) and "perfection" of the soul (intellectual/spiritual achievement).</note> is too much occupied with these necessary things (and how much more so if occupied with the unnecessary?), his desire for speculative matters <note>Speculative matters refer to theoretical sciences, particularly metaphysics and the nature of God.</note> decreases and is smothered. He pursues them remissly, negligently, and with far less care and industry. Indeed, very often he does not grasp that which he could have grasped, or he achieves only a confused and troubled understanding, mixed from both perception and ignorance.

Therefore, for the reasons cited, these matters are suitable only for singular men, equipped with peculiar gifts, and not for everyone indiscriminately. For this reason, they must be hidden from novices and beginners; all who are not fit shall be kept away from them, just as a child is forbidden from eating heavy foods or carrying heavy burdens.

### CHAPTER XXXV.
### *That it is necessary for the Removal of Corporeality to be handed down by Tradition.*

DO NOT think that within those things we mentioned in the preceding chapters—concerning the Greatness of this Wisdom, its Concealment, and the difficulty of its Apprehension—there is also included the **Removal of corporeality** <note>original: *Remotio corporeitatis*; the theological requirement to understand that God has no physical body or form.</note>, or the removal of those actions or passions which enter into this business. By no means. For just as it is highly necessary that this doctrine be spread among the common people, and that children be initiated into it—namely, that God is one, and that no other is to be worshiped besides Him—so too it is no less necessary that it be handed down to them through <term>Tradition</term> <note>original: *Cabbalam*. While the word "Cabbala" (Kabbalah) often refers to Jewish mysticism, in this context Maimonides uses it in its literal sense: a "received" or handed-down tradition of fundamental religious truths.</note> that God is not corporeal. They must be taught that there is no likeness between Him and His creatures, even in the smallest thing; that His Being is not like their being, nor His Life like the life of living animals, nor His Wisdom like the wisdom of any human being. Furthermore, they must be taught that the difference between Him and His Creatures does not consist only...

<margin>The Incorporeality of God is to be handed down through Tradition.</margin>

# PART I. CHAPTER XXXV.
### G 2

...not only in quantity and scarcity, or greatness and smallness, but in the very species of essence. That is, everyone must firmly hold that His **Wisdom** and our wisdom, His **Strength** and our strength, do not differ according to scarcity or smallness and quantity, nor according to intensity or relaxation, or in any similar thing. For "strong" and "weak" necessarily agree in their species <note>In Aristotelian logic, "species" refers to a category of things that share a common definition. Maimonides argues God does not share a category with humans.</note> and are encompassed by a single definition; in respect to these, a comparison is made between two things that are one in species, as is explained in the **Natural Sciences**. However, in whatever attribution or property God is compared with us, we will always observe that His properties differ "by the whole heaven" <note>original: "toto cœlo"; a Latin idiom meaning "entirely" or "as far as the sky is from the earth."</note> from our own, and the two cannot be encompassed under a single definition.

<margin>How the properties of God differ from ours.</margin>

Thus, "existence" or "to exist" is said of Him and of others outside of Him **equivocally** <note>The term "equivocal" (homonymia) refers to words that sound the same but have entirely different meanings. For example, "existence" for a human means something different than "existence" for God.</note>, as I will explain elsewhere. And this measure suffices for children and for the common folk: that they know God exists, and that He is most perfect, without a body or any bodily power or faculty, without any imperfection, and consequently subject to no passion.

<margin>How much and what is permitted to teach children and the common folk about God.</margin>

But regarding the matter of the <term>Attributes of God</term> (namely, the essential ones), to what extent they are removed from Him, and what the sense of other attributes is that are attributed to Him (namely, metaphorically), likewise the **Creation of the World**, His **Governance**, **Providence**, the **Will of God**, His **Apprehension**, **Science**, and **Knowledge**, **Prophecy** and its degrees, and the **Names of God** (which, although many, nevertheless teach of His unity)—all these are profound and abstruse things, and truly the **Mysteries of the Law** <note>original: "Mysteria Legis"; likely referring to the *Sitre Torah*, the hidden secrets of the Torah in Jewish tradition.</note>, of which mention is made here and there in the books of the Prophets and our Rabbis. It is not fitting to speak of these except according to their main headings, as we have shown before, and indeed only in the presence of such a person as we saw described in the preceding chapter.

<margin>What is not permitted.</margin>

However, the removal of **corporeality** <note>The quality of having a physical body.</note>, as well as the removal of likeness and passion from God, are things that must be opened and explained to everyone exactly as they are. It is necessary to hand these down by <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>In this context, Maimonides uses "Kabbalah" to mean "tradition" or "received oral teaching," rather than the specific mystical system it would later denote.</note> and oral tradition to children and women, to the unlearned and the unskilled, just as it is taught to them that He is One, Eternal, and that no other besides Him is to be served. For Unity cannot be established except by the removal of corporeality; for a body is not one, but composed of **matter and form**, which are two in number. Furthermore, a body can be divided and is subject to division. Once they have received these things and have been practiced in them, yet find themselves hesitating or confused by the words of the Prophets <note>Referring to biblical passages that describe God with human features, like "the hand of God."</note>, then those words shall be explained to them in an orderly and distinct manner, and they shall be warned about **homonyms** and metaphorical usages.

<summary>Maimonides explains that God’s attributes are fundamentally different in kind from human ones and argues that while deep mysteries must be kept secret, the "incorporeality" of God (the fact that He has no body) must be taught to everyone, including children and the unlearned.</summary>

# GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

...of the vocabulary that occurs in the scriptures, so that their faith in the **Unity of God** <note>The concept of "Tawhid" or "Yichud"—the belief that God is one, indivisible, and unlike any created thing.</note> may be confirmed, and thus they may believe in the books of the Prophets with a perfect faith.

However, if someone possesses a mind too weak to comprehend and understand the explanations of those verses—and how a certain name may be used in a different sense—it should be told to him that such an explanation is understood by the Wise and the learned. For such a person, it is sufficient to know that God (Blessed be He) is not corporeal <note>original: "corporeus"; meaning God has no physical body.</note>, nor is He subject to any **passion** <note>original: "passioni"; in historical theology, a "passion" is any state where one is acted upon or undergoes a change, such as emotion or physical sensation.</note> (because passion is a species of Change, and no change occurs in God). It is enough for him to know that God cannot be compared to any thing, nor can He be grasped under one and the same definition with any other thing; and finally, that the words of the Prophets are true and possess a certain, reliable interpretation. With such minds, one can stop there. For it is not necessary that a person’s knowledge regarding the matter of **Corporeality** and physical affections be confirmed by complex arguments; just as it is not necessary to prove to him with arguments that there is no other God, or that God has no partner or equal, or that no other God is to be worshipped.

### CHAPTER XXXVI.
->Why God is said to be Angry, and to be affected by Jealousy.<-

SINCE we are still dealing with the various attributes assigned to God, I will also explain to you in what respect it is said of God that a certain thing pleases or displeases Him, or provokes Him to anger. It is not our intention in this chapter to explain the reason why it is said of humans that they please God, or that He is angry with them; rather, we wish to show this:

<margin>
Anger, Fury, Indignation, and Jealousy are never attributed to God except on account of Idolatry. 
Deut. 11:16-17.
Deut. 6:14-15.
Deut. 31:29.
Deut. 32:21.
Deut. 6:15.
Jerem. 8:19.
</margin>
If you consider and examine the entire Law <note>The Torah.</note> and all the books of the Prophets, you will never find these words—*Fury* <note>original Hebrew: "Charon"</note>, *Anger* <note>original Hebrew: "Aph"</note>, *Indignation* <note>original Hebrew: "Caas"</note>, or *Envy, Rivalry, and Jealousy* <note>original Hebrew: "Kinah"</note>—attributed to God, except when the subject is **Idolatry**. Furthermore, you will not find anyone called an *Enemy*, *Adversary*, or *Hater of God*, except on account of Idolatry. For instance: *Lest you worship foreign gods etc. and then the anger of the Lord be kindled against you.* Likewise: *You shall not go after foreign gods etc. lest the fury of the LORD your God be kindled against you.* Again: *Provoking him to anger with the work of your hands.* Also: *They have moved me to jealousy with that which is not God; they have provoked me to anger with their vanities.* Also: *For the Lord your God is a jealous God etc.* Also: *Why have they provoked me to anger with their graven images, and...*

Part I. Chapter XXXVI.
G 3

<margin>Deuteronomy 32:19
Jeremiah 15:14
Nahum 1:2
Deuteronomy 7:10
Numbers 32:21
Deuteronomy 16:22
Deuteronomy 12:31</margin>
...with the vanities <note>original: "vanitatibus"; a common biblical term for idols, implying they are empty or breath-like.</note> of a foreign god? Likewise, *Because of anger against his sons and his daughters*. Likewise, *For a fire is kindled in my fury, it shall burn upon you*. Likewise, *He takes vengeance on his enemies*. Likewise, *He repays those who hate him*. Likewise, *Until he has driven out his enemies from his presence*. Likewise, *That which the Lord your God hates*. Likewise, *Every abomination of the Lord, which he hates*. And so it is in countless other places; if you run through them all, you will find they are all spoken in this sense. The Prophets used this amplification, or rather exaggeration, because this error—I mean Idolatry—touches upon God Himself. 

For a person who believes that Reuben <note>In medieval philosophy, "Reuben" is often used as a generic name, similar to "John Doe," to illustrate a point about an individual.</note> is standing when he is actually sitting does not stray as far from the truth as someone who believes the Element of Fire is located below the Air, or that the Element of Water is beneath the Earth, or that the Earth is flat, or something similar. This second person does not stray as far from the truth as one who believes the Sun rises from Fire, or that the sphere of the heavens is only a half-globe. This third person does not deflect as much from the truth as one who believes that Angels eat and drink, or do something of that nature. And this fourth person does not err as gravely as one who believes that another god ought to be worshipped. For when ignorance, disbelief, or "negation" concerns something great and excellent, it is considered far greater and more serious than if it concerns a trivial or lowly matter. 

I use the term **Negation** when we believe something to be different than it actually is. I use the term **Ignorance** for a simple lack of knowledge regarding a thing that can be known. Thus, the ignorance of one who does not know the mathematical proportion of a cone to a cylinder, or who does not know that the Sun is round, is not as "thick" <note>original: "crassa"; meaning dense or profound.</note> as the ignorance of one who does not know whether there is a God in the world or not. Likewise, the "negation" of one who believes a cone is half the size of a cylinder (*when it is actually a third part, according to Euclid’s Geometry, Book 5, Theorem 10, Proposition 10*) or who denies that the Sun is round, is not as great as the negation of one who denies that there is only one God. 

Furthermore, regarding Idolaters, you know that none of them worship an idol with the opinion that there is no other God besides it. No human has ever existed, nor will exist, who imagines that the figure he has fashioned from metal, wood, or stone actually created the heavens and the earth and governs them. Rather, they worship these things insofar as they hold them to be **Intermediaries** <note>original: "rebus intermediis"; things that stand in the middle, acting as a bridge between a person and the Divine.</note> between themselves and God, the Best and Greatest. This is clearly explained to us by Jeremiah when he says: *Who will not fear you, O King of nations?* etc. Likewise: *And in every place incense is offered* <term>to my Name</term> <note>Hebrew: "lishmi"; original Latin: "Nomini meo".</note> *that is, to my Name, or on account of me*. For that phrase (*to my Name*) indicates the **Primary Cause** <note>original: "caussam primam"; the ultimate source of everything, which in this context refers to God.</note> on whose account incense is offered, according to the opinion of those idolaters themselves. But we have already dealt with this matter in our larger Work <note>A reference to Maimonides' legal code, the *Mishneh Torah*.</note>; nor indeed...

<margin>How idolaters worship idols.</margin>
<margin>Jeremiah 10:7
Malachi 1:11</margin>

He clarifies that most idolaters do not believe the idol is the Creator, but rather use it as a misguided intermediary to reach God.</summary>
