### THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED
**Page 54**

None of the Sages of our Law <note>The Hebrew "Sages" (Chazal) refers to the authoritative rabbinic scholars of the Mishnah and Talmud.</note> contradict these points. However, although those heretics <gloss>the idolaters</gloss> believe that God exists, their negation or heresy consists in giving to others what is owed to God alone <gloss>namely, worship and celebration, as it is said, *And you shall worship the Lord, etc.*</gloss>. It is through this worship that the belief that God exists is confirmed in the minds of the common people. By giving this worship to others, as if it were owed to them or pertained to them as well, they lead the poor common people to the point where they no longer believe that God exists. For the common people <gloss>that is, the masses</gloss> perceive worship only externally <gloss>thinking that he is God who is worshipped by everyone</gloss>; they do not understand the reasons for these acts, nor the truth of the One who is worshipped. By this act, they achieve nothing but death, <margin>Deuteronomy 20:16</margin> as it is said, *You shall not leave alive any soul* <note>original: "Non relinques vivam ullam animam"</note>. This is so that this false opinion may be removed, lest others be lost because of it. For this cause is <margin>Deuteronomy 20:18</margin> expressly added: *Lest they teach you to do according to all their abominations* <note>original: "Ne doceant te facere secundùm omnes abominationes suas"</note>.

If, therefore, these people are called Enemies, Foes, and Haters of God, and it is said of them that they provoke Him to Anger and Jealousy, what shall be the status of those whose heresy touches God Himself directly? I mean those who believe many false and absurd things about God—those who either deny the true God and His essence, or who believe there are two Gods, or who believe God is corporeal <term>corporeal: having a physical body or material form</term> and subject to or liable to passions <term>passions: in medieval philosophy, "passions" are changes or emotional reactions that imply a being is affected by something external, which Maimonides argues cannot happen to an eternal, perfect God</term> or other imperfections?

For these people are far worse than those who worship idols as if they were mediators or intermediate things between God and themselves, or as if they possessed the power to do good or harm. Therefore, you should be firmly persuaded that if you believe in corporeality, or the affections and qualities of a body, you offend God far more gravely and provoke Him to anger and jealousy much more. You are a much more fierce Enemy, Foe, and Hater of God than one who practices idolatry. 

Nor should you think that one who believes in corporeality can be excused <margin>Ignorance and education do not excuse heresy.</margin> by saying that he was raised in that opinion, or that he does not know any better, or is of a duller wit. For in that way an idolater could also be excused, on the grounds that he does it out of ignorance, or because he was raised in that custom by his parents. Nor should you say that the literal sense of the Law <note>The "Law" refers to the Torah or Pentateuch.</note> drove them into this error; for idolaters, too, are moved to their worship by their own false and empty imaginations. 

Since all these things are so, those who are not suited by their own natural talent for the investigation of these matters, yet refuse to accept the truth from those who understand it deeply, are entirely without excuse. For I do not consider him a heretic who does not bring forward <gloss>that is, who cannot bring forward</gloss> demonstrations to remove—

<summary>Maimonides argues that believing God has a physical body or human-like emotions is a more severe form of heresy than idolatry, as it fundamentally corrupts the nature of the Divine. He asserts that ignorance or a literal reading of scripture is no excuse for such a dangerous intellectual error.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 36, which discusses why the attribution of a physical body to God is considered a grave error. It then begins Chapter 37, which initiates a linguistic analysis of the Hebrew word "Panim" (Face) to show that biblical references to God’s "face" do not imply He has physical features.</meta>

PART I. CHAPTER XXXVI.

...belief in corporeality <note>The philosophical concept of having a physical body.</note>; but I consider him a heretic who does not believe in its removal <note>That is, the necessity of denying that God has a physical form</note>: especially since Onkelos and Jonathan <note>Onkelos and Jonathan ben Uzziel were the authors of the authoritative Aramaic translations (Targums) of the Hebrew Bible, known for removing physical descriptions of God.</note> so carefully avoided everything they could in this regard.

And this is what we intended for this chapter.

### CHAPTER XXXVII.
### ->In which the word *Panim* (Face) is explained.<-

<margin>*Panim* [Face].</margin>
*Panim* (Face) is a word that is highly <term>equivocal</term> <note>In this context, "equivocal" refers to a word that has several entirely different meanings despite using the same sound or spelling.</note>.
*First*, it signifies the Face of any living being; as in, *And all faces <note>original: "panim"</note> are turned to paleness*. Likewise, *Why are your faces bad?*
<margin>Jeremiah 30:6; Genesis 40:7.</margin>

Next, it signifies Anger or Indignation: as in, *And her face <note>original: "Uphaneha"</note> (anger) was no longer with her*. In this sense, it is often used regarding the Indignation and Wrath of God: as in, *The Face (anger) of God divided them*. Likewise, *The Face (anger) of the Lord is upon those who do evil*. Likewise, *My Face (anger) shall depart, and I will cause you to rest*. Likewise, *And I will set my face (anger) etc.* And so it is used very frequently.
<margin>1 Samuel 1:18; Lamentations 4:16; Psalm 34:17; Exodus 33:14; Leviticus 20:5.</margin>

*Thirdly*, it is taken for the essence of a person and their state (that is, regarding the presence and persona of a person): as in, *In the faces <note>original: "Al pene"</note> of all your brothers he died* <note>Meaning he died in their presence.</note>. Likewise, *In the faces of all people I shall be glorified*, that is, before them, while they are present. So, *Unless he curses you to your face <note>original: "el panim"</note>*, that is, in your presence. In this sense it is said: *And the Lord spoke to Moses face to face*, that is, in His own persona, openly, without a mediator; just as it is said, *Come, let us show ourselves to be seen face to face*, and as it is said elsewhere, *Face to face the Lord spoke with you*. How these things are to be understood is explained more clearly in another place: *You heard the voice of the words, and you saw no likeness besides the voice*: and yet concerning this it says, *Face to face*. Therefore, in the same way in that passage, *And the Lord spoke to Moses to his face*, it must be understood as the declaration of words—that is, the *Ten Commandments*—just as it is said, *And he heard the voice speaking with him*. From this you see that the Hearing of the voice without the mediation or intervention of an Angel is designated by that phrase, *Face to face*. Finally, from this third meaning comes: *And my face shall not be seen*; for the sense is: The truth of my essence, or my Essence itself as it is, cannot be grasped.
<margin>Genesis 25:18; Leviticus 10:3; Job 1:11; Exodus 33:11; 2 Chronicles 25:17; Deuteronomy 5:4; Deuteronomy 4:12; Exodus 33:11; Numbers 7:89; <insert>What "Face to face" signifies.</insert> Exodus 33:23.</margin>

*Fourthly*, this word *Panim* denotes the circumstance of a Place, and signifies that which is in the face of a person, or before a person (*in one and the same place*) and in his hands. In this sense it is often used concerning God, the Blessed...

<summary>Maimonides defines the various meanings of the Hebrew word "Panim" (Face), arguing that when applied to God, it refers to His anger, His presence, or His essence, rather than a physical countenance.</summary>
<keywords>Panim, Face, Onkelos, Jonathan, Essence, Presence, Moses, Ten Commandments, Anthropomorphism, Equivocal terms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the word "Panim" (Face) from Chapter 37 and begins Chapter 38, which examines the word "Achor" (Back/Behind). These chapters are crucial to Maimonides’ project of showing that biblical descriptions of God’s "face" or "back" refer to His presence, providence, or different levels of created beings, rather than physical anatomy.</meta>

GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

<margin>Exodus 33:23.</margin>
...as when it is said *Liphne*—"Before the face of the Lord." From this meaning comes also that which follows the exposition of Onkelos <note>Onkelos was a famous 2nd-century convert whose Aramaic translation of the Torah is authoritative for his habit of removing physical descriptions of God.</note>: *And my face shall not be seen*. For he translates it as, *And those things which are before me shall not be seen*, implying that there are certain sublime and noble creatures that man cannot grasp as they truly are; namely, the **Separated and Abstract Intelligences** <note>In medieval philosophy, these "Intelligences" were considered pure minds or angelic beings that existed without physical bodies.</note>. Of these, he says that they are "before God" and "continually in His hands" because of the supreme care and providence He constantly has for them. However, those things which can be grasped according to Onkelos are those in a lower and less noble degree of existence—that is, substances consisting of matter and form (*such as natural bodies, both simple and composite*). Regarding these, He says: *And you shall see what is after me* <note>original: "quæ post me sunt"</note>, as if to say: those Beings that exist as if I had turned away from them and cast them behind my back. This is used parabolically because they are more distantly removed from the essence of God. This is indeed the exposition of Onkelos; you shall hear my own opinion regarding what Moses requested from God later in Chapter 54.

<margin>Ruth 4:7.
Psalm 102:26.</margin>
*Fifth*, it also has a meaning regarding **Time**, and signifies the same as *Kodem*—"Before," "Formerly," or "Beforehand": as in, *Lephanim*—"In former times in Israel." Likewise, *Lephanim*—"Of old," or "Before you founded the earth."

<margin>Leviticus 19:15.
Job 22:8.
Deuteronomy 28:50.
Numbers 6:26.</margin>
*Sixth* and finally, it signifies **Care and Providence**, or **Respect**: as in, *You shall not accept the person* <note>literally "the face"</note> *of the poor*. Likewise, *And the accepted man of face* <note>meaning a man of high standing/rank</note>. Likewise, *Who does not accept the face* <note>shows no partiality</note>. This is used very frequently. According to this meaning, we should also understand: *The Lord lift up his face* <note>original: "panav"</note> *upon you, and give you peace*. For it means to say: "His providence shall accompany us."

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.
### In which the word אחור (Achor/Achare) is explained.
### *Back, Afterward, etc.*

<margin>אחרי אחור
[Achor / Achare]
Back / After.
Exodus 26:12.
2 Samuel 2:23.
2 Kings 23:26.
Genesis 22:20.</margin>
**ACHOR** is an <term>equivocal</term> word <note>A word with multiple distinct meanings.</note>. First, it signifies the **Back** or the **Rear**: as in, *Achore*—"The back parts," that is, "The back of the Tabernacle." Likewise, *And the spear came out meacharav*—"from his back parts" (that is, through his back).

Next, it also signifies **Time**: as in, *Veacharav*—"And after him there arose none like him." Likewise, "After those words." And so it is used many times.

<margin>Deuteronomy 13:5.
Hosea 11:10.
Hosea 5:11.</margin>
*Third*, it signifies **Following after** something, or imitating the character and virtues of another: as in, *Achare*—"You shall walk after the Lord your God." Likewise, *Achare*—"They shall walk after the Lord." In these instances, it signifies following His will, walking in the ways of His works, and imitating His virtues. Thus it is said, "He walked *achare*—after the commandment." And in this sense it is said, *You shall see*

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of Chapter 38 regarding the "back" of God and begins Chapter 39, which focuses on the word "Heart" (Lebh). As with previous chapters, Maimonides is methodically showing that when the Bible uses physical body parts to describe God or spiritual concepts, it is using these words as homonyms—words that sound the same but have entirely different meanings depending on the context.</meta>

# PART I. CHAPTER XXXVIII.
### H

...*you shall see my back* <note>original: "videbis posteriora mea"; Exodus 33:23</note>, that is: you shall grasp those things which follow me and are likened to me, and which result from my will—namely, all my created things, as I have explained in other chapters.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.
#### *In which the multiple meanings of the word*
#### לב *Lebh* (Heart) are explained.

<margin>לב Heart</margin>
**LEBH** is a word with multiple meanings <term>homonym: a word that has the same spelling or sound but different meanings</term>. In the **first** sense, it is the name of the physical Organ in which the principle of life for all living beings resides: as in, *And he plunged them into the heart of Absalom.*
<margin>2 Samuel 18:14.</margin>

Furthermore, because this Organ is located in the center of the body, it is applied in a **second** sense to the Middle or Center of any thing: as in, *Unto the heart of the heavens.*
<margin>Deuteronomy 4:11.</margin>
Likewise, *In the heart of the fire.*
<margin>Exodus 3:2.</margin>

In the **third** sense, it is taken to mean Thoughts: as in, *Did not my heart go with you?* <note>original: "Nónne libbi cor meum ivit tecum?"</note> that is: Was I not occupied in my thoughts when such and such happened? It is in this sense that it says, *You shall not seek after your own heart*, that is: you shall not follow your own thoughts. Likewise, *Whose heart turns away today*, that is: whose thought turns away.
<margin>2 Kings 5:26.</margin>
<margin>Numbers 15:39.</margin>
<margin>Deuteronomy 29:18.</margin>

In the **fourth** sense, it signifies Counsel or Purpose: as in, *All the rest of Israel were of one heart to make David king*, that is: they were of one counsel. Likewise, *Fools die for want of heart*, that is: for want and poverty of counsel or sense. Likewise, *My heart shall not reproach me <note>original: "non יחריף לבבי jecheraph levavi"</note> all my days*, that is: my counsel or purpose shall not waver; for that verse begins, *I will hold fast my righteousness, and will not let it go.* In my judgment, the word *Jecheraph* <note>a Hebrew root meaning to reproach or, in some contexts, to betroth/assign</note> carries this same sense in the phrase *A slave girl betrothed to a man* <note>original: "Ancilla נחרפת necherephet leisch"; Leviticus 19:20</note> (that is, one who is assigned or designated for a man). Similarly, in the Arabic language, there is a related word *Harif*, which signifies one who turns away from servitude and passes into marriage.
<margin>1 Chronicles 12:38.</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 10:21.</margin>
<margin>Job 27:6.</margin>
<margin>Leviticus 19:20.</margin>

In the **fifth** sense, it signifies Will: as in, *And I will give you shepherds according to my heart.* Likewise, *Is your heart right, as my heart is with your heart?* as if to say: Is your will right just as mine is? In this sense, it is used in reference to the Creator: *He shall do according to what is in my heart and in my mind*, that is: he shall do according to my will. Likewise, *My eyes and my heart shall be there perpetually*, that is: my providence and my will.
<margin>Jeremiah 3:15.</margin>
<margin>2 Kings 10:15.</margin>
<margin>1 Samuel 2:35.</margin>
<margin>1 Kings 9:3.</margin>

In the **sixth** sense, it signifies the Mind or Intellect: as in, *A hollow man is made heartened*, that is: he becomes intelligent. Likewise, *A wise man’s heart is at his right hand*, that is: his Intellect is directed toward perfect things. So it is used elsewhere. And in this...
<margin>Job 11:12.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiastes 10:2.</margin>

<vocab>Lebh (Heart), Cor (Heart), homonymia (multiple meanings), cogitatio (thought), consilium (counsel), voluntas (will), intellectus (intellect), Jecheraph (to reproach/waver), Necherephet (betrothed/assigned)</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on God's "back" as a metaphor for his creations and begins an analysis of the word "Heart" (Lebh). Maimonides identifies six distinct meanings for "heart" in the Bible, ranging from the physical organ to the center of an object, thoughts, counsel, will, and the intellect.</summary>

<header>GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED</header>

<margin>Exodus 33:23.</margin>...as when it is said, *Before the Face of the Lord* <note>original: "Liphne Ad faciem Domini"</note>. Of these, it is said that they are "before God" and "continually in his hands," because of the supreme care and providence He constantly has for them.

However, those things that *can* be grasped according to him (I mean Onkelos), are those which are in a lower and less noble degree of existence—that is, substances, matter, and form, (*such as natural bodies, whether simple or composite*). This is used parabolically because they are further removed from the essence of God. Likewise, *An honorable man* <note>original: "Et acceptus panim facie"</note>. Likewise, *Who does not respect faces*. This usage is very frequent. It is according to this meaning that we should understand the verse: *The Lord lift up his face upon you, and give you peace* <note>The Priestly Blessing</note>. For it intends to say: His providence shall accompany us.

PART I. CHAPTER 38.
H

...you shall see my back <note>original: "posteriora mea"</note>, that is, you will grasp those things which follow me and are like me, and which result from my will—namely, all my creatures, as I have explained in other chapters.

### CHAPTER 39.

->*In which is explained the multiple meanings of the word*<-
->לב **Lebh** (Heart)<-

<margin>Heart <note>Hebrew: Lebh</note></margin>
<margin>2 Samuel 18:14</margin>
**HEART** is a word with multiple meanings <term>homonymia: the phenomenon where one word has several distinct meanings</term>. First, it is the name of the organ that is the source of life for all living creatures: as in, *And he thrust them into the heart of Absalom*.

<margin>Deuteronomy 4:11</margin>
<margin>Exodus 3:2</margin>
Secondly, since this organ is located in the middle of the body, it is adapted to refer to the **Middle** of any thing: as in, *Unto the heart of heaven*. Likewise, *In the heart of the fire*.

<margin>2 Kings 5:26</margin>
<margin>Numbers 15:39</margin>
<margin>Deuteronomy 29:18</margin>
Thirdly, it is also taken for **Thoughts**: as in, *Did not my heart go with you?* <note>2 Kings 5:26</note>, meaning: was I not occupied in my thoughts when such and such happened? It is from this sense that we find, *You shall not seek after your own heart*, that is, you shall not follow your own thoughts. Likewise, *Whose heart turns away this day*, meaning whose thought turns away.

<margin>1 Chronicles 12:38</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 10:21</margin>
<margin>Job 27:6</margin>
<margin>Leviticus 19:26 <note>The text likely refers to Leviticus 19:20, where the term 'ne-cherephet' appears</note></margin>
Fourthly, it signifies **Counsel** or purpose: as in, *All the rest of Israel were of one heart* to make David king, that is, they were of one counsel. Likewise, *Fools die for want of heart*, meaning for lack and poverty of counsel. Also, *My heart shall not reproach me all my days* <note>Job 27:6; original: 'jecheraph levavi'</note>, meaning my resolution shall not waver; for that verse begins, *I will hold fast my righteousness, and will not let it go*. In my judgment, this same sense of the word *reproach* <note>original: 'Jecheraph'</note> applies to the phrase, *A handmaid betrothed to a man* <note>original: 'ne-cherephet leisch'</note> (that is, one who is designated or assigned to a man). In the Arabic language, there is a similar word, *ne-cherephet*, which signifies one who turns away from servitude and passes into marriage.

<margin>Jeremiah 3:15</margin>
<margin>2 Kings 10:15</margin>
<margin>1 Samuel 2:35</margin>
<margin>1 Kings 9:3</margin>
Fifthly, it signifies **Will**: as in, *And I will give you shepherds according to my heart*. Likewise, *Is your heart right, as my heart is?* which is to say: is your will right like mine? In this sense, it is used in reference to the Creator: *He shall do according to that which is in my heart and in my soul*, meaning he shall do according to my will. Likewise, *And my eyes and my heart shall be there perpetually*, meaning my providence and my will.

<margin>Job 11:12</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiastes 10:2</margin>
Sixthly, it signifies the **Mind** or **Intellect**: as in, *But a hollow man is made hearted*, meaning he becomes intelligent. Likewise, *A wise man's heart is at his right hand*, meaning his intellect is directed toward perfect things. So it is used elsewhere. And in this...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Hebrew word 'Lebh' (Heart) from the previous page, specifically how it applies to God as a metaphor for Will and Intellect. It then transitions to Chapter 40, which begins an analysis of the word 'Ruach' (Spirit/Wind).</meta>

<margin>
In what sense "Heart" is attributed to God.
Deuteronomy 4:39
Isaiah 44:19
Deuteronomy 29:4
Deuteronomy 4:35
Deuteronomy 6:5
</margin>
In this sense, the term is frequently attributed to God—Blessed be He—except in a few places where it is used to mean **Will**. However, each instance must be explained according to its specific context. Thus we read: *Bring it back to your heart* <note>original: "Revocato ad cor tuum," referring to internal reflection</note>. Likewise: *And no one considers in his heart* <note>original: "Et non revocat ad cor suum," Isaiah 44:19</note>. In all such similar cases, the word is derived from the notion of the **Intellect** <note>original: "Intellectus"</note>, just as it is said: *The Lord has not given you a heart to perceive* <note>Deuteronomy 29:4</note>. Likewise: *To you it was shown, that you might know that the Lord is God* <note>Deuteronomy 4:35</note>.

But regarding the passage, *You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart* <note>original: "bechol levavecha," Deuteronomy 6:5</note>, this, in my judgment, is equivalent to saying: with all the powers of your heart—that is, with all the powers and faculties of the body. For the principle and origin of all these faculties proceed from the heart. The meaning, therefore, is: The ultimate goal of all your actions shall be the **Apprehension of God** <note>the intellectual grasping or understanding of the Divine</note>, as we have explained in our *Commentary on the Talmudic Text* and in our *Talmudic Work* <note>Maimonides refers to his Commentary on the Mishnah and his legal code, the Mishneh Torah</note>.

### CHAPTER XL.
-> In which the word **Ruach** is explained <-
-> **Spirit** <-

<margin>
**Ruach**: Wind, Spirit.
Genesis 1:2
Exodus 10:13
Exodus 10:19
Psalm 78:39
Genesis 6:17
Ecclesiastes 12:7
Numbers 11:17
Numbers 11:26
2 Samuel 23:2
Proverbs 29:11
</margin>
**Ruach** is a word with multiple meanings <term>homonyma: a term used for words that have several distinct definitions</term>. First, it signifies **Air**, which is one of the four Elements: as in, *And the Spirit (Ruach) of God moved over the waters* <note>Genesis 1:2</note>.

Secondly, it signifies the blowing air, that is, **Wind**: as in, *And the East wind (Ruach) brought the locusts*. Likewise, *A West wind (Ruach)*. It is used in this way very frequently.

Thirdly, it is taken for the **Vital Spirit** <note>the breath of life that animates living beings</note>: as in, *A spirit (Ruach) that passes away and does not return*. Likewise, *In which is the breath (Ruach) of life*.

Fourthly, it is used for that **Incorruptible Part** of man which remains after death: as in, *And the spirit (Ruach) of man returns to God, who gave it*.

Fifthly, it signifies that **Divine Intellectual Influence** instilled by God into the Prophets, by the virtue of which they prophesy—as we shall explain when we discuss the nature of Prophecy later in this book: as in, *I will take of the spirit (Ruach) that is upon you and put it upon them*. Likewise, *And it came to pass, when the spirit (Ruach) rested upon them*. Also, *The Spirit (Ruach) of the Lord spoke through me*.

Sixthly, it also signifies **Purpose** and **Will**: as in, *A fool vents all his spirit (Ruach)* <note>Proverbs 29:11</note>, that is, his entire intention and will—

<meta>This page continues Chapter 40's discussion on the word 'Ruach' (Spirit/Wind), specifically focusing on how it signifies the Divine Will. It then transitions to Chapter 41, which begins a multi-layered definition of the Hebrew word 'Nephesh' (Soul), clarifying its various biblical uses from biological life to the rational human form and the Divine Will.</meta>

Part I. Chapter XL.
H 2

...His [will]. Thus, *And the spirit [ruach] of Egypt shall be emptied out in its midst* <margin>Isaiah 19:3</margin> and *I will swallow up its counsel*, that is: its purpose will be dissipated, and its governance will be hidden. Thus, *Who has directed the spirit [ruach] of the Lord, and who was his counselor, that he might inform him?* <margin>Isaiah 40:13</margin> That is: Who is he who knows the order of His will, or who grasps and attains by what reasoning He governs this universe, and who can point it out? As we shall declare this elsewhere as well. You see, therefore, that this word **Ruach** [Spirit/Wind], <margin>In what sense it is attributed to God.</margin> when it is attributed to God, is everywhere taken partly in the fifth, partly in the sixth and final meaning <note>referring to the categories of meaning Maimonides established earlier in this chapter</note>, insofar as it signifies **Will**. Let it be explained in each place according to the nature of the matters and circumstances.

### CHAPTER XLI.

-> In which the word נפש **Nephesh** is explained <-
-> **Soul** <-

<margin>**Nephesh**: Soul.</margin> **Nephesh** is an equivocal term <note>a word with multiple distinct meanings</note>. First, it is a general name for the **Soul** of any living thing endowed with sensation: as in, *In which there is a living soul [nephesh]* <margin>Genesis 1:30</margin>.

Secondly, it is also taken to mean **Blood**: as in, *You shall not eat the soul [hannephesh] with the flesh* <margin>Deuteronomy 12:23</margin>.

Thirdly, it specifically signifies the **Rational Soul**, that is, the <term>form</term> of a human being <note>In Aristotelian terms, the 'form' is the essential nature or defining characteristic of a thing; for humans, this is the intellect</note>: as in, *As the Lord lives, who made for us this soul [hannephesh]* <margin>Jeremiah 38:16</margin>.

Fourthly, it denotes that thing which remains surviving even after the death of a human: as in, *And the soul [nephesh] of my lord shall be bound in the bundle of the living* <margin>1 Samuel 25:29</margin>.

Fifthly, it signifies **Will**: as in, *To bind his princes according to his soul [benaphsho]* <margin>Psalm 105:22</margin>, that is, according to his will. Likewise, *Do not deliver me over to the soul [benephesh] of my enemies* <margin>Psalm 27:12</margin>, that is: Do not surrender me to their judgment and desire. Likewise, *If it be with your soul [im naphshechem] to bury my dead* <margin>Genesis 23:8 <note>The Latin text cites Gen. 38:8, but the quote is from Gen. 23:8</note></margin>, that is: if it is your mind and will. Also, *If Moses and Samuel stood before me, my soul [naphshi] would not be toward this people* <margin>Jeremiah 15:1</margin>, that is: my will would not be for them, as if to say: I would not wish to preserve them. And thus the word **Nephesh** (Soul) is used for **Will** <margin>In what sense it is attributed to God.</margin> whenever it occurs regarding God the Blessed, as for example in the recently cited passage: *Just as in my heart and in my soul [ubenaphshi] he shall do* <margin>Book 1, chapter 39</margin>, for the meaning is: In my will and in my mind. I also explain in this way that passage: *His soul [naphsho] was shortened in the labor of Israel* <margin>Judges 10:16</margin>, that is: His will ceased and desisted from afflicting Israel. Jonathan <note>Jonathan ben Uzziel, the traditional author of the Aramaic translation (Targum) of the Prophets</note> did not explain this verse because he thought "Soul" here was taken...

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of 'Ruach' (Spirit) as Divine Will and begins an exploration of 'Nephesh' (Soul). It identifies five distinct biblical meanings for 'Nephesh', ranging from biological life and blood to the rational human intellect and the Divine Will.</summary>
<keywords>Ruach, Nephesh, Soul, Will, Rational Soul, Blood, Divine Attributes, Jonathan ben Uzziel, Biblical Exegesis</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the word "Nephesh" (Soul/Will) from Chapter 41 and begins Chapter 42, which examines the meanings of the Hebrew words "Chaj" (Life) and "Mut" (Death) in a theological and physiological context.</meta>

...in the first sense <note>the sense of the animal soul or vitality</note>, from which "passion" would follow (<note>original: "h.e. Deum passioni esse obnoxium"</note> *that is, that God would be subject to emotional change or suffering*); for this reason, he left that meaning untouched. But if it is indeed understood in this final sense <note>as the Divine Will</note>, the explanation is clear and simple. For it was stated previously that God’s providence had deserted them, and God did not save them until they had died and cried out seeking salvation. But after they had performed serious penance, and their misery increased while their enemies prevailed, He had mercy on them, and His will to continue their cross and affliction further ceased. Therefore, observe this carefully, for this explanation is very distinguished.

<margin>The prefix *Beth* is frequently used in place of *Mem*.</margin>
Furthermore, the prefix *Beth* (*In*) in the word בעמל <note>original: "be-amal"</note> *In labor* is in the place of the prefix *Mem* (*From/By*), as if it were said מעמל <note>original: "me-amal"</note> *From labor or trouble*. Nor is this something new; for those skilled in this language have long ago observed many such instances, such as:
<margin>Leviticus 8:32. Leviticus 25:52.</margin>
*That which remains* בבשר ובלחם <note>original: "be-basar u-be-lechem"</note> *in the flesh and in the bread* <note>meaning "from" the flesh and bread</note>. Likewise, *If little remains* בשנים <note>original: "be-shanim"</note> *in the years, etc.*

### CHAPTER XLII.
-> *In which is explained* חי **Chaj** *Living, Alive, To Live: and* מות **Mut**, *To Die.* <-

<margin>**Chaj**: To Live, and **Mut**: To Die.</margin>
**Chaj** is a noun that applies first to the vegetable and the sensitive [animal kingdom]: as in, *Every creeping thing that is a living [chaj] creature.* <margin>Genesis 9:3.</margin>

<margin>Isaiah 38:9. Joshua 5:8. Leviticus 13:16.</margin>
Secondly, it signifies the same thing as "to be healed" or "to recover" from a grave illness: as in, *And he lived [vajechi] from his sickness* <note>original: "Vajechi Et vixit"</note>. Likewise, *They stayed in the camp until they lived* <note>meaning "until they recovered" or "revived"</note>. So too, *Living [chaj] flesh.*

<margin>1 Samuel 25:37.</margin>
Conversely, **Mut** [to die] signifies likewise first Death, and secondly a certain grave illness and sickness, as in: *And his heart died, and it became a stone* <note>referring to Nabal</note>, that is, his illness was growing heavy.

<margin>1 Kings 17:17.</margin>
For this reason, to avoid this <term>homonymia<gloss>equivocation, or words that sound the same but have different meanings</gloss></term>, it is said of the son of the woman of Zarephath: *And his illness grew so strong that no breath remained in him.* For if it had been said, *And he died [vajamoth]*, it could have been interpreted as him being sick unto death, or lethally ill, just as Nabal was after he heard the words of his wife.

<margin>1 Samuel 25:37. Andalusians.</margin>
Certain **Andalusians** <note>referring to Jewish scholars from Muslim Spain</note> have argued that his breath had only failed, so that he no longer breathed—just as often happens in apoplexy, "suffocation of the womb" <note>an old medical term for hysterical or respiratory distress</note>, and other acute diseases where for several days it is doubtful whether such people are alive or dead.

<margin>Proverbs 3:22. Proverbs 8:35. Proverbs 4:22.</margin>
Thirdly, this word **Chajah** [to live] is used not infrequently for the possession of Wisdom: as in, *And they shall be life [chajim] to your soul.* Likewise, *He who finds me, finds life [chajim].* Also, *For they are life [chajim] to those who find them, etc.* And it is for this reason that good and salutary sciences or opinions are call- <note>The text cuts off at the catchword "vocan-" for "vocantur" (are called).</note>

<summary>Maimonides explains how the word "Soul" (Nephesh) refers to God's Will rather than human-like emotions. He then transitions to Chapter 42, defining "Life" (Chaj) and "Death" (Mut) as terms that can refer to physical vitality, recovery from illness, or the acquisition of wisdom.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the metaphorical meanings of "Life" and "Death" from Chapter 42 and begins a new lexical analysis of the Hebrew word "Kanaph" (Wing) in Chapter 43, exploring its transitions from physical anatomy to clothing, geography, and divine protection.</meta>

PARS I. CAP. XLII.
<sig>H 3</sig>

...are called *Life*; conversely, those things which are ruinous and pestilential are called *Death*: just as when God says: *Behold, I have set before you life and good, death and evil*: where he explicitly <margin>Deuteronomy 30:15.</margin> shows that Good is Life and Evil is Death. Thus I explain that which God says, *That you may live*, according to the accepted opinion, as meaning <margin>Deuteronomy 6:18.</margin> *That it may be well with you*. And because this metaphorical shift is frequent in this language <note>Hebrew</note>, our Rabbis of blessed memory <note>original: "p.m." for "piae memoriae"</note> say: *The righteous, even in their death, are called living; and conversely, the wicked, even while living, are called dead.*

### CHAPTER XLIII.
#### In which is explained כנף Kanaph (Wing).

<margin>כנף **Kanaph**: Wing, Edge/Hem.</margin>
**Kanaph** is an equivocal word <note>a term with several different meanings</note>, and through metaphor it is adapted to various things.

*First*, in its primary application, it signifies **the wings of flying creatures**: as in, *Every bird, every winged kanaph*. Likewise, *Every winged bird that flies <margin>Genesis 7:14. Deuteronomy 4:17.</margin> under the heavens.*

*Second*, it is transferred to the **wings or edges of garments**: as in, *On the four <margin>Deuteronomy 22:12.</margin> kanphoth (wings) of your garment.*

*Third*, it is applied to the **extremities of the habitable earth**, and its corners or regions far distant from our places: as in, *That he might take hold of the <margin>Job 38:13.</margin> kanphoth (wings) of the earth*. Likewise, *Mikkenaph (From the wing) of the earth* <note>meaning: from the end of the earth</note> *we have heard <margin>Isaiah 24:16.</margin> songs.*

*Fourth*, Aben Ganach <note>Jonah ibn Janah, a 11th-century Hebrew grammarian</note> said that it also occurs with the meaning of **Hiding**, just as in the Arabic language they say *kanafa al-shay* <note>original: "כנף אלשי", meaning to enclose or protect</note>. Thus, he explains that passage in Isaiah, *Your teacher shall not be made winged (jiccaneph)*, as: *Your teacher shall <margin>Isaiah 30:20.</margin> not be veiled, nor hidden from you*. And this exposition is elegant; according to it, in my judgment, this passage should also be explained: *He shall not <margin>Deuteronomy 22:30.</margin> uncover the kanaph (wing) of his father*, that is, he shall not reveal the hidden, occult, or secret thing of his father. Likewise, *And spread your kenaphecha (wing) <margin>Ruth 3:9.</margin> over your servant*, I explain as *your concealment and protection over your servant*. And in this sense, in my judgment, the term *Kanaph* is transferred to God (Blessed be He) and the Angels, because Angels also, according to our opinion, are incorporeal <note>they do not have physical bodies</note>. What is said: *You who have come to take refuge <margin>Ruth 2:12.</margin> under his kenaphav (wings)*, the explanation is: *You who have come to take refuge under His concealment and protection*. Likewise, when it is said of Angels, it similarly signifies Hiding or Concealment. For you know, when it is said, *With two (wings) he covered his face, and with two he covered his feet*, <margin>Isaiah 6:2.</margin> the Prophet intends to signify that the cause of the Angel’s essence (which is understood by the Face) and those things of which the Angels are the cause (that is, the effects and works of the Angels, which are understood by the Feet), are...

<summary>This page concludes the metaphorical definitions of life and death as good and evil, then examines the Hebrew word 'Kanaph' (wing), tracing its meaning from bird anatomy to clothing, geography, and eventually the concept of divine protection and the nature of angels.</summary>

<header>THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED</header>

<margin>Chapter 49, Part 1.</margin> I have set down under the word *Foot* <note>Hebrew: *Regel*</note>, they are very hidden and secret. For the actions of the Intelligences <note>In medieval philosophy, "Intelligences" refer to non-physical, purely intellectual beings, often identified with angels</note> are hidden, and their purpose is not easily apparent. This is for two reasons: one concerns the beings themselves, and the other concerns us. That is, it is partly due to the weakness of our own talent and understanding, and partly due to the extreme difficulty of grasping abstract truths as they truly are. Furthermore, as for what is said—*And with two wings he flew*—I will explain that to you in a specific chapter: namely, the reason why "Flight" is attributed to Angels.

### CHAPTER XLIV.
#### ->In which the word עין (Ajin), meaning "Eye," is explained.<-

<margin>**Ajin**: Eye. Genesis 16:7. Exodus 21:24.</margin> **Ajin** or **En** (Eye) is an equivocal word <note>A term that has several different and distinct meanings</note>. First, it signifies a **Spring of water**: as in, *By the fountain <note>original: "Al en"</note> of water in the desert.* Second, it signifies the **Eye**, the organ of sight: as in, *An eye <note>original: "Ajin"</note> for an eye.*

<margin>Jeremiah 39:12. *In what sense it is attributed to God.* 1 Kings 9:3. Deuteronomy 11:12. 2 Chronicles 16:9. Isaiah 37:17. Psalm 11:4.</margin> Third, it signifies **Care or Providence**: as when it is said of Jeremiah, *Take him... and set your eyes upon him.* For the meaning is: "Take care of him; let him be your concern." It is used in this sense when spoken of God the Blessed: as in, *And my eyes <note>original: "enaj"</note> and my heart shall be there perpetually*, which means "my providence and my intention," as we explained in previous sections. Likewise, *The eyes <note>original: "ene"</note> of the Lord your God are always upon it*, meaning His providence. Also, *The eyes of the Lord run to and fro throughout the whole earth*, meaning His Providence extends to all things that are on the earth; this will be explained further when we discuss Providence. 

However, when the verb *To See* <note>Hebrew: *Raah* or *Chafah*</note> is joined to this word *Ajin* (Eye), such as: *Open your eyes and see* <note>original: "ureeh"</note>; or, *His eyes shall behold* <note>original: "jechefu"</note>; then it denotes **Intellectual Apprehension**, not physical sensation. For everything perceived by the senses, as you know, is subject to "passion" <note>In this context, "passion" refers to the philosophical concept of being acted upon or changed by an external force</note>; but God acts and is never acted upon, as I shall explain.

### CHAPTER XLV.
#### ->In which the verb שמע (Schamah), meaning "He Heard," is explained.<-

<margin>**Schamah**: He Heard. Exodus 23:13. Genesis 45:16.</margin> **Schamah** is an equivocal word. First, it signifies the **Hearing of the ear**; second, it signifies the **Acceptance or Reception** of something. From the meaning of "Hearing" comes, *It shall not be heard <note>original: "jisschamah"</note> through your mouth.* Also, *And the report was heard <note>original: "nischmah"</note> in Pharaoh’s house.* And so it appears very frequently. This word *Schamah* also occurs often with the meaning of "Acceptance"...

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the page is "nis," indicating the start of the next word on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Maimonides explains that physical terms like "Eye" and "Hear" are used metaphorically for God to describe His providence and intellectual understanding, rather than physical organs or sensations. He emphasizes that God is an active force who is not subject to the physical changes or "passions" that humans experience through their senses.</summary>

Likewise, *And shall we then listen <note>original: "hanischmah"</note> to you?* Likewise, *Whoever will not hear <note>original: "schamah"</note> your words*.

<margin>Deuteronomy 28:49.</margin> Thirdly, it signifies Notice or Knowledge: as in, *A nation whose language you shall not hear <note>original: "tischmahu"</note>*, which means, whose speech you shall not know or will be ignorant of. Thus, whenever this word **Hear** <note>Hebrew: *Shama*</note> is attributed to God, if according to the literal sense of the Scripture it appears to belong to the first meaning <note>physical hearing of sound</note>, it is instead taken to mean Apprehension, and falls under the third meaning: as in, *And the Lord heard <note>original: "Vajischmah"</note>*. Likewise, *In His hearing <note>original: "Beschomeo"</note> your murmurings*. For all these signify the Apprehension of the mind. If, however, according to the literal sense of the text it appears to belong to the second meaning <note>obedience/reception</note>, then it signifies that God wills to **Answer** <note>The Latin *exaudire* implies not just hearing, but listening favorably or granting a request</note> the cry of the one calling to Him and to fulfill his petition, or that He does not wish to hear him nor satisfy his request: as in, *Hearing I will hear <note>original: "Schamoah eschmah"</note> his cry*. Likewise, *And I will hear <note>original: "Veschamati"</note>, for I am gracious*. Likewise, *Incline your ear and hear <note>original: "uschemah"</note>*. Likewise, *And God did not hear <note>original: "schamah"</note> your voice, nor did He turn His ears to you*. Likewise, *Though you multiply your prayer, yet I do not hear <note>original: "schomeah"</note>*. Likewise, *Because I do not hear <note>original: "schomeah"</note> you*, etc.

### Chapter XLVI.

### *By what means we are guided to the knowledge of God through His virtues, works, and creatures. Also, that bodily affections are attributed to God so that the people may learn that the perfection of all those things exists in God.*

<margin>Part 3, chapter 33.</margin> There is a great difference, as we have already hinted at in another chapter, between the guidance toward the **existence** of a thing and the **truth of its essence** and substance (that is, *between the knowledge of **the fact that it is** <note>original Greek: τὸ ὅτι (to hoti)</note> and **why/what it is** <note>original Greek: τὸ διότι (to dioti)</note> of a certain thing*). For the guidance used to demonstrate the existence of a thing is drawn either from its accidents, or its operations, or from certain other remote attributes of it: as for example, when—

<margin>How the existence of a thing is declared.</margin>

<vocab>**Shama**: To hear or obey. **Apprehension**: Mental grasping or understanding. **Exaudire**: To hear and grant a petition. **Quiddity**: The essence or "whatness" of a thing. **Accidents**: Non-essential properties (like color or size) that can change without changing the nature of the thing.</vocab>

It then introduces Chapter 46, which discusses the philosophical distinction between knowing that God exists through His works versus knowing His actual essence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Part I, Chapter 46 of Maimonides' *Guide for the Perplexed*. The previous page concluded the discussion on the Hebrew word "Shama" (to hear) and transitioned into how the Divine Law uses human analogies to describe God's existence and attributes to the common people.</meta>

->## THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED<-

->64<-

...if you wish to make a certain King known to one of his subjects who does not know him, you could describe him in various ways. For you might say: "He is a tall man, fair, pale, and grey-haired," thus describing him by his **accidental qualities** <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are characteristics like color or height that do not change what a thing essentially is.</note>. Or you might say: "He is the one around whom you see that great troop of infantry and cavalry, with drawn swords, banners raised above his head, and trumpets sounding before his face." Or: "He is the one who lives in this palace, in that land, which is in this or that climate." Or: "He is the one who ordered this house, wall, or bridge to be built." By these and similar means, you describe him partly by his works and partly by other external attributes.

But you can also teach and show the fact of his existence <note>original: *τὸ ὅτι* (to hoti)</note> through more hidden things. For instance, if someone asks you, "Does this land have a King?" and you answer, "It certainly does," what proof would you bring for this? You might say: "Since that money-changer <note>original: *numularius vel trapezita*</note> whom you see is a weak man of small stature, with a great abundance of gold before him, while that other man is robust and large-bodied but destitute, standing before him and seeking even a single penny <note>original: *obolum*</note> as alms—yet he not only receives nothing from him, but is severely rebuked and repelled with insulting words—surely if there were no fear of the King, he would kill him, drive him away, and take all his substance." In this way, you would have demonstrated that the province has its King; and from the **good order** <note>original: *εὐταξίᾳ* (eutaxia)</note> and civil arrangement of things, the cause of which is the fear of the King and his punishments, you would have shown that the King exists. In all these examples we have brought forward, there is nothing that teaches of the King's **essence** <note>What the King is in his own nature.</note>, or the truth of his substance insofar as he is a King.

This same method is usually followed in all the books of the Prophets and the Divine Law when God is described for the sake of the multitude and the common people.

<margin>*How God's existence is demonstrated in the Sacred Books for the common people.*</margin>

For when necessity demanded that all people be directed and led so that they might believe God exists and possesses all perfections—that is, that He does not exist simply in the way that the heaven and the earth exist, but that He is an existing being, or a living, wise, omnipotent, and active entity, and the rest that must be believed concerning Him (as will be declared in the following pages)—then, so that God's existence might be impressed upon their minds, this was demonstrated to them through the likeness of **corporeality** <note>The state of having a physical body.</note>; and that He is living, through the likeness of **Motion**. For the unlearned masses do not see how anything can truly and without doubt exist unless it is a body. And whatever is not a body, but only an "affection" <note>A quality or property that exists within a body, like color or heat.</note> in a body, does exist according to them and is a being, but it is less perfect than a body; because, for its existence...

<summary>Maimonides uses the analogy of a King to explain that while we cannot know God's true essence, we can know His existence through His actions and the order of the world. He argues that the Bible uses physical descriptions (corporeality) of God to help common people conceive of His reality, as they struggle to understand non-physical existence.</summary>

<keywords>analogy of the king, essence vs accidents, divine attributes, corporeality, existence of God, common people, Maimonides, philosophy of language</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Part I, Chapter 46 of the Guide for the Perplexed. In the preceding section, Maimonides discussed how the human intellect struggles to conceive of existence without physical attributes, leading the Prophets to use human analogies to describe God's presence and actions.</meta>

# Part I. Chapter XLVI.

->I<-

...a body is necessarily required. Indeed, that which is neither a body nor an affection <note>In medieval philosophy, an "affection" is a property or state of being</note> in a body, clearly does not exist in the principle of formality (that is, the *intellect*) of man, and especially not according to his imagination. Thus, people also do not grasp how anything can Live without Motion: hence, whatever does not move with voluntary and local motion does not live in their opinion, even though motion is not of the essence of a Living being, but an accident <note>A characteristic that is not essential to the thing's definition</note> inherent to it. So, because no Perception <note>original: *Apprehensio*</note> is known to us except that which occurs through the senses; and because we also do not understand how something can be transferred from the mind of one man into the mind of another except through speech or voice—which is formed by the lips, tongue, and the rest of the members and organs pertaining to speech—therefore, when they (the *Prophets*) wished to direct and dispose us to believe that God perceives, and that many things reach us from Him through the Prophets, they described Him to us as Hearing and Seeing. This means that He perceives and understands those things which are seen and heard. Likewise, they describe Him as Speaking, meaning that many things reach the Prophets from Him; the underlying nature of this Prophecy will be explained to you most fully in another place.

<margin>Why do the Prophets describe God as Seeing and Hearing?</margin>
<margin>Why as Speaking?</margin>

Furthermore, because we cannot understand how it can be brought about that something exists without contact, they described Him to us as Acting and Working. Likewise, because the common people <note>original: *vulgus*</note> do not grasp how a thing can Live if it lacks a soul, they also described Him as having a Soul. However, the word "Soul" is then called *ruach* <note>Hebrew: רוּחַ, meaning spirit, breath, or wind; Maimonides argues here it refers to the vitality of life</note>, as we explained above: for the sense is simply that He Lives.

<margin>Why as Acting?</margin>
<margin>Why as having a Soul?</margin>
<margin>chapter 41.</margin>

Furthermore, because the said Actions or Operations (*motion, sight, hearing, speech, and action*) are not performed in us except by corporeal <note>physical</note> organs and instruments, therefore, organs are also attributed to Him by which local motion occurs, such as feet, the soles of the feet, and so on; organs of hearing, sight, and smell, such as Ears, Eyes, and the Nose; and moreover, the organs and instruments of speech, such as the Mouth, Tongue, and Voice; and finally, those things used by one who does something, such as the Hands, Fingers, Palms, and the Arm. And all these things are for this purpose: to teach that God is without any imperfection. Corporeal organs are adapted to Him to teach us about their functions; yet the functions are meant to denote some other perfection, not those physical actions themselves. For example, the Eye, Ear, Hand, Mouth, and Tongue are attributed to Him to teach about Sight, Hearing, Action, and Speech; yet those actions, like Sight and Hearing, are attributed to Him to signify Perception <note>original: *Apprehensio*</note> in general. Hence it is that in this Hebrew Language, the Perception of one Sense is often put for the Perception of another sense: as in, *See the Word of the Lord* <note>original Latin: *Videte Verbum Domini*; citing Jeremiah 2:31</note>, that is...

<margin>Why do they attribute body parts to Him?</margin>
<margin>Perceptions of the Senses are confounded in the Hebrew Language. Jeremiah 2:31.</margin>

| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Anthropomorphism | Corporeal Organs | Sight |

<summary>Maimonides explains that the Bible attributes physical organs and senses to God (anthropomorphism) solely to help humans understand Divine perfections, such as knowledge and action, using the only vocabulary available to us.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion in Part I, Chapter 46 regarding why physical attributes are ascribed to God in the Scriptures. Maimonides is explaining that human language uses sensory terms to describe divine perception and action to make these concepts accessible to the human mind.</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

<margin>Genesis 27:27</margin>
To "hear the word of the Lord" means that the goal is for people to grasp the meaning of His word. Likewise, in the passage: *See, the smell of my son is like, etc.*, it means "Observe, the smell of my son is..."; for the intention is that he should perceive that smell. Another such instance is:
<margin>Exodus 20:18</margin>
*And all the people saw the voices and the torches*, (meaning, they perceived them); except that in this case, it must also be observed that this occurred within a Prophetic Vision, as is generally agreed among us. Operation, or Action and Speech, are attributed to Him because of the **Influence** <note>original: *Influentia*; this refers to the "overflow" or emanation of divine power or knowledge from God to the world</note>, 
<margin>Part 2, Chapter 12</margin>
which flows from Him (*specifically into these lower things*), as will be explained elsewhere.

Thus, all the corporeal organs and instruments attributed to God by the Prophets are either for **Local Motion**, to teach us of His Life; or **Senses**, to teach us of His Perception <note>original: *Apprehensione*</note>; or **Touch**, to teach us of His Action; or **Speech**, which teaches us of the Infusion of intellects upon the Prophets (that is, concerning the influence of intellectual matters which He pours into the Prophets), as will be explained. Therefore, all these things tend toward this: to confirm for us that God is a Being, Living, Acting, or bringing about all things that are outside of Himself, and Perceiving His own works. But how all these things refer to only one principle—namely, the Substance of God, the Best and Greatest—I shall teach elsewhere, when I am to treat the removal of external forms from God. For in this chapter, our purpose is only to declare in what sense those corporeal instruments are attributed to God: namely, to teach us of the functions and operations of those things which we consider perfections, so that we are compelled to confess that He is most absolute and perfect in every respect, just as we have already 
<margin>Part 1, Chapters 26 & 33</margin>
pointed out elsewhere when we explained that saying: *The Law speaks in the tongue of the children of men* <note>original: *Loquitur Lex lingua filiorum hominum*; a fundamental principle in Jewish thought that the Torah uses human language and metaphors to describe the divine</note>.

<margin>Examples of the aforementioned.</margin>
But let us now also propose examples of those things which have been said so far. First, therefore, instruments or organs of **Local Motion** are attributed to Him: such as, *The footstool of my feet.* Also, *The place of the soles of my feet.* Next, the instruments of **Touch**: such as, *The hand of God.* Also, *By the finger of God.* Likewise, *The work of your fingers.* Also, *And you shall lay your hand upon me.* Also, *And the arm of the Lord. The right hand of the Lord.* Then, the instruments of discourse or **Speech**: such as, *The mouth of the Lord has spoken. And He opened His lips. The voice of the Lord with power. And His tongue like a consuming fire.* Likewise, the **Organs of the Senses** are also attributed to Him: such as, *His eyes shall see. Also, His eyelids shall try. The eyes of the Lord run to and fro. Incline your ear to me and hear. You have kindled a fire in my nose* <note>The Hebrew idiom for anger is often "the nose burning"</note>.

### Part I. Chapter XLVI.
**I 2**

<margin>Of the internal members, none but the **Heart** is ascribed to God.
Chapter 39.
Jeremiah 31:20.

What the word *Meajim* (Bowels) signifies, and how it is attributed to God.
Psalm 40:9.
Jeremiah 31:19.
Isaiah 63:15.
*Hamah* (To Moan/Stir).
Jeremiah 4:19.</margin>...as we have said), and because the heart is the principle of life in living beings. For this reason, the word <term>Meajim</term> <note>original: מעים; literally "bowels" or "internal organs"</note> is attributed to God, because it is used as a substitute for the **Heart**. For when it is said, *My bowels were stirred* <note>original: *Fremuerunt meaj*; see Jeremiah 31:20</note>, *that is, my internal organs for him:* and likewise, *The multitude of your bowels*, nothing else is meant but the **Heart**. For the word *Meajim* is used either generally or specifically. Specifically, it refers to the intestines; but generally, it refers to any internal member, and consequently to the Heart itself. This is clearly shown by the passage where it is said, *And thy law is in the midst of my bowels* <note>original: *meaj*; Psalm 40:8 in English Bibles, 40:9 in the Latin/Hebrew numbering</note>. This is nothing other than if he had said, "In the midst of my heart." Therefore, the previously cited passages—*My bowels were stirred* and *The multitude of your bowels*—must be understood in this sense. For the verb <term>Hamah</term> <note>original: המה; meaning to roar, moan, or be in a state of commotion</note>, "to moan" or "to stir," is used in reference to the Heart more than any other member, as in: *My heart moans within me* <note>Jeremiah 4:19</note>.

<margin>Why Shoulders are not attributed to God.
Likewise the Organs of nutrition and nourishment.</margin>Conversely, **Shoulders** are not attributed to Him, because they are commonly used for carrying burdens, and because men are greatly exhausted by them. Much less are those organs attributed to Him which serve for taking in food and nourishment. For even at the first thought, those are manifest signs and indications of imperfection.

Furthermore, the reasoning regarding all the Members is, in truth, one and the same: namely, that they are the instruments of the various functions of the soul. For some of them are necessary for the **Preservation of the Individual**, such as all the internal members; some for the **Preservation of the Species**, such as the organs of generation; and some are necessary for the individual to carry out its affairs and complete its actions, such as the hands, feet, and eyes, which are instruments of Motion, Action, and Perception. Motion is necessary for humans to pursue what is beneficial and healthy for them, and conversely to flee from what is ruinous to them. The senses are necessary for knowing and discerning those things that are contrary or suitable to them. Artificial actions (crafts) are necessary for them to provide for themselves food, drink, clothing, and shelter; for it is necessarily required by their nature that they can acquire necessities for themselves, just as we see in many irrational animals certain skillful operations which they require for their needs. Now, I do not think any man is so dull-witted that he does not know that God lacks nothing for His own continuation and preservation, or for the directing and disposing of His affairs. From this it follows that He also does not possess such instruments and organs, but is **Incorporeal**. For He performs all His actions through Himself, or through His essence, and not through external instruments. Furthermore, if He does not have—

<summary>Maimonides explains that internal organs like the "bowels" are only attributed to God when they symbolize the heart (the seat of life and intellect), while organs implying physical need or fatigue, such as shoulders or stomachs, are never used. He concludes that because God is self-sufficient and perfect, He has no need for physical organs and is therefore entirely incorporeal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument that God lacks a physical body. Having established that internal organs like the heart or viscera are only attributed to God metaphorically to signify life or will, the text now explains that since God has no physical organs, He lacks the biological functions associated with them. It moves into a defense of the ancient Sages (the Rabbis), arguing that they always understood these descriptions as parables rather than literal truths.</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

...other organs and instruments, the functions and operations of those organs will also not be in Him, because these functions are understood through the instruments (that is, *they follow from them*). In other words: there will be nothing in Him except His essence, through which He might see, know, or will anything. For those external forms or faculties are distinguished by name alone. But our Rabbis, of blessed memory,

<margin>How the Talmudists remove Corporeality from God.</margin>
long ago handed down a general Rule, by which they refute all those things that seem to attribute physical forms of this kind to God in the Prophets. For that rule teaches you that Corporeality <note>The state of having a physical body</note> never entered the mind of any of them, nor did anyone ever doubt it. Hence you will find that in the entire Talmud and all the *Midrashim* <note>original: *Medraschot*; ancient Jewish commentaries and parables explaining the Bible</note> they follow the literal sense of the Prophetic words, because they knew the matter was confessed by everyone and placed beyond all risk of doubt: that all these things are to be understood parabolically, and that no error or heresy was to be feared from it. Therefore, when they saw that God was likened (by the Prophets) to a King who commands, warns, punishes, and bestows rewards on his subjects—who also has his servants and ministers, proclaiming his mandates and doing whatever seems good to him—therefore they (that is, *our Sages*) also imitated this. They spoke of God according to everything that follows from that parable: that He speaks, hears, answers, and whatever else Kings are accustomed to do and perform, being persuaded that there was no matter for doubt or hesitation here. Moreover, that general Rule which we mentioned is this, which is read in *Genesis Rabbah* <note>original: *Bereschit rabbah*; a foundational collection of ancient Jewish interpretations of the book of Genesis</note>: *Great is the power or strength of the Prophets, who liken the form to its Former* <note>original: *Gadol cocham shel nevi'im...*; a principle meaning that prophets are forced to use human language to describe the Divine Creator</note> (or *to its author*, that is, *the former or author of the form with the form, or that which is formed*), just as

<margin>Ezekiel 1:26.</margin>
it is said, *And upon the likeness of the throne was a likeness like the appearance of a man*. By these words they wished to teach that all those figures and forms which the Prophets saw in a Prophetic Vision were figures or forms created by God. This is most true. For every form that is conceived in the imagination is created. It is very wonderful, however, what they say: "Great is their power," that is, *Great is their virtue*. For by this way of speaking they show that this seems to them to be extremely difficult.

<margin>How the Rabbis are accustomed to speak about some unusual thing.</margin>
For they are almost always accustomed to speak in this way when they hear of some unusual thing, action, or deed. For example: *Someone said, Rabbi N. did such a deed in such a place, and indeed alone, and at night. N. answered: How great is his power or strength, that he did this alone! For "How great is his strength" is the same as "Great is his power."* Therefore, it is just as if they were saying: "How great is this thing, I ask, which the Prophets were forced to do, when they found it necessary to describe the essence of God through things created by God."

<summary>Maimonides explains that the Sages of the Talmud used physical metaphors for God (like a King) only as parables, and he introduces a Rabbinic rule that justifies the Prophets using human imagery to describe the Divine essence.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion in Chapter 46 regarding how the ancient Jewish Sages interpreted physical descriptions of God. Part I. Chapter XLVI.

...declare! Thus they have clearly and openly demonstrated that they do not believe in Corporeality <note>The philosophical concept that God possesses a physical body</note>, and that all the forms and figures shown in a Prophetic Vision are created things. However, these things are "likened to the form of the Form-giver," as they say <gloss>that is, forms created by God are attributed to God Himself, who is the author of all shapes and forms</gloss>. But if anyone wishes to interpret their intentions in a worse light, or to accuse these men—whom he has neither seen nor known—of imperfection, let him know that he is merely wasting his time and effort <note>original Latin idiom: "losing his oil and his labor"</note>, and will in no way diminish the authority of those whose memory is for a blessing <note>A traditional honorific for deceased Sages</note>.

### Chapter XLVII.

->*Why Sight, Hearing, and Smell are attributed to God, but not Taste and Touch. Also, why Intellect or Understanding is attributed to Him, but not Phantasy or Imagination.*<-

<margin>Chapter 26, page 1.</margin>
We have already indicated many times that the Prophets attribute nothing to God which the common people consider to be an imperfection, or which the common mind cannot conceive as being appropriate for God. This remains true even if the underlying logic is the same for those qualities that *are* used to describe or "paint a picture" of Him for us; this is because the qualities used for description leave a certain impression of perfection upon our thoughts—or at least, they are capable of doing so.

<margin>Why Taste and Touch are not attributed to God, while the other senses are.</margin>
Therefore, it seems necessary to explain why Taste and Touch are not attributed to God, while the remaining senses—namely Sight, Hearing, and Smell—are. In truth, the Majesty of God is equally free from all the senses. Furthermore, all senses are "imperfections" in the context of Apprehension <gloss>meaning the way all senses perceive is an imperfection</gloss>, even in those beings who can only perceive through the senses. This is because the senses are subject to passive experience, exhaustion, and pain, no less than any other bodily organs or instruments. Therefore, since the meaning of the phrase "the Blessed God Sees" is that He perceives visible things, and when it is said "the Blessed God Hears," it means He perceives audible things, then by that same logic, Taste and Touch could have been said of Him, explaining that He perceives the objects of Taste and Touch. For the logic of all the senses is one and the same; thus, if you remove the apprehension of one sense from Him...

<summary>This page concludes a defense of the Sages' metaphorical view of God's form and begins explaining why the "higher" senses like sight and hearing are used as divine metaphors while "lower" senses like touch and taste are rejected as too physical.</summary>
<keywords>Corporeality, Prophetic Vision, Divine Attributes, Senses, Apprehension, Metaphor, Rabbinic Authority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 47, specifically focusing on why certain physical senses and mental faculties are attributed to God metaphorically while others are strictly excluded.</meta>

# THE GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED

you simultaneously remove the perception of all five senses; and, if you were to affirm that He possesses the perception of even a single sense, you would logically have to affirm the perception of all five. Nevertheless, despite this, we find written in our Sacred Books: *And God saw. And God heard. And God smelled.* <note>original Latin quotes: "Et vidit Deus. Et audivit Deus. Et odoratus est Deus."</note> Yet, nowhere does it say: *And God tasted,* or *And God touched.*

Therefore, I say the cause for this is that it is fixed and settled in the minds of all men that God is not joined with bodies through physical contact; because, of course, they cannot see Him. However, these two senses—namely Taste and Touch—do not perceive things perceived by the senses (that is, their objects) except through physical contact. But the remaining senses can perceive their objects even if those substances, which are the subjects of those qualities, are far removed (that is, even if their objects are far away). Therefore, according to the common imagination of the people, these distant senses could appropriately be attributed to God.

Furthermore, the senses are assigned to God for this end and purpose: to teach us that God perceives our actions. Now, sight and hearing are sufficient for Him to perceive anything that exists outside of Himself.

Or, this may even be said for the purpose of our own instruction and reminder, just as our Sages say: *Know, or pay attention to, what is above you: namely, a seeing Eye and a hearing Ear.* <note>A famous quote from the Mishnah, Pirkei Avot 2:1</note>

You understand, therefore, that in the truth of the matter, the logic for all of the senses is one and the same; and in the same way that Taste and Touch are removed from God, so also could Sight, Hearing, and Smell be removed from Him. For all of them, universally, are bodily and passive <note>meaning they are acted upon by external objects</note> perceptions. The difference lies only in this: that the imperfection of some is immediately apparent, while others appear to be perfect. Thus:

<margin>
*Why Fantasy and Imagination are not attributed to God.*
</margin>

Because Fantasy and Imagination appear to be imperfections, while Intellection does not, therefore Fantasy or Imagination (which is expressed by the word *Damah* <term>דמה - to imagine or liken</term>) is not assigned to God. Instead, Thought and Intelligence, or Intellection, are assigned to Him (which are expressed by the words *Bun* <term>בין - to understand</term>, *Haskil* <term>השכיל - to gain insight</term>, or *Hitbonen* <term>התבונן - to contemplate</term>). For example: *Just as I thought—I intended to do.* <note>original Latin: "Quemadmodum chaschabhti cogitavi facere." A reference to Jeremiah 18:8</note> Likewise:

<margin>
Jeremiah 18:8.
Jeremiah 10:12.
</margin>

*And by His understanding He stretched out the heavens.* <note>original: "Ubitbhunato Et intelligentiâ suâ inclinavit cælos."</note> From this you see that the same rule is observed regarding internal perceptions as is observed regarding external sensory perceptions: namely, that certain things are attributed to God, while others are not. And all of this is "according to the language of the sons of man" <note>A core principle for Maimonides: the Torah uses human language to describe God so that people can understand Him</note>; that is, whatever humans consider a perfection, the Prophets attribute to God, and whatever they consider an imperfection, they do not attribute to Him. In the truth of the matter, however, God has no form...

<summary>Maimonides explains that the Bible attributes sight, hearing, and smell to God because they operate at a distance and seem "perfect" to humans, unlike taste and touch which require physical contact. He applies the same logic to mental faculties, noting that "understanding" is attributed to God while "imagination" is rejected as a human imperfection.</summary>
