<meta>This page continues the discussion from the end of Chapter 48, regarding how God's attributes are described without implying a physical body, and begins Chapter 49, which focuses on the translation techniques of Onkelos.</meta>

...an external essential form apart from His own substance, as we shall demonstrate.

### Chapter 49

## On the manner in which Onkelos the Translator <note>original: "Paraphrastes Chaldæus," referring to the famous 2nd-century Aramaic translation of the Torah known as Targum Onkelos</note> explains "Seeing" and "Hearing" in relation to God.

<margin>How Onkelos explained the acts of "Hearing" and "Seeing" regarding God, the Best and Greatest.</margin>
If you examine the translation of Onkelos, you will find that he was extremely careful in those places where the word *Hearing* occurs in relation to God the Best and Greatest. For if it appears to be taken in its primary, physical sense, he translated it so that the thing (of which the Hebrew word *Shama* <note>original: *Schamah*, meaning "to hear"</note> is spoken) reaches or has reached Him; as if to say, that He has apprehended or grasped it. If the word is used to describe a Cry or a Prayer, then he says that God has *Accepted* it, or has not *Accepted* it. For invariably, when we read *The Lord heard*, he renders it as *It was heard before the Lord*. In the other sense of a Cry or Crying—for example, when it says *Hearing, I will hear his cry*—he translates it as *Accepting, I will accept his cry*. And this is so consistent in his translation that he does not deviate from this explanation in even a single instance.

In the same way, when the word *Seeing* <note>Hebrew: *Raah*</note> is attributed to God, this same Onkelos provides wonderful explanations, the underlying logic of which I do not everywhere fully grasp. For sometimes, instead of *And the Lord saw* <note>Hebrew: *Vajar*</note>, he writes *And the Lord perceived* <note>Aramaic: *Vachasa*</note>; elsewhere, for the same phrase, he puts *And it was revealed before the Lord* <note>Aramaic: *Ugele*</note>. Now, while in certain places he renders the word *to see* through the Aramaic word *Chaza*, it appears from this that <margin>The word *Chaza* (חזה) is a homonym in the Aramaic language.</margin> the word *Chaza* <note>original: *Chasah*</note> in the Aramaic language is also a homonym <note>A word with multiple distinct meanings</note>, and is used both for the apprehension of the Intellect and for the apprehension of the physical senses. 

Hence it is a wonder why he avoided using that word in some places and instead rendered it as *And it was revealed before the Lord*. When I consulted the copies of that translation and compared them with the lessons I heard while I was still a student, I saw that in all places where "Seeing" is joined to a mention of Iniquity, Dominion, Violence, or Robbery, he interprets it as *And it was revealed before the Lord*. Because there is no doubt that the word *Chaza* in that language signifies the grasping and certain knowledge of a thing exactly as it is; therefore, when he found the word *Seeing* joined with the mention of Sin or Iniquity, he did not say *And he perceived* <note>original: *Vachasa*</note>, but rather *And it was revealed before the Lord*. However, throughout the entire Law <note>The Torah, or Pentateuch</note>, I have found the word *Seeing*, when it is attributed to God (Blessed be He), rendered...

<summary>Maimonides analyzes how the translator Onkelos systematically avoids attributing physical senses to God by using terms like "it was revealed before the Lord" or "He accepted" instead of "He saw" or "He heard."</summary>
<keywords>Onkelos, Targum, Aramaic translation, anthropomorphism, divine perception, Shama, Raah, Chaza, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the translation choices made by Onkelos, focusing on the specific Hebrew and Aramaic terms used to describe God "seeing" human affairs without implying physical sight or a change in God's nature.</meta>

-> GUIDE FOR THE PERPLEXED <-

...by the word *Chafah* <note>Aramaic for "to behold" or "perceive"</note>, except for the following instances: <margin>Genesis 29:32</margin> "Because the Lord has **seen** <note>Hebrew: *raah*</note> my affliction"; Onkelos renders it: "Because my affliction was **revealed** <note>Aramaic: *gele*</note> before the Lord." <margin>Genesis 31:12</margin> "Because I have **seen** all that Laban has done to you"; Onkelos renders it: "Because it was **revealed** before me": where although an Angel is speaking, he still does not attribute to him a sensory apprehension or a fixed cognition <margin>Exodus 2:25</margin> of the iniquity (*namely, that which Laban had practiced against Jacob*). Likewise, "And the Lord **saw** the children of Israel"; Onkelos: "And the servitude of the children of Israel was **revealed** before the Lord." <margin>Exodus 3:7</margin> "**Seeing, I have seen** the affliction of my people who are in Egypt"; Onkelos: "In **revealing, it was revealed** before <margin>Ibid. verse 9</margin> me the subjection of my people." Also, "**I have seen** the oppression": Onkelos: "The oppression was **revealed** before me." <margin>Exodus 4:31</margin> And "**when He had seen** their affliction"; <margin>Exodus 32:9</margin> Onkelos: "**When it was revealed** before Him their affliction." "**I have seen** this people": Onkelos: "**It was revealed** before me this people," that is, <margin>Exodus 2:25</margin> "**I have seen** their rebellion," just as it is also said elsewhere, "And the Lord **saw** <margin>Deuteronomy 32:19</margin> the children of Israel," that is, their affliction and trouble. "**And the Lord saw** and was provoked": Onkelos: "And it was **revealed** before the Lord." <margin>Ibid. verse 36</margin> "**When he shall see** that their power is gone": Onkelos: "**When it shall be revealed** before him"; for in this place also, the iniquity and tyranny of enemies is signified. In all these cases, he respected and followed the saying <margin>Habakkuk 1:13</margin> of the Prophet: "**You cannot look upon iniquity**"; therefore, whenever he encountered the word "Seeing" in relation to Iniquity, Injury, or Rebellion, he translated it as: "**And it was revealed** before him"; or, "**And it was revealed** before <margin>Onkelos's Paraphrase is corrupted in several places.</margin> me." However, this most elegant and excellent explanation has been corrupted and debased in our copies without controversy in three places, in which I find *Vachafah* <note>perceived</note>, when according to this principle they should have been rendered *Ugele* ("And it was revealed before the Lord"). These <margin>Genesis 6:5</margin> places are: 1. "And the Lord **saw** that the wickedness of man was great." 2. "And <margin>Ibid. verse 12</margin> the Lord **saw** the earth." 3. "And the Lord **saw** that she was hated." <margin>Genesis 29:31</margin> For to me it seems very probable that some error by the scribes has crept in here; because, however, we do not possess Onkelos’s own <term>autograph<note>original: αὐτόγραφον; a manuscript written in the author's own hand.</note></term>, we can affirm nothing for certain, but we must believe that this was Onkelos’s intended <margin>Genesis 22:8</margin> explanation. As for the phrase, "God will **provide** [see] for himself a lamb," which he explains as, "**The lamb was revealed** before the Lord," he did so so that he would not appear to suggest that God Himself would search for and provide the lamb; or also, because in that language (*Aramaic*) it is considered absurd to refer the Apprehension of God to an irrational animal. Regarding the earlier passages, they must be diligently investigated in ancient manuscripts; but if they are found everywhere as we have cited them, I frankly confess that I do not know his reason or intention.

<summary>Maimonides explains how the translator Onkelos uses the Aramaic word for "revealed" instead of "saw" when the Bible describes God witnessing sin or suffering, avoiding the implication that God directly "looks" at evil. He also notes potential errors in surviving manuscripts where this rule isn't followed.</summary>
<keywords>Onkelos, Targum, Maimonides, anthropomorphism, divine sight, Aramaic translation, Biblical exegesis, Habakkuk, scribal errors</keywords>
